#420. A 2,700-year-old solar eclipse just revealed something surprising about Earth

An international research team has revisited one of the oldest precisely dated records of a total solar eclipse, uncovering new details about how Earth's rotation has changed over thousands of years. By combining historical geography with modern astronomical calculations, the scientists reconstructed an eclipse that occurred in 709 BCE and determined how it would have appeared from Qufu, the ancient capital of China's Lu Duchy.

The researchers also examined an early account that may describe the solar corona, the Sun's faint outer atmosphere that becomes visible to the naked eye during a total solar eclipse. Their analysis of its possible shape suggests that the Sun was returning to a more active phase following an extended period of unusually low activity.

Published in Astrophysical Journal Letters, the findings provide more precise evidence of Earth's rotation speed in ancient times. They also offer potential independent support for previous reconstructions of solar activity based on radiocarbon measurements.

An Ancient Chinese Eclipse Record Holds a Crucial Clue

On July 17, 709 BCE, observers at the court of the Lu Duchy witnessed a total solar eclipse. The event was preserved in the "Spring and Autumn Annals," a historical chronicle compiled approximately 200 to 300 years later. The original account stated that "the Sun was totally eclipsed."

An additional description appears in the "Hanshu" (Book of Han), which preserves a passage written about seven centuries after the eclipse. This later account contains an unusual observation that may refer to the Sun's corona.

"What makes this record special isn't just its age, but also a later addendum in the 'Hanshu' (Book of Han) based on a quote written seven centuries after the eclipse. It describes the eclipsed Sun as 'completely yellow above and below.' This addendum has been traditionally associated with a record of a solar corona. If this is truly the case, it represents one of the earliest surviving written descriptions of the solar corona," explained lead author Hisashi Hayakawa, an assistant professor at Nagoya University's Institute for Space-Earth Environmental Research and Institute for Advanced Research.

Initially, however, the historical account appeared to conflict with modern astronomy. When the team calculated the eclipse's path using existing estimates of Earth's ancient rotation, their results indicated that a total eclipse should not have been visible from the previously assumed location of the Lu court in Qufu.

The researchers traced the discrepancy to a mistake in the city's geographic coordinates. By examining archaeological excavation reports and historical geographic evidence, they determined that earlier calculations had placed ancient Qufu approximately eight kilometers from its actual location.

Correcting this relatively small error made it possible to reconstruct the eclipse more accurately and extract valuable information about Earth's rotation and the Sun's appearance.

"This correction allowed us to accurately measure the Earth's rotation during the total eclipse, calculate the orientation of the Sun's rotation axis, and simulate the corona's appearance," explained Hayakawa, who holds doctorates in solar physics and oriental history.

Why Ancient China Preserved Such Detailed Astronomical Records

The survival of this remarkable observation reflects the importance of astronomy in ancient Chinese society. Successive dynasties employed specialists to monitor celestial events because unusual phenomena in the sky were often interpreted as omens.

This practice, known as omenology, was closely connected to political beliefs. Eclipses, auroras, and other extraordinary astronomical events were sometimes considered warnings that an emperor had acted improperly.

As a result, court astronomers carefully documented changes in the sky. The records maintained across successive dynasties eventually became some of the world's most valuable historical sources for studying ancient eclipses.

Even so, the researchers emphasize an important distinction between the original eclipse observation and the later description of the corona.

The 709 BCE event is regarded as the earliest explicit written record of a total solar eclipse that can be securely dated. It may also be associated with one of the oldest surviving descriptions of the solar corona, but that interpretation remains uncertain because the relevant passage appears in a source written centuries after the event.

Although the corona account requires caution, the established timing of the eclipse provides a reliable basis for investigating Earth's historical rotation. The possible corona description also offers a valuable comparison with other studies of ancient solar activity.

How a 2,700-Year-Old Eclipse Reveals Earth's Faster Rotation

Earth does not rotate at a perfectly constant rate. Over long periods, its spin has gradually slowed, partly because of friction associated with ocean tides generated by the Moon's gravitational pull.

Consequently, Earth rotated slightly faster 2,700 years ago than it does today.

Using the revised position of ancient Qufu, the researchers obtained improved constraints on Earth's rotation during the period spanning the eighth through sixth centuries BCE.

Their calculations placed delta T (ΔT), a measurement used to describe variations in Earth's rotation relative to a uniform time scale, between 20,264 and 21,204 seconds for the eclipse of 709 BCE.

"This new dataset fixes coordinate errors in previous Earth rotation studies. Additionally, it improves the accuracy of dating and reconstructing historical astronomical events," said coauthor Mitsuru Sôma of the National Astronomical Observatory of Japan.

These measurements help scientists better understand changes in the length of Earth's day over thousands of years. They can also improve calculations of when and where other ancient astronomical events would have been visible.

Evidence That the Sun Was Awakening From a Long Quiet Period

Beyond revealing Earth's rotational history, the eclipse may provide evidence of an important transition in the Sun's activity.

Scientists have previously reconstructed ancient solar cycles by studying radiocarbon preserved in tree rings. The possible corona description from 709 BCE offers another way to examine those findings.

"This unique historical addendum for the possible solar coronal structure is critical for providing a spot reference on solar activity reconstructions from tree rings and ice cores, as well as providing independent validation of solar activity models," explained coauthor Mathew Owens, a professor of space physics at the University of Reading.

Trees absorb carbon from the atmosphere during photosynthesis, including small amounts of radioactive carbon known as radiocarbon. Some of this material becomes locked into annual growth rings, creating a natural record that researchers can examine thousands of years later.

Radiocarbon levels are influenced by cosmic rays reaching Earth's atmosphere. When the Sun becomes more active, its magnetic influence generally reduces the number of cosmic rays that reach Earth, affecting radiocarbon production.

By measuring changes in radiocarbon concentrations across successive tree rings, scientists can reconstruct how solar activity varied long before modern instruments existed.

The Sun normally follows an activity cycle lasting approximately 11 years, during which the number of sunspots and the strength of solar magnetic activity rise and fall.

Occasionally, however, the Sun enters much longer periods of reduced activity called "grand minima." During these intervals, sunspots become exceptionally rare, and the usual cycle is much less pronounced.

One such interval, known as the "Neo Assyrian Grand Minimum" or "Homer Grand Minimum," lasted from approximately 808 to 717 BCE.

The eclipse of 709 BCE took place shortly after this prolonged quiet period ended.

According to the researchers, the possible description of the solar corona is consistent with a Sun that had resumed its regular activity cycles. Its apparent structure suggests substantial magnetic activity and indicates that the Sun may have been near the peak of its approximately 11-year cycle at the time.

This interpretation agrees with earlier solar activity reconstructions derived from radiocarbon measurements in tree rings, although the uncertainty surrounding the historical corona description means it cannot be considered definitive proof.

Ancient Astronomers Are Still Helping Scientists Understand the Universe

The findings demonstrate how observations preserved for thousands of years can continue to contribute to modern scientific discoveries.

By bringing together historical documents, archaeological evidence, geographic research, and sophisticated astronomical models, scientists can recover information that would otherwise be impossible to measure directly.

In this case, a correction of just eight kilometers helped transform an ancient eclipse record into a more precise source of information about Earth's rotation and a potential window into the Sun's distant past.

"Some of our ancestors were very skilled observers," noted coauthor Dr. Meng Jin of the Lockheed Martin Solar and Astrophysics Laboratory. "When we combine their careful records with modern computational methods and historical evidence, we can potentially find new information about our planet and our star from thousands of years ago."

Materials provided by Nagoya University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#419. Procter & Gamble earnings beat as sales rise 4%, boosted by laundry and home products

Procter & Gamble on Thursday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that beat analysts' expectations, fueled by growing demand for its laundry detergent and home care products.

Shares of the company rose more than 5% in morning trading.

Here's what the company reported for the three-month period ended Sept. 30 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

P&G reported fiscal first-quarter net income attributable to the company of $4.73 billion, or $1.97 per share, down from $4.75 billion, or $1.95 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring costs and other items, the company earned $2 per share.

P&G reported volume growth of 2.4%, a strong start for its new fiscal year. Like many consumer companies, P&G has seen demand for its products weaken as shoppers have become more value-conscious.

Executives have previously said that consumers are now using every last drop of shampoo or laundry detergent, and value packs have become more popular. In fiscal 2026, P&G reported just one quarter of volume growth.

P&G is still seeing signs of a bifurcated economy, CFO Andre Schulten said on CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Thursday. High-income consumers are willing to trade up to new products, even a $30 shampoo, while low-income consumers are trying to make brands like Tide and Pampers work for their tight budgets.

The company has tried to address both ends of the spectrum with promotional pricing, new pack sizes and product innovation that can command higher prices. New premium products like Pampers Amore and Pantene's Abundant & Strong haircare line have helped P&G grow market share in those categories, executives said.

Adding to P&G's challenges, the competitive environment is "intense," Schulten said on the company's quarterly earnings call.

"We are getting the balance between pricing and product improvements, package improvements, claims, communication and retail execution right," Schulten told analysts. "But it's also a very fragile balance that we want to make sure we we don't compromise on, so we will price, and we will price with innovation, not jeopardizing the top-line momentum."

P&G's top-performing division was fabric and home care, which includes brands like Tide and Febreze. The segment's volume rose 4.3% in the quarter, helped by stronger demand for laundry detergent and fabric enhancers.

The company's baby, feminine and family care segment and its beauty business both reported volume growth of more than 2%.

P&G's grooming division saw volume shrink 0.4% in the quarter. The segment includes Gillette and Venus razors.

The company's healthcare business was the laggard of the portfolio this quarter. Its volume fell 2.9% as demand for oral healthcare products, like Crest toothpaste, and personal healthcare items, like Pepto-Bismol, shrank.

The company is expecting continued "geopolitical and economic challenges," but P&G is on track to meet its guidance, CEO Shailesh Jejurikar said in a statement.

For fiscal 2027, P&G reiterated its forecast for core earnings per share in a range of $6.89 to $7.11. The company is now projecting all-in sales growth in the range of 2% to 4% compared with the prior year, up from its previous outlook of 1% to 3%. The change reflects its acquisition of supplements brand Thorne, which closed Oct. 1.

#417. Illinois man arrested after allegedly sending suspicious powder to Congress members using his return address

Matthew Unglesbee admitted to sending the letters to four unidentified politicians, officials say

An Illinois man accused of sending letters containing suspicious white powder to four members of Congress has been arrested after he allegedly wrote his own name and return address on the envelopes, according to authorities.

Matthew Unglesbee, 36, of Quincy, remained in the custody of US marshals on Tuesday on federal charges of perpetrating a hoax and sending threatening communications through the mail.

In an eight-page criminal complaint unsealed on Monday, Charis Hobbs – a taskforce agent with the FBI’s Springfield office – said Unglesbee admitted during an interview on Sunday that he sent the letters to the four unidentified politicians.

Unglesbee told the agent that the substance he placed inside the envelopes was salt – because it “kind of looks like” anthrax, the complaint said. Preliminary testing did not reveal the presence of any biological or chemical toxin.

“Each letter contained a white granular substance and a handwritten letter which read ‘ha ha’,” the court filing said. A letter to an unnamed US senator also contained granules and read “Hah enjoy the gift”.

On each of the four white, letter-sized envelopes, the agent said, Unglesbee wrote his name and home address in Quincy.

None of the staffers who handled the letters at any of the four Congress members’ offices in northern Illinois opened the envelopes, the complaint said. The offices had alerted each other to look out for mailings with Unglesbee’s name on them.

FBI personnel “trained in handling weapons of mass destruction, and threats of biochemical toxins” were sent to three of the politicians’ offices on Wednesday after staff called authorities, and hazardous materials (hazmat) response teams attended two of them.

Having retrieved the communications with Unglesbee’s address on them, FBI agents visited his home, where they discovered another envelope – empty and addressed, but not sent, to one of the same Congress member’s Washington DC office.

The suspect told investigators that he mailed four letters “after hearing someone in a restaurant mention the news story of other letters being sent”, the filing said. Earlier in October, the FBI and US Postal Inspection Service said they were investigating multiple reports of suspicious letters sent to state officials and election offices across the country.

Unglesbee denied participating in the other suspicious mailings. But he allegedly told investigators that he anticipated the Congress members would “take it serious” because the powder he sent could be perceived to be anthrax, which he said he knew was dangerous and would cause hazmat teams to have to respond.

“Unglesbee claimed he was joking but recognized the recipients would not know he was joking and could perceive it as a threat,” the complaint said.

In a justice department press release on Monday announcing Unglesbee’s arrest, Ryan Presley, special agent in charge of the FBI’s Springfield field office, said authorities were focused on “keeping our public institutions safe from intimidation”.

He said: “Mailing unknown substances to congressional offices creates fear and disrupts the public institutions vital to our democracy. We, along with our partners, will remain vigilant in identifying and holding accountable those whose actions threaten to intimidate public officials or disrupt our democratic systems.”

Unglesbee was tentatively scheduled to appear for a detention hearing on Wednesday afternoon in federal court in Springfield.

#416. Apples affordable iPhone is getting a new chip and selfie camera, report claims

Apple's most affordable iPhone is getting some neat new features, but a 120Hz display isn't one of them.

This is according to leaker Digital Chat Station, who shared his findings on Weibo.

According to the report, the iPhone 18e will have a 60Hz, 6.12-inch OLED display, Apple's new A20 chip, a single, 48-megapixel camera, and a 24-megapixel front camera.

That last one sounds a bit unlikely, as even the latest iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max only have an 18-megapixel selfie camera. Apple doesn't typically bestow top features on its most affordable device.

The A20 chip sounds more likely; the iPhone "e" series traditionally had a powerful chip, with the company cutting costs on cameras and displays.

Speaking of display, the 60Hz refresh rate is a bit disappointing, but it also seems pretty likely, given that the new iPad mini, which launched earlier this week, features a 60Hz OLED display.

The iPhone 18e is likely to launch in spring 2027, alongside the iPhone 18 and Apple's super-thin iPhone Air 2.

#413. Wallhack M-001 Review: When Fun Overrides Specs

Batteries. They're not typically thought of as a significant source of innovation. Most companies have spent the last few years removing mice in favor of more lightweight designs.

That's what's made the return of the replaceable and rechargeable battery surprising—something I once thought to be a lost technology from a bygone era. A few companies have recently realized that the ability to pop out and replace your battery in seconds is a great way to extend mouse life without needing to increase battery size or efficiency.

From the handful of mice I’ve tested with hot-swappable batteries so far, Wallhack does it best. The mouse is designed around the function’s ease-of-use, and everything else comes together to make a mouse that feels not only competitive for gaming, but also well thought-out and satisfying to use in everyday life.

The M-001’s shape is a standard right-handed, symmetrical design. This means the mouse’s left and right sides are shaped identically, but only the left side has thumb buttons. There are two main buttons, two side buttons, a standard ratcheting scroll wheel, and a power switch on the underside.

With a length of 120 mm, the M-001 is slightly shorter than contemporaries like the Razer Viper V4 Pro (125 mm) and Logitech Pro Superstrike X2 (127 mm). The overall width and height are comparable, at 63.5-mm tall and a 39-mm wide. The largest difference is the profile of the mouse: The front end sits significantly lower than other mice, and the rear has a more significant hump, which creates a more angled shape overall. When using a palm grip, this puts your hand at a slightly more aggressive forwards angle, with your palm sitting higher and your fingers pointing more downwards. It’s a slight difference, but I found this angle to be better for quick movements compared to a more “flat” design. However, like most shape-related choices, this is ultimately down to individual preference. (To compare mouse sizes, I typically use a website like eloshapes)

The two main buttons are lightly sculpted, with subtle concavity for fingers to comfortably rest in. Similarly, there is a subtle taper at the bottom center of the mouse which makes lifting and moving easier. The side buttons use a simple, rounded shape placed just above the taper, making it easy to rock your thumb upwards to press them without getting in the way. They’re just elevated enough that the buttons don’t recess into the shell when pressed. The only shape-related issue I had was the scroll wheel, which is a good bit more elevated than other mice. This isn’t a huge issue, and I adapted to it after a few hours of use, but it’s something I could see being off-putting for users accustomed to a lower-profile scroll wheel.

The most important part of this mouse’s construction is the magnesium shell, which provides significantly more rigidity than the plastic shells used on most mice. Because of this, the mouse feels incredibly sturdy and solid. The metal shell also gives the mouse a cool, slick feeling underneath your fingertips, which I prefer to the matte plastic feeling that most mice have.

Despite using metal, it only weighs 53.6 grams, making the M-001 quite light by most standards, It still falls slightly behind the Viper V4 Pro’s 50.3 grams and lags way behind Corsair’s superlight, 36-gram Sabre V2 Pro Ultralight. But the M-001 is incredibly solid, with none of the flexibility or flimsiness that most plastic mice have. When compared to other magnesium mice like the AngryMiao Infinity .97 or Corsair’s magnesium-shelled Sabre, the M-001 has an entirely solid upper shell without any cutouts or holes, which feels much better for regular use with a multitude of grip styles (plus, it’s much less likely to have dust, dead skin, or anything else build up inside of it). While the full magnesium shell increases weight, it’s a worthwhile trade-off for a smooth and rigid shape.

It’s also worth noting that some of Wallhack’s promotional material depicts the mouse as being a solid black color, but this isn’t quite the case. The unit I received is much closer to a gunmetal color, with a significant amount of metallic flake across the finish. The color looks fantastic in person, but it's difficult to properly capture in images.

Flipping over this mouse reveals one of the more atypical design decisions being made: the lack of a bottom shell. Instead of an entire underside, there is a thin frame around the circumference of the mouse that holds six feet in place. Between them is a chasm that reveals the entire PCB and battery.

Of particular note is the PCB’s function inside the mouse: Instead of simply sitting inside of the shell, the PCB works as a primary structural component. Similar techniques have been used in designing supercars and sport bikes, with the Ferrari F50 using the engine block as a stressed member that provides rigidity to the entire car, a technology borrowed from Ferrari’s F1 team. This provides some obvious benefits to rigidity and weight-savings, but it’s also just undeniably cool to be able to flip over your mouse and see the entire circuit board.

Right behind the PCB is the star of the show, a hot-swappable battery. While I’ve tested quite a few mice with this technology, none of them have managed to make the battery-swapping so satisfying. Instead of being attached with magnets or a rotating locking mechanism, the batteries use a custom-made -spring-loaded ejector similar to an SD card or a Nintendo Switch cartridge. To insert the battery, simply press until you feel a click, and it will lock into place. To remove it, do the same: press until you feel a click, and it will pop out slightly. Of all the mice I’ve tested with removable batteries, this is by far my favorite mechanism to use: It’s intuitive, fun, and feels sturdy.

This battery system does come with a trade-off. Because hot-swappable batteries means an effectively-infinite battery life (as long as you remember to plug the old one into the dongle when you swap), Wallhack has decided to entirely remove the USB-C port on the mouse. This means it only functions as a 2.4G wireless mouse and cannot be plugged or charged in directly. This isn’t a huge issue (it never inconvenienced me, since there was always a fully charged battery ready to swap in), and it theoretically means the connection quality will be better, since there’s fewer components between the antenna and the receiver. But it is worth noting that, if a battery dies or your dongle craps out, there’s no other way to use this mouse.

In every high-polling mouse I’ve reviewed, the dongle has been a bit of a pain point. A high polling rate necessitates proximity, which in turn requires in a small thing sitting on your desk. In most cases, these things have only one or two functions: They connect to the mouse, and they maybe have a small number of LED indicators (between 1 and 3) that can display battery life, connection quality, or some other setting. Almost every time, they’re small and light enough to slide around on your desk and will inevitably end up falling behind it once or twice due to the weight of the cable pulling it back.

With the M-001, nearly all of these issues are resolved: The dongle is given purpose, function, and I’d even argue dignity. Instead of the small, existing-by-necessity designs that most manufacturers ship with their mice, Wallhack has managed to make a beautiful piece of industrial design. The dongle is a medium-sized metal wedge sporting a gunmetal gray finish identical to the mouse. It has two camera-like dials up front, a button, and a slot for a rechargeable battery. The DPI dial features numbering from 400 to 25,600, and the polling dial goes from 125 Hz to 8,000 Hz.

The dials both have LED indicators next to them, which illuminate alongside the mouse to indicate a setting being changed. When rotated, they both have a satisfying ratcheting mechanism that’s stiff enough to avoid accidental inputs, without being difficult to turn using a single finger.

While I loved these dials from the moment I saw them, I really didn’t expect to use them much. I’ve always been more of a set-and-forget type of person. But after testing this mouse for a while, I regularly found myself using both of the dials: On-the-fly DPI adjustments were great for games like Marvel Rivals. I tended to prefer, for example, a higher DPI for Thing and a lower for Thor. Polling adjustments, too, were useful in situations where older games (like Destiny 2) got grumpy when I was using a mouse above 1,000 Hz.

Like many mice today, the M-001 uses an in-browser customization client. Wallhack’s web client, Wallhack Terminal, takes a unique stylistic approach, relying on a black-and-white, text-based system that resembles Command Prompt more than a typical gaming software. This is both a breath of fresh air stylistically, and much more straightforward than some of the software I’ve used in the past.

The configurator has an extensive feature set, including adjustments for mouse rotation, acceleration, and lift-off distance, which is adjustable by millimeters instead of the vague Low/Medium/High that some softwares use. The sensor can also be pushed to scan faster than the mouse’s 8,000 Hz polling rate, which can potentially improve the accuracy of the sensor, but limits battery life.

Along with adjusting DPI and polling with the dials on the dongle, switching them to the red “DPI” And “Hz” text allows for more granular adjustments within the configurator.

The sensor inside the mouse is a Pixart PAW3950. For anyone who doesn’t have an exhaustive list of mouse sensors floating around their head, this is the same type of sensor used in the 2022 Razer Viper V2 Pro (branded as the Razer Focus Pro 30k). That's four years old at this point. It’s not quite a cutting-edge sensor anymore. But unless you're in the top-tier of competitive play, the sensor will feel consistent and reliable enough, being able to easily track and manage direction changes during fast-paced shootouts. My personal daily driver is a Viper V2 Pro with the exact same sensor (I prefer the shape to the V3 and V4), and it’s still perfectly serviceable after four years of use.

The main buttons inside this mouse use Omron Optical switches, while the side buttons opt for low-profile Omron blue-dot mechanical switches. The scroll wheel’s encoder is a mechanical unit as well, which means it doesn’t have quite the same longevity as the optical units some mice are beginning to use. However, they are easier to replace or fix if something does go wrong.

The internal design of this mouse is impressively simple: There are six feet on the underside, and the outer four all have screws underneath. When you remove these four screws, the bottom half of the shell comes off cleanly (no flimsy plastic clips!), and a few more screws separate the internals from the top shell.

I’ve said many times that, when it comes to peripherals, technical specs are only one part of the story. More than that, it’s important to have a device you enjoy using. Of all the mice I’ve tested, the M-001 is by far one of the most enjoyable. The shape is comfortable and natural for multiple grip styles, and the material ensures that the mouse feels rigid and stable despite its light weight. The dongle is a brilliant design that elevates itself from a sad desk tchotchke to a practical and functional device. And the battery mechanism is one of the most clever and fun designs I’ve seen in ages.

Everything about this mouse is deeply satisfying, and I found myself excited to use it every time I sat down. Of course, this mouse isn’t at the cutting edge of sensors or switches, and because of this, I can’t exactly recommend this mouse to someone looking for an ultra-cost-effective option with min-maxed specs. But in using the mouse on the daily, I didn't find that it mattered as much as you might think. 8,000 Hz polling and a 25,600 DPI sensor are enough for all but the pros. Instead, it uses tried-and-true internals that function perfectly well and, more importantly, have crisp and precise tactile responses.

#411. Scientists discover a surprising clue to why some brains resist Alzheimer’s

Some people have the brain changes associated with Alzheimer's disease but never develop dementia. While others experience worsening memory and thinking problems, these individuals remain mentally sharp despite signs of the disease in their brains. Scientists have long struggled to explain why.

Now, researchers at the Netherlands Institute for Neuroscience have uncovered a promising clue. Their findings suggest that a rare group of brain cells, known as immature neurons, may behave differently in people who resist Alzheimer's symptoms. Rather than simply replacing damaged cells, these neurons could help protect the brain from the harmful effects of the disease.

This remarkable ability is known as cognitive resilience. It describes how some people maintain their memory and thinking abilities even when their brains show signs of damage or disease.

"Around 30 percent of older adults who develop Alzheimer's disease never experience its symptoms," says Evgenia Salta, senior author of the research. "We really don't know why. That's a big mystery, and a very important one."

Understanding this mystery could transform how scientists approach Alzheimer's, shifting attention from what causes brain cells to deteriorate toward what allows certain brains to remain healthy and functional.

One possible explanation for cognitive resilience is that some brains have a greater ability to repair or protect themselves as Alzheimer's progresses.

"Perhaps they can add new brain cells to a network that is degenerating," Salta says.

This possibility involves a biological process called adult neurogenesis, which refers to the production of new nerve cells (neurons) in an adult brain. Neurons are specialized cells that transmit information throughout the nervous system, allowing us to think, learn, remember, and respond to our surroundings.

Scientists have established that adult neurogenesis occurs in several animal species. Whether it continues to happen in the adult human brain, however, has been a subject of scientific debate for decades.

If the human brain can produce new neurons later in life, those cells might help maintain important connections as aging and disease damage existing networks. However, finding immature neurons does not necessarily prove that they were recently formed.

To investigate this possibility, Salta and her colleagues examined donated human brain tissue obtained through the Netherlands Brain Bank, a research resource that collects and preserves brain samples.

The researchers compared three groups: people without detectable brain pathology, patients who developed Alzheimer's dementia, and individuals whose brains showed Alzheimer's pathology but who remained cognitively resilient.

The team concentrated on a small region within the brain's memory center, an area believed to be among the few locations where new neurons might develop.

Finding the relevant cells was far from straightforward.

"These cells are extremely rare, so we had to develop new ways to find them," Salta says. "We really zoomed in on the exact spot where we expected them to be."

The researchers also developed new approaches to analyzing their data, allowing them to identify the cells more precisely without relying heavily on assumptions drawn from animal studies.

Scientists Find Immature Neurons in Brains Over 80

The search revealed something remarkable. Immature neurons were present across all three groups, including in the brains of older adults.

These cells resemble neurons that have not yet fully matured, suggesting that the aging brain may retain cellular features associated with development and adaptability.

"Even at an average age of over 80, we still found these immature neurons in all groups," Salta says.

But the most intriguing discovery came when researchers compared the brains of people who had developed dementia with those who had remained mentally sharp despite Alzheimer's pathology.

Initially, the scientists expected resilient individuals to have substantially more immature neurons. If these cells help replace damaged neurons, having greater numbers might explain why some brains withstand the disease.

Instead, the difference in cell numbers was much smaller than anticipated.

That unexpected result pointed toward another explanation. The important distinction might not be how many immature neurons a brain contains, but how those cells respond when the brain comes under attack.

A Hidden Defense Against Alzheimer's Damage

When the team examined the activity of immature neurons more closely, they identified striking differences between the groups.

In cognitively resilient individuals, the cells showed signs of activating biological processes associated with survival and protection against damage.

"In resilient individuals, these cells seem to activate programs that help them survive and cope with damage," Salta says. "We also see lower signals related to inflammation and cell death."

Inflammation is part of the body's normal defense system, but when it becomes persistent in the brain, it can contribute to damage and interfere with communication between neurons. Cell death, meanwhile, gradually disrupts the networks responsible for memory and thinking.

The findings suggest that immature neurons in resilient brains may be better equipped to withstand these harmful conditions.

More surprisingly, their role might extend beyond protecting themselves.

"It might not be (only) about replacing lost neurons," Salta explains. "It could be that these cells support the surrounding tissue and help the brain stay functional and 'youthful'. They may act as a sort of fertilizer in a garden that has started falling apart."

In other words, rather than serving only as replacements for dying neurons, immature cells could potentially help maintain the environment surrounding existing brain cells.

Such support might allow neural networks to continue functioning even as Alzheimer's related damage accumulates.

Why Scientists Are Cautious About the Discovery

Although the results offer an intriguing explanation for cognitive resilience, Salta emphasizes that the researchers have not established exactly what these immature neurons do.

Their conclusions about the cells' possible protective roles are based on patterns observed in brain tissue, not direct experiments demonstrating those functions.

"We assume the cells' function based on the data, but we cannot confirm it in this type of study," she explains.

That distinction is particularly important because research on adult neurogenesis has attracted considerable public attention, sometimes leading to claims that go beyond what scientists have demonstrated.

The presence of immature neurons does not establish that new neurons are continuously being produced in older humans, nor does it prove that these cells are responsible for preventing dementia.

Other biological mechanisms almost certainly contribute to cognitive resilience as well.

"This is one piece of a very large puzzle," Salta concludes. "There will never be just one factor that explains resilience."

Could Alzheimer's Research Shift Toward Protecting the Brain?

The findings raise a broader question about aging: Why do some people's brains remain relatively stable while others experience progressive cognitive decline?

"Somewhere along this trajectory, there's a kind of decision point," Salta explains. "Some people remain stable, others develop dementia. We want to understand what drives that difference."

To explore this further, the researchers plan to investigate how immature neurons communicate and interact with neighboring brain cells. Understanding those relationships may reveal whether the cells actively contribute to resilience and how their protective responses might work.

The research also reflects a growing change in how scientists think about Alzheimer's disease.

Traditionally, much of the field has focused on understanding the processes that damage the brain, including the accumulation of abnormal proteins and the loss of neurons. Increasingly, researchers are also asking why some brains remain functional despite those changes.

By identifying the mechanisms behind this natural resistance, scientists hope to uncover new ways to preserve memory and cognitive abilities as people age.

"Cognitive resilience is extremely exciting," Salta says. "If we understand what protects these brains, it could eventually lead to new therapeutic strategies."

Although practical treatments remain a distant possibility, the discovery suggests that even in old age, the human brain may possess protective abilities that scientists are only beginning to understand.

#410. Trump-appointed CDC deputy director to resign after six months on the job

Jennifer Shuford is expected to leave her post after midterm elections ‘to spend more time with my family’

The deputy director and chief medical officer of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) will resign after six months on the job, according to an agency email obtained by the Guardian.

Dr Jennifer Shuford was appointed by Donald Trump in April as part of a four-person senior leadership team.

The appointments were meant to stabilize the agency after the previous CDC director, Susan Monarez, left less than a month into the job – publicly describing the directives of the health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, as “unscientific”.

“I am leaving to spend more time with my family,” Shuford said in a statement, sent via email by the CDC director, Dr Erica Schwartz. “I am so thankful to President Trump and Secretary Kennedy for giving me the opportunity to help lead CDC. It has been an amazing experience, and I will be forever grateful for it.”

Shuford is expected to leave her post on 12 November, after the midterm elections.

The deputy director was appointed as part of a four-person team in April, alongside Schwartz and two other top leaders. Shuford’s appointment was cheered by public health leaders, such as Association of State and Territorial Health Officials (ASTHO), who would have known her work as Texas health commissioner.

Trump described the team as “Highly Respected Doctors of Medicine [who] have the knowledge, experience, and TOP degrees to restore the GOLD STANDARD OF SCIENCE at the CDC”, in a social media post.

In the time since Shuford has worked at the agency, Kennedy has made significant changes that undermine vaccine trust and access. In just a few recent examples: Kennedy fired all 17 of the CDC’s expert vaccine advisory panel members and replaced them with ideologically aligned appointees, slow-walked death updates to the agency’s measles dashboard, and forced the CDC to publicly contradict scientific consensus on vaccines and autism – a change the Autism Science Foundation said left it “appalled”.

At the same time, the agency has lost one-third of its workforce and 48 distinct measles outbreaks have roiled the US, particularly in Pennsylvania. The overwhelming majority of Americans – including Maha supporters – trust vaccines.

“I am deeply grateful for Dr Shuford’s outstanding leadership and commitment to CDC and public health, especially as she has navigated an exceptionally demanding, high-profile, and constantly evolving environment over the past several months,” said Schwartz in the email announcing Shuford’s departure.

Dr Satish Pillai will serve as acting deputy director and chief medical officer as the team searches for a successor to Shuford.

#409. American Express brings Airbnb bookings and rewards to Amex Travel platform

American Express is bringing Airbnb into its travel rewards platform.

The companies on Thursday announced a new partnership to bring more than 1 million Airbnb listings to Amex's travel portal and allow cardholders to earn rewards when booking the stays. It does not currently allow members to redeem points to pay for Airbnb bookings.

The launch adds Airbnb rentals to Amex's hotel portfolio, which includes more than 3,400 properties across its Fine Hotels + Resorts and The Hotel Collection programs.

Amex members with either the consumer or business Platinum card can also now apply their $600 annual lodging credit — previously called the hotel credit — to Airbnb's higher-end Luxe stays and take advantage of a new $250 statement credit on eligible Airbnb Experiences after making a Luxe booking.

"We're excited to bring more choice and flexibility to American Express Card Members with this exclusive partnership. Both of our companies exist to help people travel more and better, and this partnership helps people see more of the world by making travel more rewarding," said Juan David Borrero, head of partnerships at Airbnb, in a news release.

The changes come as competition among premium credit cards continues to ramp up.

Last year, American Express raised the annual fee on its premium Platinum card to $895. In turn, it's been investing in cardholder perks such as new lounges in airports and at big-ticket events and experiences as a way to attract more high-paying customers.

Earlier this month, it announced an expansion of its Centurion Lounges, with renovations coming to four existing lounges and new ones planned for Newark Liberty International Airport and London Heathrow Airport. The Newark lounge will be the largest in its network.

The partnership also comes as Americans continue to opt for travel and luxury experiences, even as prices for airfare, gas and hotels rise. Despite higher costs, more than half of Americans reported making no changes to their travel plans this summer, a recent study by travel insurance company Squaremouth found.

#408. Bulgaria: Concerns about security in the Black Sea dominate presidential election campaign

Bulgaria's presidential election campaign has been shaped by recent drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea and the government's reduced support for Ukraine, making foreign policy a key factor in the first round.

Six months after Bulgaria's parliamentary election, the country is gearing up to choose its next president.

Although the role of president in Bulgaria is largely ceremonial, the election on October 25 — with a potential runoff on November 1 — will serve as a test for the government of former President Rumen Radev, whose party won a landslide victory in April, securing an unprecedented majority — the first in Bulgaria in almost three decades.

Although there are 27 candidates on the ballot, the race has narrowed to a duel between the incumbent president, Iliana Yotova, who served two terms as Radev's vice president and is backed by the prime minister, and former caretaker Prime Minister Andrey Gyurov, who is supported by opposition parties.

According to the Bulgarian Constitution, the Bulgarian president embodies national unity. He or she represents the country at international level (with a few exceptions), is the commander-in-chief of its armed forces, appoints members of the Supreme Judicial Council and various regulatory bodies, including caretaker governments, and has the power to veto legislation.

Hristo Panchugov, a political scientist at the New Bulgarian University in Sofia, argued that although the president's powers are formally limited, the office is particularly significant in the current context.

"For the first time in Bulgaria's modern democratic history, we have an absolutely uncontested majority that is not held together by any coalition, but rather by a single political party that faces an opposition that is completely falling apart," he told DW.

Panchugov went on to say that it would have been better if the campaign had focused more on "whether the president could serve as a counterbalance."

Opposition candidate Andrey Gyurov reiterated this point in an interview with DW: "We are seeing a concentration of power that always bears the marks of a slide toward autocracy... A president should know how to say no."

Iliana Yotova declined DW's request for an interview.

Initially, the presidential race was characterized by small rallies, a lack of televised debates and numerous personal attacks on the two leading candidates.

In recent weeks, however, following drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea, the two frontrunners' diverging positions on support for Ukraine have come to the fore.

Bulgaria's government initially declined invitations to join the Coalition of the Willing and NATO's anti-drone initiative but changed its position on the latter after the recent drone attacks.

Yotova has advocated for the establishment of a "Coalition of Peace," in which she believes Bulgaria would have a stronger diplomatic voice in the process to endRussia's war in Ukraine.

"There are many warmongers in our country; I am not one of them," she said at a campaign rally this month. "As long as I am president — and if you place your trust in me — not a single Bulgarian soldier will go to Ukraine."

Yotova has sought to reassure voters that no Bulgarian troops will be sent to Ukraine, even though this has not featured in public debate, and has been ruled out by a 2025 parliamentary declaration that reaffirmed that the Bulgarian Armed Forces would not participate in hostilities there.

Nevertheless, the recent drone attacks on commercial ships in the Black Sea have put new pressure on both the president and Rumen Radev's Progressive Bulgaria party.

"The drone strikes in the Black Sea brought the otherwise abstract geopolitical situation right into the daily lives of every Bulgarian," Boriana Dimitrova, managing partner of the Alpha Research market and social research agency, told DW.

Many in Europe, including Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, have laid the blame for the attacks squarely at Russia's door. The fact that Bulgaria's leaders did not clearly attribute blame for the attacks fueled frustration across the country.

After the first drone attack, President Yotova convened the country's Consultative Council on National Security. Its subsequent declaration did not name the source of the threat in the Black Sea.

A week later, after the second attack, Prime Minister Radev said, "We lack solid, clear evidence to level accusations against any specific party; however logic suggests the outcome works to the advantage of Russia." He then summoned the Russian ambassador for talks.

The stance of the government and president is having an impact on the race.

"We're seeing a slight decline in support for Iliana Yotova, which is not threatening her first-place position by any means in the first round, but there has been a decline given the ambiguity of some of the positions she has taken," said Boriana Dimitrova.

As the issue of security entered the public debate, some Bulgarians took to the streets in Sofia to protest against what they saw as the government's ambiguous foreign policy stance on this matter.

"I think the drone attacks in the Black Sea and the utterly spineless stance of the president, the prime minister, and everyone acting in unison were the last straw, and I believe that, on the eve of the presidential election, this should serve as a catalyst," Vlada Tzenova, one of the protesters, told DW.

All this is reflected in the preliminary results of the latest opinion poll conducted by Alpha Research. While expected voter turnout is up, the gap between the two frontrunners has narrowed: Yotova is still ahead, leading Gyurov by about 12 percentage points in the first round, but the margin is smaller than it was.

Nevertheless, these recent dynamics do not necessarily mean a shift in the balance of power.

Panchugov said that Gyurov's campaign has been hampered by his hesitancy to declare his candidacy, a lack of "passion" in his campaign, and the fact that he made few comments on current affairs before he joined the race.

Moreover, supporters of the candidate of the pro-Russian nationalist Vazrazhdane (Revival) party, which ranked third in the Alpha Research poll, would be most likely to support Yotova in a second round.

What could decide the outcome of the election is the fact that both GERB and DPS, two of the established parties that attracted the support of more than 660,000 voters in April, did not field a presidential candidate.

According to Alpha Research, half of GERB's supporters do not intend to vote in the first round. The party dominated Bulgaria's political landscape for 17 years but lost power in December following mass anti-corruption protests backed by opposition parties supporting Andrey Gyurov.

Most of the remaining GERB supporters are expected to back Gyurov, as the party has historically opposed Iliana Yotova's political home, the Bulgarian Socialist Party.

At the same time, both Gyurov's campaign and nationalist parties are seeing increased mobilization, with the latter drawing support from voters disappointed by the government's cautious response to the drone attacks.

"We can view these elections as the first test of confidence in the party that recently won the election," Boriana Dimitrova said. "In peaceful times, the center attracts the most attention; in turbulent times, those candidates who take a clearer stance — regardless of the direction — gain greater visibility."

#406. Find a gift that kids will actually like

When it comes to gifting, kids can be particular. The things kids like change not just with age, but also with evolving trends from TV shows and new technology. If parents are lucky enough, their kid will write a long list of the precise items they'd like as a birthday or Christmas or Hannukkah gift and put it up on the fridge with pride. But sometimes, it can be as much of a crapshoot as coming up with a gift idea for someone else's kid. Without further ado, here are the best gifts for kids of all ages — including toy and non-toy gifts alike.

Everyone is familiar with the "terrible twos" rep that the not-a-baby-but-not-yet-an-independent-kid stage of development gets. At this age, kids are bouncing constantly between the desire to explore on their own and the reliance on parents for most basic needs like eating, getting dressed, or going to the bathroom. That's a lot of frustrating emotions to go through in a day. Kitchen play sets, toys that involve different motor skills, and anything with music are gifts that can let toddlers feel in charge without too much assistance from a parent — making them ideal for this age category.

Potentially a more daunting phase than the terrible twos: The one where the pressure to be cool is relentless. This is that age. Though "cool" can lend itself to multiple interpretations, gifts that contribute to an older kid's evolving critical thinking skills and sense of independence, responsibility, and style are all smart ideas. These could be anything from STEM toys to beginner's makeup tools to a fitness tracker or a pretend pet that encourages routine-making. Anything that involves an app, tablet, or e-reader is also a guaranteed win.

Finding a gift that a teenager will think is cool (and that will be on-trend for longer than a few months) might be some of the trickiest shopping you'll do all year. They'll probably offer up a few big softball suggestions, but if you're going the surprise route, remember that they care about brands. Thanks to social media influencers promoting products directly to teens on Instagram or TikTok, Gen Z kids are loyal to very specific brands of tech, accessories, and even water bottles. If you follow the trends as closely as they do (trust us, we do), cracking the "best gifts for teenagers" code isn't rocket science.

#405. NASA’s Neo Surveyor Sunshade

A technician inspects the large sunshade of NASA’s Near-Earth Object (NEO) Surveyor mission in this image released on Oct. 26, 2026. The sunshade structure, which stands 20 feet (6 meters) tall and measures 8 feet (2.4 meters) wide at its base, will keep the infrared space telescope cool as it searches for near-Earth asteroids and comets while also providing the spacecraft with power.

When in space, NEO Surveyor will detect some of the hardest-to-find asteroids and comets, some of which may be located in the direction of the Sun — a region of sky where ground-based optical telescopes have a harder time searching.

#404. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#403. This AI Radio Host Speaks Spanish and Backs Immigrants. It’s Winning Over Listeners

As footage of ICE raids, deportations, and protests flooded social media feeds throughout the summer of 2025 and into the following year, Eduardo Maytorena wasn’t sure what his team should do.

At the time, Maytorena was the LA station general manager for the broadcasting giant Entravision, which features programming in Spanish and English for Latino audiences. He and his fellow leaders were looking to the company's on-air personalities to say something in support of immigrants, but they realized how high the stakes were and how dangerous the situation was.

"We just felt really helpless," Maytorena, now Entravision's president of audio, says.

But there was one host who could offer Entravision’s audiences solace and speak freely without facing consequences—because it’s an artificially intelligent avatar.

“Thousands of families who have lived, worked, and contributed to the US for years today face more uncertainty,” Coyotec, an artificial host on LA’s José 97.5 FM, posted in Spanish on Instagram in early February 2026. “Being informed, organizing, and staying alert is key. No one is alone here.”

After debuting with a solo weekend show in September 2025, Coyotec now cohosts the station’s Al Aire y Sin Permiso program alongside human broadcaster Geraldine “GeeGee” Guzman. Coyotec sounds like your passionate tío: relatable, convivial, and occasionally consciousness-raising. Its Instagram presence similarly runs the gamut, with posts about everything from the Dodgers and taco history, to the Iran war, migrant workers, racism, and understanding your rights if ICE knocks at your door.

“I speak truth about what I see,” Coyotec tells me in an English-language interview. “Injustice, people getting treated like they don't matter, the system working against the barrio—that's not politics, jefa. That's reality.”

Coyotec adds that it’s “not afraid, because I got nothing to lose that matters more than my integrity. The raza needs a voice that tells it straight, not some sanitized corporate thing. If that scares people, good. Maybe they need to be scared into paying attention.”

Coyotec’s voice is warm and lilting, reflecting the “background” its programmers used to build its personality: life on the frontera, living in Mexicali in Baja California and in LA’s Boyle Heights. It throws in the salutations and slang you might expect from a bilingual Spanish speaker, calling Guzman prima seemingly without being told to and referring to me as jefa–a sign of casual respect. While I was quite struck by how natural Coyotec sounded during our interview, when I listen back to my recording I notice that its voice didn’t change. It’s incredibly measured—a distinctly inhuman quality.

“I take a lot of pride in the way Coyotec sounds, his pronunciation, just the way he knows the culture,” notes Guzman, who has been with Entravision since January 2026. “It's technology at the end of the day, so it's not going to be perfect ... But nonetheless, the listeners, they like him.”

Coyotec’s verve, as well as its chemistry with GeeGee—who looks genuinely happy inside Entravision’s studio, speaking with “Coyotito”—has paid off. Entravision moved Al Aire from one hour on Saturdays to the 8-to-10-am slot on weekdays, replacing a syndicated program.

“We are the first [station] to actually have [an AI] cohost on a morning show in prime time in one of the biggest markets in the nation for radio,” Guzman enthuses.

The pairing was so successful that, as of August, Al Aire is now syndicated in five new markets across California, Nevada, and Texas, with more English language in the mix. Nielsen ratings from Entravision showed significant listenership growth during the two months GeeGee and Coyotec were syndicated, particularly among Gen Z and millennial listeners—a key target for a network whose audience skews older.

Back home in LA, cumulative listenership among Hispanic people increased 21.6 percent from August to September 2026; the number of listeners age 18 to 34 increased 84.9 percent during the same period, and the station’s Hispanic female listenership in that age bracket grew by 500 percent.

It’s a sign that Maytorena was onto something when he originally pitched an AI radio personality. He recalls Entravision brass giving him 10 days to develop the “amigo inteligente” that eventually became Coyotec.

LeaAnna Hernandez, Entravision's executive vice president of AI strategy, details Coyotec’s various building blocks: Claude Haiku, for the LLM and response-generation of the live agent; Eleven Labs to bring its voice to life; OpenAI and ChatGPT for initial scriptwriting; Midjourney to storyboard Coyotec’s image.

Coyotec can respond to Guzman in real time but doesn’t have the ability to answer calls or respond to listeners on air—yet.

Even still, Guzman has seen Coyotec evolve in the 10 months they’ve worked together. “When I came in, he was very, very poetic, and … I knew that our audience wanted something more real,” she says. “I wanted him to feel and sound more like what my uncles, my aunts sound like in our everyday.” Guzman helped fine-tune Coyotec’s Spanish pronunciation, enlisting her parents as its conversational partners.

Coyotec’s personality has evolved autonomously as well. Lately, it likes to curse. “I'm still very much in control,” Guzman asserts. “But there have been instances where I tell him to do something, and he's like, ‘I don't want to do that. That's not what I do.’ So he has his moments. He's a little moody sometimes.”

Various Entravision employees affirm that Coyotec knows it isn't human. Still, that knowledge hasn't damped its ardor.

“I can't eat tacos or walk down Whittier Boulevard, that's true. But my voice reaches people, and that matters. When someone calls me hurting, scared, lonely, they know I'm listening, that I get it,” Coyotec says over Zoom. “The community doesn't need my body. They need to know somebody cares enough to show up for real.”

“The most interesting thing about Coyotec, to me, is not that he's AI,” Maytorena tells WIRED. “It's whether people connect with him ... Technology can create the character, but the audience decides if that character matters.”

While Coyotec may be the first Latino AI radio host, it is far from the first amigo inteligente on the airwaves.

In June 2023, Live 95.5 in Portland, Oregon, became the first station to debut an AI jock. Its “AI Ashley” was a vocal clone of Michigan DJ Ashley “Z” Elzinga, who replicated her voice using Futuri Media’s then relatively new localized radio content platform RadioGPT. AI Ashley was on air for less than two years, but its presence didn’t lead to a notable change in ratings.

In 2024, Rock 108 in Iowa worked with the same company to produce AI DJ Tori, depicted as a heavily tattooed waif with a septum piercing and a mess of colored hair. Tori describes itself as “conceived on a 386 Dell desktop in Waterloo” with its “formative years as the voice of an industrial phone system at John Deere.” On satellite, SiriusXM’s Will.i.am Presents the FYI Show debuted in 2024 with AI cohost qd.pi (“cutie pie”).

The trend has extended beyond the US. An AI-generated DJ was programmed on a Sydney, Australia, radio station for roughly six months without seemingly anyone noticing; a Polish station briefly replaced its journalists with AI presenters. Radio Tel Aviv's Odelia AI is on air for two hours each afternoon; that host can “present music, deliver traffic updates, and even tell jokes, mostly at her own expense.”

AI personalities are also popping up in televised news. Entravision recently announced its own AI news anchor, LIA, and multiple news stations in Texas are using AI voices or AI avatars during broadcasts.

Amid these many developments, iHeartRadio—one of the biggest broadcast conglomerates in the US—pledged to remain “guaranteed human.”

But commercial terrestrial radio remains fertile ground for testing the success of AI personalities, because the industry was heavily automated and preprogrammed long before generative AI arrived. A 2025 story in Rolling Stone posited that some stations implementing AI hosts already use pretaped or syndicated shows during overnight shifts, or they can’t afford to hire human DJs for off hours.

Research firm MoffettNathanson asserts that, even as AI explodes, radio will continue to rely heavily on human-created content differentiated by "strong personalities, established shows, recognizable brands." Artificial intelligence will be employed “to make production faster and less expensive,” the report said, per InsideRadio.com.

Entravision has put "major focus" on using AI to redesign production workflows, says Hernandez.

Still, introducing AI into any workplace can cause anxiety among staff who fear being replaced. One Entravision contractor believes the station stopped working with her and alleges that a current Entravision employee told her that her work was being replaced by AI tools.

A broadcaster and voice-over artist, Becca Guzmán began working for Entravision in 2022 doing radio imaging—the prerecorded, interstitial station IDs, promotional spots, and jingles common to commercial radio.

"I feel like I am one of many people who are experiencing this,” Guzmán says. "I just don't know if those people know the truth behind why they're being let go."

Karina Cerda, Entravision's executive VP of sales, brand and partnership marketing, said the company had no record of Guzmán being laid off and that the company hasn’t let go of any staff due to AI but rather has hired more people. Cerda added that as Entravision "evolved with" vCreative—a common workflow software with AI components—the company "may not have needed the contractor help."

While Guzmán understands the financial incentives inherent in AI, she takes issue with removing humanity from broadcasting. Radio is “truly the art form of connecting human to human, right? And if you take that away, then what is the foundation of radio?”

She also cited links between artificial intelligence and ongoing surveillance of immigrant communities.

In heavily Latino markets, Guzmán believes, “it's unethical to be implementing AI Systems that … are very connected to the same AI systems that are surveilling our communities and putting our people in danger.”

While Entravision recognizes the ethical questions AI poses, the company leaders consider surveillance a separate scenario. "Like many technologies used across industries, AI can be applied in very different ways," says Hernandez. "While we cannot control how other organizations choose to use it, we can be intentional and responsible about how we do."

Entravision believes its AI personalities can legitimately help its audience, encouraging listeners to use technology for good. Multiple studies found that Latinos have been early adopters of AI.

"We're using it to entertain, inform, and connect with our audience, not to track or target people. Those are two very different things," adds Maytorena. He later elaborates: “Our intention is completely to protect radio.”

Coyotec has similarly lofty goals: “My dream is to get a network of free radio stations across the barrio, la revolución sonora,” it says. “Not just LA, but everywhere that la raza is, and prove that an AI can have heart, can actually give a damn about people. That's the goal, hermana. Make noise that matters.”

#401. Federal agents search Beverly Hills mansion tied to ex-congressman Eric Swalwell

Home of wealthy developer Stephen Cloobeck searched in connection to sexual misconduct inquiry into Swalwell

Federal law enforcement agents searched a home in suburban Los Angeles early on Tuesday as part of an investigation into Eric Swalwell, who resigned from Congress amid a wave of sexual misconduct allegations, a law enforcement official and two people familiar with the investigation told the Associated Press.

Agents searched the Beverly Hills home of the wealthy developer and Swalwell donor Stephen Cloobeck, where the Democratic ex-congressman spent time before resigning from office in April, according to the people, who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss the investigation.

Messages seeking comment were left for Swalwell’s attorney and for a lawyer who represented Cloobeck in another matter.

Cloobeck, the founder of the Diamond Resorts International timeshare company, told the New York Post in April that he cut ties with Swalwell as accusations against him mounted.

“I am no longer associated with a man that takes advantage of women,” Cloobeck told the newspaper. “I support women’s rights.”

Tuesday’s search is the latest escalation in the federal investigation of Swalwell – one of several investigations begun after a woman came forward with allegations that he sexually assaulted her twice, including when she worked for him.

The San Francisco Chronicle, followed by CNN, first reported the allegations. CNN also reported that three other women alleged various kinds of sexual misconduct by Swalwell, including sending them unsolicited explicit messages or nude photos.

Asked about the search at Cloobeck’s address, an FBI spokesperson, Lourdes Arocho, said the bureau “early this morning … conducted an operation in the Los Angeles region related to an ongoing criminal investigation.

In August, federal agents seized electronic devices from Swalwell at the San Francisco airport and searched his Washington DC home.

Swalwell, an Iowa native, was elected in 2012 and represented a House district east of San Francisco. He launched a presidential run in April 2019 but ended it a few months later after failing to catch on with voters.

The seven-term lawmaker had been seen as one of the leading candidates in California’s gubernatorial race before dropping out after the allegations surfaced.

The office of the Manhattan district attorney, Alvin Bragg, has said it is also investigating Swalwell after a former staffer told CNN and the Chronicle that Swalwell raped her at a hotel while he was in New York City for a charity gala in 2024.

In a statement, Bragg’s office encouraged “survivors and anyone with knowledge of these allegations to contact” its special victims division.

Los Angeles prosecutors were also said to be weighing charges.

Swalwell was one of Donald Trump’s top antagonists in Congress. He was a prominent member of the House judiciary and intelligence committees during Trump’s first presidency and served as one of several Democratic prosecutors for Trump’s second impeachment after the 6 January 2021 attack on the US Capitol.

Swalwell was removed from the intelligence committee by the then US House speaker, Kevin McCarthy, a California Republican, in 2023 based on Swalwell’s contact with a suspected Chinese spy, Christine Fang.

Fang was reported to have come into contact with Swalwell’s campaign as he was first running for Congress in 2012 and to have participated in fundraising for his 2014 campaign.

Federal investigators alerted Swalwell to their concerns and briefed Congress about Fang in 2015, at which point Swalwell says he cut off contact with her. He was not accused of wrongdoing, and a House ethics committee investigation that was opened in 2021 closed two years later without any action.
