#399. Exercise doesn't just strengthen your heart. It rewires it

Exercise does more than make the heart stronger. New research suggests it also physically reshapes the nerves that control the heart, revealing an unexpected difference between the left and right sides of the body.

Scientists at the University of Bristol (UK) discovered that moderate aerobic exercise changes the structure of nerve cells involved in regulating heart activity. Even more surprisingly, the changes were dramatically different on the two sides of the body. The findings, published in Autonomic Neuroscience, could eventually help doctors develop more precise treatments for irregular heartbeats, chest pain, angina, and stress-induced 'broken-heart' syndrome.

The discovery was made in rats, so further research is needed to determine whether the same changes occur in humans. Nevertheless, it offers a new perspective on how physical activity influences the nervous system that keeps the heart functioning.

Exercise Changes the Heart's Hidden Control System

The heart is regulated partly by the autonomic nervous system, a network of nerves that automatically manages essential functions such as heartbeat, breathing, and digestion. One branch of this system helps the heart beat faster when the body needs more oxygen, such as during exercise or stressful situations.

Small clusters of nerve cells called stellate ganglia play an important role in this process. Located on both sides of the lower neck and upper chest, these nerve hubs send signals that influence heart rate and other aspects of heart function.

The new research suggests that regular exercise changes these nerve clusters in ways scientists had not previously recognized.

Study lead author Dr. Augusto Coppi, Senior Lecturer in Veterinary Anatomy at the University of Bristol, said: "The discovery points to a previously hidden left-right pattern in the body's 'autopilot' system that helps run the heart.

"These nerve clusters act like the heart's dimmer switch and we've shown that regular, moderate exercise remodels that switch in a side-specific way. This could help explain why some treatments work better on one side than the other and, in future, help doctors target therapies more precisely and effectively."

Just 10 Weeks of Exercise Produced Striking Nerve Changes

To investigate how exercise affects these nerve clusters, the researchers examined rats that underwent 10 weeks of moderate aerobic training. Aerobic exercise is physical activity that increases heart rate and breathing, such as brisk walking, swimming, or cycling.

The study involved researchers from University College London (UCL) in the UK, the University of São Paulo (USP), and the Federal University of São Paulo (UNIFESP) in Brazil.

The team used advanced three-dimensional analysis techniques known as stereology to examine the nerve cells. This approach allows scientists to estimate the number, size, and distribution of cells within tissues.

The results revealed an unusual imbalance between the two sides of the body. In the trained rats, the cardiovascular nerve cluster on the right contained roughly four times as many neurons as the cluster on the left, a striking difference from the pattern observed in untrained rats.

The nerve cells also changed in size, but in opposite directions. Neurons on the left nearly doubled in size, while those on the right became slightly smaller.

These findings suggest that exercise does not affect all the nerves controlling the heart equally. Instead, it produces distinct structural changes on each side, potentially providing clues about how the nervous system adapts to regular physical activity.

Could the Discovery Lead to Better Heart Treatments?

The findings may have important implications for treating conditions involving abnormal nerve activity around the heart.

Arrhythmias occur when the heart beats too quickly, too slowly, or irregularly. Angina is chest pain or discomfort typically caused by insufficient blood flow to the heart muscle. Another condition, commonly called 'broken-heart' syndrome, involves a sudden weakening of the heart muscle that is often triggered by intense emotional or physical stress.

In some patients, doctors treat these problems by reducing excessive nerve signals reaching the heart. Procedures such as stellate ganglion nerve blocks temporarily interrupt nerve activity, while denervation involves disrupting certain nerve pathways.

Understanding how the left and right nerve clusters differ could eventually help physicians determine which side should be targeted for the greatest benefit.

Dr. Coppi explained: "Irregular heart rhythms, known as arrhythmias, stress-induced 'broken-heart' syndrome, and certain types of chest pain are often treated by dialing down overactive stellate ganglia -- the paired small nerve hubs in the lower neck/upper chest area that send 'go faster' signals to the heart.

"By mapping how exercise changes these ganglia on each side, the study offers clues that could one day fine-tune procedures like nerve blocks or denervation to the side most likely to help. The findings are early-stage and in rats, so clinical studies would need to follow."

What's Next for Exercise and Heart Research?

Although the structural changes were substantial, scientists still need to establish exactly how they affect heart function.

The research team plans to investigate whether the differences between the left and right nerve clusters influence how the heart behaves during rest and physical activity. They also intend to look for similar patterns in other animal models and eventually in humans.

For human studies, the researchers hope to use noninvasive markers, which could provide information about nerve activity without requiring surgery or tissue removal.

If the same left-right differences are found in people, the discovery could help guide more personalized treatments for arrhythmias, difficult-to-treat angina, and stress-induced 'broken-heart' syndrome.

Dr. Coppi added: "Understanding these left-right differences could help us personalize treatments for heart rhythm disorders and angina. Our next step is to test how these structural changes map onto function and whether similar patterns appear in larger animals and humans."

Materials provided by University of Bristol. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#398. American Airlines forecasts bigger loss in 2026 on fuel spike, plans to slow growth next year

American Airlines forecast deeper losses this year and said it will slow its capacity growth next year as record revenue failed to help the airline recover from a spike in fuel costs.

American said Thursday that it expects to lose between $1 and $1.60 a share, on an adjusted basis in 2026. In July, it forecast adjusted full-year earnings per share between a loss of 65 cents and a profit of 65 cents.

The Fort Worth, Texas-based airline is the fourth major U.S. carrier to cut its forecast for 2026 due to a jump in jet fuel following the start of the Iran war. Rising jet fuel prices have driven up airfare more than 20% from last year, according to airline reports and federal data. Delta Air Lines, United Airlines and Southwest Airlines also slashed their outlooks this month.

Jet fuel is airlines' biggest expense after labor, and the spike has added billions to costs this year. American's fuel bill was up 67.5% in the third quarter alone, from last year to $4.64 billion.

"American delivered record third-quarter revenue and the strongest year-over-year revenue growth in my career, with premium performance leading the way," American CEO Robert Isom said in a news release.

Despite the lower expectations for deeper losses in 2026, Isom said on an earnings call Thursday that he has "great confidence" that the company will return to profitability next year.

The CEO has been under pressure to boost profits at the carrier, which has dramatically trailed profitable rivals Delta and United. American has been investing in new lounges and revamped cabins with more premium seats and said this year that it plans to add seat-back screens again to its narrow-body fleet.

Earlier this month, the airline named a new network chief. It was the latest move in a management shake-up that has included new hires in its operations department and its commercial team. Last year, American brought in a new commercial chief, industry veteran Nat Pieper.

International and premium-travel demand has remained especially robust, executives said. Late last month, American said it would deepen its partnership with Alaska Airlines as the Seattle-based carrier would join American's trans-Atlantic and trans-Pacific joint businesses.

The airline is also closing in on an order for new wide-body airplanes, executives said. CFO Devon May told CNBC on Thursday that the airline has considered the Boeing 787 Dreamliner, Airbus A330 and Airbus A350.

American forecast it will lose between 50 cents and $1.10 a share, adjusted, in the fourth quarter, with revenue expected to be up as much as 22.5% from a year earlier and capacity up between 5.5% and 6.5%.

Here is what American reported in the third quarter compared with Wall Street estimates compiled by LSEG:

For the third quarter, American had a net loss of $269 million or 41 cents a share, compared with $114 million, or 17 cents a share, a year earlier. Adjusting for one-time items, American reported a loss of 28 cents a share. Revenue jumped 19.3% from a year earlier to $16.34 billion.

#396. Can Germany revive its economy before the far-right AfD gains ground?

Conflicts, rising costs and Chinese competition are testing Germany's economic model like never before. Can new growth sectors create jobs and ease voters' economic fears, thereby helping contain the far-right AfD?

Germany's export-led economic model faces unprecedented strain just as support for the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party reaches a new peak.

Chinese rivals are steadily eroding the market share of major German industrial players, while persistently high energy prices, elevated labor costs and excessive red tape are adding to the structural crisis.

After two years of contraction, real GDP edged up only 0.2% last year, according to the Federal Statistical Office, Destatis. Exports to China fell 9.7% in 2025, withGerman vehicle and part shipments plunging by a third.

While there are signs the German economy is turning the corner, the government's latest forecast of 1.3% growth in 2026 is still modest compared with the pre-COVID boom years.

The AfD is currently polling ahead of all other political parties in Germany. The country's many economic challenges have been a major factor in the right-wing populists' rise.

Germany's domestic intelligence agency sees reliable indications that the AfD is right-wing extremist in its entirety.

The party has vowed to put Germany back on a growth path by ending the transition to renewable energies, lowering taxes and renegotiating European Union treaties, while also reforming welfare and sharply restricting immigration.

As Chancellor Friedrich Merz's coalition searches for a fresh growth strategy to fend off the rise in populist politics, the government has set out its high-tech agenda focusing on six priority sectors.

These are artificial intelligence (AI), quantum computing, microelectronics, biotechnology, clean energy (including nuclear fusion) and climate-neutral mobility.

Irene Bertschek, head of the Digital Economy research unit at the Leibniz Center for European Economic Research (ZEW), argues that Germany's research in these "key enabling technologies is very strong, particularly in the area of climate-neutral technologies."

Yet, the results are often commercialized abroad, Bertschek told DW, with the United States leading in AI and China closing the gap quickly.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A report published Monday by the Made for Germany initiative — a group of 147 companies and investors — builds on the government's plan, estimating that these and other new technologies could attract up to €4.1 trillion ($4.6 trillion) in investment by 2035.

Realizing that potential, however, will depend less on today's industrial giants, like Volkswagen, Siemens and BASF.

"It will not primarily be the established companies that conquer these new fields, but innovative newcomers — startups," Oliver Falck, director of the ifo Center for the Economics of Innovation and Digital Transformation, told DW.

The task, Falck said, is to create the right conditions for startups to emerge, including proximity to top universities, access to private capital and enough freedom to experiment.

He argues for state financing during the early-stage "valley of death" — the risky phase between research and commercial viability when private financing is often scarce.

There are clear signs this shift is already happening. Data from Deutsche Bank and startupdetector, a German startup database and market intelligence platform, show more than 3,000 startups were founded in Germany in the first half of 2026, up 88% from the same period last year. One-third of the new ventures have an AI focus.

Venture-capital investment soared 44% to €5.8 billion in the first half of 2026, with firms such as Neura Robotics and drone manufacturers Stark and Quantum Systems benefiting.

US investors, meanwhile, are increasingly buying up Germany's famed Mittelstand — its traditional small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) — with an eye on their specialized technology.

"The German Mittelstand is highly innovative. Now it is essential to invest in key technologies such as artificial intelligence and to tap into the potential that arises at the intersection with established technologies," Bertschek stressed.

The expert, who is also chair of the German government's Commission of Experts for Research and Innovation (EFI), thinks SMEs should make better use of their huge amounts of production data and collaborate with AI startups to help those young firms grow.

Another idea for accelerating the shift to new high-tech sectors comes from Sven Gabor Janszky, chairman of the Leipzig-based futures-research institute 2b AHEAD. He argues that Germany needs special economic zones to help catch the next wave of economic growth.

"For a few specific technologies, you lower the regulations, you recruit ambitious workers and pump money into these zones," Janszky told DW.

This, he added, would follow the examples of Silicon Valley and Chinese mega-cities Shenzhen and Shanghai's Pudong district, and echoes the conditions that helped rebuild the German economy after World War II.

In Janszky's view, these zones would help Germany position itself as a first mover in emerging fields rather than remaining tied to older industrial strengths.

"EU regulations may try to prohibit this move, but Germany will have to overcome them so we can grow the economy again," he added.

The sharp rise in Germany's defense spending will also help several of the high-tech sectors now being prioritized.

According to the International Institute for Strategic Studies (IISS), the defense budget is expected to reach around €120 billion in 2026 — roughly 2.6% of GDP. The government wants this figure to hit 3.5% by 2029.

The resulting procurement will provide a major boost to the production of drones, sensors, communications systems and other dual-use technologies that have both civilian and military applications.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Yet, with major rival China benefiting from much lower energy and worker costs, along with huge government subsidies, Germany will continue to face similar price disadvantages when exporting its new high-end tech. But that doesn't have to spell failure, said Falck, the ifo expert.

"Take the semiconductor industry as an example. It is probably unrealistic to aim for leadership along the entire value chain. But it is enough to lead at individual choke points," he pointed out.

"And that is exactly where Germany is strong: as a supplier of equipment for state-of-the-art chip fabs and as a supplier to the world's AI gigafactories, with power semiconductors."

In other words, with a first-mover advantage, decades of high-quality production data and a focus on specialized niches, Germany can remain a major force in advanced industrial and engineering technologies.

#395. We tested the most popular Ninja appliances, and these are our favorites

The popularity of Ninja and Shark appliances is undeniable. The brand touches nearly every room in your home. It makes vacuums to keep your floors clean, air purifiers to refresh the air, hair stylers for at-home blowouts, and an appliance for every step of the cooking process. But with such an expansive catalog, it can be hard to sort through the best of the brand.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

We wanted to test our way through the best Ninja had to offer in kitchen appliances. We brought the most popular products from the brand into our kitchens to see how the Creami, Slushi, Foodi, and Ninja Kitchen System stacked up.

Read our full review of the Ninja Creami Scoop and Swirl.

If you are an ice cream enthusiast in need of a sweet, frozen treat every night, you need the Ninja Creami Scoop and Swirl. It builds off Ninja's existing Creami technology to spin and churn your homemade pints of ice cream, froyo, and fruit whip. However, the Swirl also has a soft serve dispenser for serving up creamy, fluffy swirls of ice cream. It's the most impressive Creami for the hosts with the most, but it's also just straight-up fun.

Having tested both the basic Creami and the Scoop and Swirl, the latter is the best ice cream maker from Ninja. Its ease of use is exceptional. To use, mix up your concoctions and let the pints freeze for 24 hours. Once frozen, put them in the Creami, where a blade spins through the ice cream to give it a fluffy, smooth texture.

Any model in the Creami lineup is great, but the Swirl is the best because it nails texture. The Swirl manages to get more into the pints when spinning them for a light and spreadable treat. When making my favorite chocolate hazelnut frozen yogurt, it only needed one spin in the Swirl to get the perfect texture. Previously, when I used the Ninja Creami Deluxe, I needed to spin the ice cream several times to get the perfect whipped consistency.

My caution to those interested in a Ninja Swirl is that it's expensive and quite large, occupying a lot of counter space. However, for an ice cream aficionado, it'll be a worthwhile investment. And word of warning: it's so loud, but that will be the case for any Ninja Creami model you go for.

If you're trying to make the decision between investing in a blender or a food processor, the answer is to get both and get them with the Ninja Blender Kitchen System. This blender is for the kitchen multitasker who wants to do it all without shelling out money for multiple appliances. It's for beginners and kitchen professionals alike, as you can use manual settings or use BlendSense technology to take the thought out of it.

Maybe it's because I've been an apartment dweller for so long, but my biggest pet peeve is a one-use appliance. If you're going to invest in a new kitchen tool, you want it to work overtime. That's why I adore the Ninja Detect Kitchen System, it's a full-sized blender, food processor, and personal blender.

Having owned and used multi-use kitchen appliances, I find that you sometimes have to make some sacrifices and lose features. That's not the case with the Ninja Detect Kitchen System. It has an incredibly powerful motor and no matter what attachment you're using, you can choose from a variety of settings with each attachment.

The device uses one base to run everything, which saves space on multiple bases. Just swap out the attachments, whether that be the personal-sized blade and cup or the big 72-ounce pitcher. It's exceptionally powerful and able to quickly mince vegetables or crush ice.

While it's nice to have a single base for the different attachments, it saves space in one sense. However, beware that the attachments are still very large and take up quite a bit of room.

Air fryers are great for anyone, but the Ninja Crispi is made for meal preppers. With its portable design, it stores nicely and can be used on the go. Plus, it actually works. The controls are simple, and the glass baskets come with storage lids for easy food storage.

Since we originally tested this device, it's become a favorite of the Mashable shopping team. Not to mention, the Crispi has gotten quite a bit of attention online as folks seek a plastic-free air fryer, and the Crispi is really the best option available.

There are certain devices I look at and think, there's no way that's going to work, always hoping to be proven wrong. The Ninja Crispi is one of those devices, with its glass basket and air-frying lid, I was skeptical of its efficacy. I'm pleased to say I was wrong. It delivered food that was crispy on the outside and fluffy on the inside.

When it comes to testing air fryer crispiness, my go-to trials are always tofu and frozen French fries. The Ninja Crispi handled both with flying colors. In less than 20 minutes, it air-fried cubes of tofu tossed in cornstarch to crunchy perfection. Frozen French fries also turned out great. I tossed a handful into the container, not bothering to lay them without any overlap, and still, the Ninja Crispi made every fry perfectly crispy on the outside while remaining soft on the inside.

Air fryers are an appliance of convenience, able to get food hot and crispy quickly, and the Ninja Crispi maximizes that with its design that allows you to meal prep and store food directly into glass baskets, where you will then reheat it. The storage containers are all dishwasher safe, a rare trait in air fryer baskets.

Compared to other air fryers, the settings on this device may be a little limited, as you can't adjust the temperature. However, I have tested the Crispi on a variety of foods, and I didn't miss being able to adjust the temperature.

If you've ever felt intimidated by using an espresso machine, the Ninja Luxe Café Premier strikes the perfect balance of user-friendly design with high-end settings.

Plus, if you're a divided household, with latté drinkers or drip coffee loyalists, this machine bridges the gap with the ability to grind and brew both espresso and coffee. This machine may not be for professional baristas, but it will be for someone who has expert taste but prioritizes convenience.

While my partner and I are espresso enthusiasts, any time our parents come into town, we run into the problem of not having a good drip coffee setup. The Ninja Luxe Café Premier is the ideal solution for offering both without any pods. Instead, it grinds beans to serve brew espresso, coffee, and cold brew. It has adjustable grind settings while providing suggested grind settings based on your chosen coffee setting — much easier than troubleshooting on a standard espresso machine.

Using the machine is pretty foolproof. While I do have a barista background and know my way around an espresso machine, I enjoyed just how easy it was to use. The machine tells you exactly how fine to grind your beans, grinds them, and then pulls an espresso shot with a rich layer of crema, all with the press of a button.

Milk frothing is automated on the Luxe Café Premier. The milk pitcher includes a steaming wand as well as a circular frother at the bottom. While I enjoyed the seamlessness of getting steamed milk, this is where the machine won't be for everyone. If you prefer to steam your own silky microfoam for lattés, you won't be able to with this machine, as you can't angle the wand without placing the pitcher on its pedestal.

I've had this machine for over a year and still love it, using it daily to make both espresso, cold brew, and drip coffee.

Put away the blender! If you're going to make frozen drinks this summer, let it be with the Ninja Slushi. It's a little extra but if you're looking to host all summer long and want something to impress your guests, do it with the Ninja Slushi, which freezes and spins drinks until they're icy.

You can make frozen drinks with a lot of different appliances, like a blender or a Ninja Creami, but the most foolproof way is with the Ninja Slushi. It's certainly not a necessary appliance, but if you're a frozen drink fanatic, it's essential.

Unlike the Creami, it freezes drinks in the machine and churns them until it has the perfect slushy texture. It does require a bit of math work, as you need the right sugar or alcohol levels to perfect the texture.

That being said, at $349.99, it's quite expensive. As my colleague Leah Stodart pointed out, people pay twice that for an espresso machine, so depending on your enthusiasm for frozen drinks, it may be a worthy price to pay.

So you only have space for one appliance in your kitchen, and you can't decide between a rice cooker and a slow cooker? Choose the Ninja Foodi Everyday Possible Cooker. This device doesn't look like a lot, essentially a hot plate and non-stick pot, but it's incredible how much it can do. It makes rice in the blink of an eye, can boil a pound of pasta, and slow-cooks food to tender perfection.

Ninja makes some big claims with the Ninja Foodi Everyday PossibleCooker as it supposedly makes rice 50 percent faster than a traditional rice cooker. As the No. 1 fan of my rice cooker, I obviously had to test this claim first. The Ninja Foodi does indeed make rice 50 percent faster — almost.

While my rice cooker cooked one cup of white rice in 25 minutes, the Ninja took just 15 minutes, and even better, it was perfectly fluffy without any overcooked crisp bits on the bottom. And it does much more than that. I made slow-cooked BBQ chicken, and it produced tender, moist chicken that was a crowd-pleaser.

Ninja has plenty of other multicookers, but this one is my favorite because it's the most affordable option and replaces multiple appliances at once.

There's a big camp of people who need their water to be flavored. If you happen to be in that group, then the Ninja Thirsti might prove itself to be an essential kitchen appliance. It delivers both sparkling and still options and can infuse water with flavor. There are subtle, unsweetened options like a hint of lime or caffeinated, sweet fruity options for at-home energy drinks.

Having previously used Sodastream carbonated drink makers, I was pleased by Ninja's higher-tech spin on CO2 machines. Rather than pumping CO2 into bottles of water, the Ninja Thirsti delivers personalized glasses of flavor-infused beverages. It allows you to adjust flavor and carbonation levels for a customized drink.

The flavor add-in is what sets the Ninja Thirsti apart from other drink makers. With others, you need to drop in flavor after carbonation, but the Ninja Thirsti infuses it right into your cup, taking out the guesswork of flavoring your drink. However, to use this feature, you have to use Ninja's own flavor pods.

There's one part of the Ninja Thirsti that I really don't like, and it's that the brand recommends using cold water for the best product. For room temperature water enthusiasts, this is not ideal. Plus, it requires refrigerating the water ahead of time, which requires some forethought. Not to mention, the water tank's lid isn't super secure, leaving it susceptible to spillage.

Otherwise, this is a fantastic carbonated drink maker, best for the flavored water devotee.

There are plenty of kitchen appliance brands out there, so why Ninja? Generally, we find its products to be some of the best value products. They tend to be the most affordable options on the market that still deliver excellent performance.

Plus, Ninja products are really beginner-friendly devices that are easy to use with minimal setup required. The appliances bring ease with delicious end products. The Creami is a perfect example of this. Traditional ice cream making requires a bit of skill, knowing how to cook custards to the right temperature with hours of churning. But the Creami simplifies ice cream making with a user-friendly product that still delivers a fantastic end result.

Just because they're beginner-friendly devices doesn't mean they're only for beginners. They're convenient devices with professional-level performance that help develop a more efficient kitchen.

Having rounded up the best Ninja kitchen appliances means we tested a lot of products and not all of them made it to the list, two notable exceptions being the Ninja Creami Deluxe and Foodi Grill and Air Fryer.

The Ninja Deluxe was my introduction to the Creami lineup, and it is great. It spun up creamy frozen confections and even featured a frozen drink setting. However, compared to the Swirl, it required several re-spins to get the right texture.

Meanwhile, the Foodi Grill was another great multicooker from Ninja. The indoor grill made delicious, juicy chicken breasts with actual char marks. Plus, its air-frying capabilities were a little too good. It got things crispy fast, but often felt like it dried out food in the process. Plus, it was just so large that it didn't feel like a practical device for most people.

That being said, these devices are good, but not in our all-star line-up.

Each device on this list was tested by a Mashable expert. To thoroughly evaluate each device, we used them in our kitchen for weeks at a time. We cooked a variety of meals in each appliance and assessed the following qualities in each device:

Did the device have a learning curve? How much set-up did it require? Are its parts dishwasher safe?

: Does the device have multiple uses? How effective is it at each use?

How large is the device? Is it difficult to store in small spaces?

How does its price match up with its overall functionality? If it's expensive, does it offer multi-functionality? Or if it's cheap, is it still effective?

Samantha Mangino, is an e-reader expert. She's tested all the Kindle and Kobo e-readers, diving into the buzziest models. Her love of reading extends beyond the e-reader beat, covering books and authors, too. In the tech space, she covers tablets, smart rings, and headphones.

#394. Valve Steam Frame Review: Costly and Inconsistent

Valve has had a complicated relationship with virtual reality gaming. Its Steam client—the number one distribution platform for PC games—has long supported VR titles and works with headsets from other manufacturers. But the company's flagship VR headset, 2019’s Valve Index, never quite escaped its niche. It saw a huge sales spike in the run-up to the release of headline title Half-Life: Alyx, but by February 2025, it accounted for only 13.2 percent of VR headsets used on Steam.

Enter the Steam Frame, Valve’s second attempt at snatching the VR gaming crown. More than a hardware refresh, it promises to reimagine PC VR gaming, operating as a stand-alone headset like the Meta Quest 3, the current reigning champion, while letting users seamlessly stream games from a more powerful remote machine. It mostly succeeds—but a few issues hold it back.

Specs-wise, the Steam Frame is one of the better VR headsets, though far from a powerhouse. It packs a Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 chip—a slightly dated offering, having debuted in 2023—with 16 GB of RAM, and either 256 GB (at the $1,059 price) or 1 TB ($1,299) of storage, plus a microSD slot for expansion, allowing it to install and run games locally.

It’s aesthetically pleasing, with a sleek black shell and glossy front that have given it a futuristic look. More importantly, it’s comfortable to wear, weighing 440 grams—75 grams lighter than the Quest 3. Valve smartly moved the 21.6-watt-hour Lithium-ion battery to the rear of the head strap for better balance, and thanks to cozy padding, it doesn’t feel hot against your head, even when running demanding games. The cable linking the battery to the headset makes adjusting the elastic straps a little fiddly, though.

The only real comfort drawback is if you wear glasses. While the Steam Frame comes with a spacer to add more depth (which easily clips on magnetically between the inner headset and the padded face gasket), the internal dimensions were still too narrow for me to wear with any of my spectacles. Custom Zenni prescription lenses are available if contact lenses aren’t an option, but it’s a pricey add-on.

Once it’s on your head, dual speakers push audio down the arms directly to your ears, with physical volume controls easily accessible on the left side of the headset. The audio lacks bass but has good directionality, bringing those immersive VR experiences to life. Dual microphones let you use voice controls, while outward-facing cameras enable positional tracking and provide a pass-through view—albeit monochrome with everything looking bluish. A dial at the top lets you adjust lens spacing to your required interpupillary distance, and an inner switch locks it in place.

The lenses themselves are decent, but again not spectacular. Valve uses two LCD pancake lenses (though rumors already hint at a revised OLED model), with a per-eye resolution of 2160 x 2160. That means sharp visuals and legible text—I could easily read fine details on notepads in Half-Life: Alyx, for instance—but you can discern pixels if you peer closely. The real win comes with a 110-degree field of view, enabling wide spans of virtual real estate, and refresh rates ranging from 72 to 120 Hz, with an experimental mode boosting it up to 144 Hz. As a result, games can look incredibly smooth when running on the Steam Frame.

The controllers might be the best part of the package. The dual grips mimic the Quest 3 controllers but outdo them with a full controller layout spread across the pair. They boast the same highly precise Tunneling Magnetoresistance thumbsticks as the regular Steam Controller, along with a full D-pad on the left, four ABXY face buttons on the right, two shoulder triggers and an underside button on each—squeeze that in, and the grip switches from controller mode to laser pointer to make selections in VR.

Position detection is great, with full six-degrees-of-freedom tracking, while excellent haptics deliver solid rumble and per-finger capacitive sensing for nuanced, precise interactions with virtual objects. You can also pair a regular controller to the Frame via Bluetooth, but the included ones are so good you likely won’t ever feel the need to—these are now the pinnacle of multipurpose VR controllers.

After setup—a now-familiar routine for VR gamers that includes running necessary updates and drawing safe boundaries so you don't knock into furniture—you’ll sign in to your Steam account directly on the Steam Frame. It runs SteamOS 3 just like the Steam Deck or Steam Machine, the familiar home screen now floating in a VR void. Navigate to your library, and you can install whatever you like directly onto the Frame.

Unfortunately, while there’s a filter for games officially categorized as compatible, it’s unclear what rubrics Valve uses, or how many games have even been tested. Of my 1,300-plus-strong Steam library, only a meager 18 were marked “Great on Frame.” The strangest absence from that list is the totemic Half-Life: Alyx (which comes free with every Steam Frame purchase), especially odd given it runs without issue. Other dedicated VR games are hit and miss when installed directly on the Frame—I tried Obduction (opened and crashed, repeatedly), Doctor Who: The Edge of Time (ran with juddery performance and could only move by clicking in on the thumbsticks), Dungeons of Eternity (fine, bar some aiming and tracking issues), and Surgeon Simulator VR (totally fine).

You’re not limited to VR games on Frame. You can install and play anything in your library as 2D on a vast virtual cinema screen. Don’t bother with demanding AAA games—the hardware isn’t up to running them—but indies and AA-tier games can look phenomenal like this. Playing Hades II blown up to Imax-scale was a fantastic experience. Again, performance is unreliable—gorgeously animated Metroidvania MIO: Memories in Orbit launched when installed locally, but couldn’t load its assets, leaving me standing on invisible platforms and with no way to navigate the world.

The Frame also seems to default 2D games to 720p resolution. You can override this, but it may drop performance. Frame rates for all of the above games hopped all over the place, though mostly hovering around the 72 or 90 fps benchmarks. It feels like Valve has a lot of updating work to do to ensure Steam Frame can reliably function as a gaming platform for VR or non-VR titles. Right now, running anything is a gamble.

The most annoying thing is that the Steam Frame isn't designed solely as a stand-alone headset—pairing it with a more powerful desktop (or laptop) should, in theory, let the PC do the grunt work and stream the output to the Frame. The package includes a dedicated 6-GHz wireless adapter—plug it into any device with a USB-A 3.0 port running Steam, and it should provide a dedicated network for the Frame. The headset connects to both the adapter and your home network, creating a mesh that reduces latency by using whichever has the least network traffic.

In practice, I could barely get it working. I tested it on a Windows 11 PC, a Windows 10 PC, and a Steam Machine—in every case, the Frame could connect to the remote device through the home network, but the adapter appeared as connected but unpaired. When it detected and created that mesh network (on rare occasions), streaming was flawless, but when connected only through the home network, performance was laggy enough to irritate.

Whether playing natively or through streaming, battery life is poor—expect two hours at the high end for locally run games, and maybe three if you’re purely streaming. Such short playing time makes the inability to physically connect Steam Frame to a PC with a cable all the worse, which would also overcome the streaming issues.

The biggest hurdle for most VR gamers is likely price. Like all of Valve’s recent tech, the Steam Frame is a casualty of RAM and storage shortages across the electronics industry. Like the Steam Machine, it feels almost compromised, especially that three-year-old processor. It doesn’t feel like $1,000-plus worth of headset, especially when its overall performance only just about beats the significantly cheaper Quest 3.

Yet despite its many early hiccups, the Steam Frame is incredible fun. The VR titles that work without issue are brilliant to play here, and the cinema mode for 2D games is the best implementation of that feature I’ve experienced on a VR headset. Streaming shows potential if Valve can work the networking kinks. If it gets that polish over the coming months as Valve pushes out inevitable updates, it could really shine.

I especially love how it’s unapologetically a gaming platform. It’s not trying to claim VR is useful for productivity, or office tasks—that ship sank along with Apple’s Vision Pro—and mercifully, there’s not even a hint of a blighted “Metaverse” here. The Steam Frame is for gaming, end of story. If Valve can iron out its software issues and make sure more games run on it properly, it’ll be a great way to play.

#392. Doctor removes prostate from 35 miles away in first telerobotic surgery in US

The patient was at a facility in Minneola, Florida, while the surgeon operated remotely from another facility

A Florida surgeon has performed the first telerobotic operation between two US hospitals, removing the prostate of a man with cancer from 35 miles away, according to officials.

Dr Vipul Patel, founder and medical director of the Global Robotics Institute at AdventHealth Celebration in suburban Orlando, on Sunday performed an investigational robotic prostatectomy on a 66-year-old prostate cancer patient who volunteered for a clinical study evaluating a robotic telesurgery system.

The patient, identified as Mark, was at an AdventHealth facility in Minneola, Florida, while Patel operated remotely from another AdventHealth facility in the community of Celebration. Study co-principal investigator and surgeon Marcio Moschovas provided onsite support to Mark during the procedure, an AdventHealth news release said.

Mark was recovering well after the procedure, AdventHealth told ABC.

The surgery was part of a clinical research study conducted under a Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved investigational device exemption. Such an authorization permits the system to be studied but does not constitute FDA approval or clearance.

The procedure done on Mark came after 10 investigational telesurgeries between Orlando, Florida, and Luanda, Angola, while marking the study’s expansion into US hospitals, AdventHealth’s news release said.

“For generations, access to highly specialized care has often depended on where a patient lives,” a statement attributed to Patel said. His statement added: “This study allows us to evaluate whether telesurgery could one day help reduce geographic barriers to specialized treatment.”

Telesurgery allows surgeons to control robotic instruments remotely but researchers are still evaluating its safety, reliability and cybersecurity. AdventHealth said no previous reports had documented a telesurgery procedure between two US locations and cautioned that Sunday’s surgery did not establish the technology’s general clinical performance.

Robotic prostatectomy carries risks including bleeding, infection, incontinence and erectile dysfunction – as well as the possibility of switching to open surgery. Telesurgery adds risks such as equipment or connection failures and cybersecurity threats, making it essential for there to be an on-site team to take over if needed.

Sunday’s procedure in Florida unfolded after a similar headline-making operation just months earlier, when a surgeon in London used a four-armed robot equipped with a 3D camera to remove a man’s prostate in Gibraltar, about 1,500 miles away.

The London-Gibraltar surgery was successful, with a lag of just 0.06 seconds between the surgeon’s movements and the robot’s response.

#390. Do spiders dream? Scientists just found a surprising clue

Scientists at the University of Bonn have discovered that spiders experience distinct stages of sleep, including a REM-like state that shares surprising similarities with human sleep. During this stage, the spiders move their eyes and twitch their muscles, behaviors also associated with REM (rapid eye movement) sleep in humans and other vertebrates.

The findings provide the first comprehensive experimental confirmation that spiders experience genuine, regulated sleep rather than simply remaining inactive. They also raise intriguing questions about how sleep evolved and whether animals with very different nervous systems might share some of its fundamental features.

Researchers at the University of Bonn's Institute of Organismic Biology made the discovery while studying the Australian jumping spider Portia fimbriata, a small but remarkably sophisticated predator.

A Tiny Spider With Remarkable Hunting Skills

The Australian jumping spider Portia fimbriata is only about the size of a one-euro coin when fully grown, but its hunting abilities make it particularly interesting to scientists.

Unlike many spiders that primarily capture insects, members of the genus Portia specialize in hunting other spiders. They use a variety of sophisticated strategies to catch their prey, including imitating the vibrations made by struggling insects trapped in spiderwebs.

These deceptive signals can lure web-building spiders toward what appears to be an easy meal, only for them to encounter the hunting Portia instead.

Such complex behaviors suggest that these spiders have unusual learning abilities, making them valuable subjects for investigating the relationship between sleep, nervous system function, and cognition.

Scientists Discover Two Distinct Stages of Spider Sleep

Although sleep has been extensively studied in humans and other vertebrates, scientists still know relatively little about how it works in animals with very different nervous systems.

To investigate whether spiders experience genuine sleep and whether that sleep includes different stages, the researchers closely monitored Portia fimbriata over several days and nights.

They discovered that the spiders exhibit two clearly distinguishable sleep stages. One of these resembles REM sleep, the phase in humans associated with rapid eye movements, muscle activity, and vivid dreaming.

During this REM-like stage, the spiders displayed characteristic eye movements and muscle twitches. These observations suggest that some features of sleep previously studied mainly in vertebrates may also occur in spiders.

The researchers emphasize that identifying REM-like movements does not establish whether spiders dream. However, the discovery provides important evidence that sleep in these animals is more complex than previously understood.

How Researchers Confirmed That Spiders Really Sleep

To study the spiders' sleeping behavior, the team placed individual Portia fimbriata spiders in plastic dishes measuring 12 centimeters in diameter. They monitored the animals under different lighting conditions to examine their daily activity and rest cycles.

Infrared cameras allowed the researchers to observe the spiders throughout the night without relying on visible light. Detailed recordings captured their sleeping positions, periods of quiet rest, and episodes of REM-like movement.

The team also conducted experiments to determine whether the spiders were genuinely asleep rather than simply sitting motionless.

Using sound stimuli, the researchers measured how quickly the spiders responded to disturbances. These tests helped distinguish sleep from ordinary inactivity.

Another important part of the investigation involved sleep deprivation. The researchers examined whether the spiders displayed "sleep rebound," meaning they compensated for interrupted sleep by increasing their subsequent sleep.

Together, the behavioral observations and experimental tests provided strong evidence that Portia fimbriata experiences regulated sleep with distinct stages.

Studying Spider Sleep Presented Unusual Challenges

Determining whether a spider is truly asleep is considerably more complicated than observing a sleeping human.

Because spiders sense their surroundings differently from people, the researchers first had to identify a sound stimulus that would produce a consistent response during their experiments.

They also needed to avoid assuming that the observed eye movements and muscle twitches necessarily represented the same processes seen during human REM sleep.

One major limitation was that the researchers could not measure the spiders' brain activity. Instead, they relied entirely on carefully documented behavior to identify different sleep states.

This required extensive high-resolution video recordings collected over many days and nights, producing enormous amounts of data for analysis.

Despite these obstacles, the combination of behavioral monitoring, response testing, and sleep disruption experiments allowed the researchers to build a detailed picture of how these spiders sleep.

What Spider Sleep Could Reveal About Evolution

The discovery has implications that extend well beyond spiders.

Most scientific knowledge about sleep comes from studying vertebrates, particularly mammals and birds. Finding comparable sleep features in animals with radically different nervous systems could help researchers understand when and why different types of sleep evolved.

The results also open new opportunities in behavioral science, neuroscience, and chronobiology, the study of biological rhythms.

By investigating sleep in a wider variety of animals, scientists hope to identify features that are shared across species and uncover the fundamental biological functions that sleep may have served early in evolutionary history.

The researchers argue that future investigations should move beyond traditionally studied animals and include species with unusual adaptations or much simpler nervous systems.

Such comparisons could reveal whether certain sleep mechanisms are widespread throughout the animal kingdom or developed independently in different evolutionary lineages.

Even after decades of intensive research, many basic questions about sleep remain unanswered, including important mysteries surrounding human sleep.

Could Spiders Dream? Scientists Have More Questions

The University of Bonn team is now expanding its research to investigate how sleep, particularly REM-like sleep, varies across different spider species.

Scientists are also comparing spiders sleeping in natural environments with those observed in laboratories to determine how surroundings influence their behavior.

Another major goal is to investigate the relationship between sleep and cognitive abilities. Researchers want to understand what happens inside the brains of spiders and insects during sleep, and whether different sleep stages contribute to learning and other mental processes.

Environmental changes are also becoming an important focus. The team plans to examine how climate change and human activities, especially artificial light at night (ALAN), may interfere with the sleep patterns of spiders and other arthropods.

For now, the findings establish that even a tiny jumping spider can experience a surprisingly complex form of sleep. Whether spiders actually dream remains unknown, but their nighttime behavior is offering scientists new clues about one of biology's most enduring mysteries.

Materials provided by University of Bonn. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#389. Cruise lines chase luxury travelers with suites for up to $25,000 a night

The latest push into high-end travel is taking place on the high seas, as cruise lines increasingly lean on luxury offerings.

On Regent's Seven Seas Splendor, an all-luxury ship, cabins cost anywhere from $1,800 to $15,000 per night for its top-of-the-line booking, the Regent Suite.

The difference is not just the roughly 5,100 square feet of space. The top-tier suite has a Steinway piano, a private dining room and a wraparound deck with a hot tub and a view comparable to the one from the ship's bridge.

Multiple passengers wanted the suite for the Splendor’s 151-night global cruise that embarks in January, Regent told CNBC, and it is almost always occupied.

Regent is setting its sights even higher, as the cruise line plans to debut the Seven Seas Prestige in December, its first new ship class in a decade. Its top suite will cost $25,000 per night.

While megaships with surfing and waterslides can carry more than 7,500 passengers, the smaller, elite ships — some carrying fewer than a thousand passengers — are powering profits for companies like Norwegian Cruise Line Holdings. The company owns luxury lines Regent and Oceania.

Norwegian CEO John Chidsey has said he's focused on sharpening the luxury brands' positioning, improving the products and guest experience, and strengthening their commercial performance.

Regent and Oceania, he said, are in good shape for 2027, though Chidsey did not break out metrics between the companies.

Cruise competitors are also betting big on luxury offerings, investing in new ships and more specialized experiences.

Regent competes with established luxury cruise lines including Royal Caribbean's Silversea and Carnival's Seabourn, as well as newer entrants carrying hotel names into the market. Four Seasons, Aman and Orient Express are among the brands bringing luxury hospitality to yacht-style vessels.

The cruise industry projects the luxury cruise fleet will grow from 104 ships in 2026 to at least 130 by 2036.

And while global cruise passengers are forecast to increase about 11% between 2025 and 2028, according to the Cruise Lines International Association, or CLIA, the industry's investment dollars are increasingly flowing to luxury and expedition brands, with orders rising for smaller ships and fewer berths through the next decade.

From 2020 through 2025, there has been a 71% increase in the number of luxury ships and an 84% increase in berths, according to CLIA. The orders indicate another 26% jump in the number of ships through the end of 2029 and a 32% increase in capacity.

Other hospitality companies are investing and expanding their uber-upscale offerings, too. Hotels, casinos, airlines, restaurants, spas and other travel and leisure companies see the trend: Affluent customers remain willing to pay for more space, personal service and distinctive experiences.

United Airlines this year launched its larger Polaris Studio suites with privacy doors and caviar service. American Airlines increased its lie-flat capacity and reported on Thursday that its premium passenger unit revenue rose 18.5% in the third quarter, compared with 14.4% in its main cabin.

Marriott’s second-quarter results showed luxury revenue per available room growing faster than the broader hotel business in the U.S. and Canada, rising 9.1% year over year, compared with 5% for all Marriott hotels in the region. In June, CEO Anthony Capuano told CNBC there's an “almost insatiable appetite” for luxury travel.

Wynn Resorts, which caters almost exclusively to high-end customers, has opted to maintain its high room rates in Las Vegas even as visitation has slowed over the past year, rather than filling every room with travelers looking for a bargain.

Asked onstage at the Global Gaming Expo in September whether Wynn is extracting more profit from fewer customers, CEO Craig Billings said, "We will always focus on what's the most comfortable experience for the customer and where can we drive the highest possible EBITDA."

But offering luxury vacations doesn't guarantee a company's fortunes.

The Ritz-Carlton Yacht Collection, for instance, has struggled with occupancy despite major marketing campaigns and has accumulated nearly $700 million in losses since 2017, according to the Financial Times.

More competition in the space could undercut the perception of scarcity. As more companies add luxury products, they have to convince travelers that the experience is worth the price.

Las Vegas Sands reported earnings Wednesday that missed expectations for revenue and profit, hurt by the high-end segments in both Macao and Singapore.

"When the customers don't show up, it's a little problematic," CEO Patrick Dumont said on the company's earnings call.

Dumont said he couldn't point to any single cause for the softness, adding, "perhaps people are not feeling as good about the market," but he said he believes this quarter is an aberration.

CNBC asked Dumont during the NBA China Games in Macao on Oct. 9 whether luxury travel could become overcrowded.

In response, he pointed to the long-term wealth creation in Asia and said the challenge was making experiences customers want to return to.

“It’s not about, are there enough customers,” he said. “It’s, can we create enough of an interesting set of experiences where people find us indispensable in their lives.”

#388. Ukraine: Life near the front line in Sloviansk

As Russian continues to attack Sloviansk in eastern Ukraine, thousands of residents refuse to leave or struggle to find a way out. For many, poverty, family ties and beloved pets make evacuation painfully difficult.

A few days ago, three Russian aerial bombs struck a residential neighborhood on the outskirts of Sloviansk. One hit a five-story apartment building, injuring seven people. Along with neighboring Kramatorsk, Sloviansk is one of the last major towns in the eastern Ukrainian region of Donetsk still under Ukrainian control. Since summer, the city has been a combat zone.

In a courtyard surrounded by damaged apartment blocks, a dusty carpet hangs over the metal frame of a swing set. Tetjana, a pensioner who lives in the building across the street, has brought it out of her rubble-strewn apartment to clean it. She was in her kitchen when the bombs struck.

"The bomb hit the second floor, and a woman who lived on the fourth floor was screaming," she tells DW. "They managed to rescue her before that entire section of the building collapsed," she adds.

Despite being offered the opportunity to evacuate, Tetjana has yet to leave Sloviansk. "I don't know where I would go," she says. Rent can cost up to €400 ($449) a month. Tetjana's pension is the equivalent of just €67 ($75) a month. Even with monthly relocation assistance of about €40 ($45), she could not make ends meet. "I couldn't live on that. Nowhere!"

Meanwhile, electricity and water are still available in her neighborhood, but the gas supply has been cut off. "We'll get through this," Tetjana says hopefully. "Everything will be OK. That's what I tell everyone. I want that so much."

Days after the attack, the few residents remaining in the damaged buildings were still clearing out their apartments. But another attack hit as they worked. This time, the bombs struck a building housing a state-run emergency assistance center and the Red Cross. While firefighters put out the blaze, rescue workers and volunteers retrieved belongings left inside and cleared away broken glass. Eight people were injured in the attack.

"I was on the balcony when there was a deafening bang. It felt as if someone had hit me over the head," says Natalja, describing one of the recent attacks. The pensioner comes to this part of the city to care for her twin sister. "She can no longer get around. Today, I couldn't bring her bread, only crispbread," she tells DW. Although shops and a few stalls remain at the burned-out market, bread is not available every day.

"I tried to persuade my sister to leave. I would go to a nursing home and she would go to a hospital. But her dog is sick, and I have one, too," Natalja says, noting that pets are not allowed during evacuations. "I can't leave my sister behind. She is worried and angry with me, as if everything were my fault. I have no strength left."

Natalja slowly sets off for home, "so I can get there before dark." The city center is nearly 4 kilometers (2.5 miles) away. Public transportation has been suspended since 30 people were killed in an attack on two buses in neighboring Kramatorsk.

The center of Sloviansk is heavily damaged. Few people are out on the streets, though a handful of shops remain open. Of the city's 100,000 residents, about 20,000 remain, most of them elderly people who are unable to leave.

Vasyl, a pensioner, is from the village of Mayaky, north of Sloviansk. A neighbor drove him into the city center, where he sat down for lunch in a cafe. "We're used to living under fire by now," he says. "But it's a bad habit. We should have broken it long ago."

Vasyl wants peace for his homeland. But he rejects Russian President Vladimir Putin's demand that the part of the Donetsk region under Ukrainian control be handed over to Moscow.

Two elderly women talk on a street covered with nets to protect against drones. "I can't remember the last time I slept through the night," says Iryna, a pensioner. "It's terrible, but I have nowhere to go. I'm supposed to leave my two cats behind, but I love them so much. They're all I have."

"We can only hide, trembling, and pray to God that everything will be all right," Iryna continues. "When I have to go to the market, I don't know if I'll make it there alive."

Russian drones attacked the central market several weeks ago, injuring at least three people. Since then, fewer vendors and customers have been coming to the market.

Valeriya sells women's underwear. With kindergartens closed, she has to bring her 5-year-old daughter Sofia to work. Their neighborhood in Sloviansk is not yet subject to the mandatory evacuation of families with children.

"We're used to this life by now. We were evacuated in 2022, but later returned. Now we're waiting to be evacuated again," Valeriya says.

Sofia races through the market aisles with her friend Anya, who is a year older. Anya's mother is a soldier defending her hometown. Her grandmother, Tetjana, sits nearby selling men's clothing. She, too, has to look after her granddaughter while working.

Tetjana has started shipping her belongings to the Dnipropetrovsk region, where she hopes to start a new life. She is sending them while the post office is still open. "It hurts to see our Ukraine being destroyed," she says, fighting back tears. "Who will rebuild our cities?"

She hopes to return one day. But the fate of Bakhmut weighs on her mind. "Look at Bakhmut. There are no houses left. I'm counting on our boys to hold our cities, even though it's very difficult." Bakhmut, about 50 kilometers (31 miles) from Sloviansk, fell to Russian forces in 2023 after months of heavy fighting.

The women see little hope for a ceasefire. "Putin won't want one. And our younger generation will ask us what we fought for in the first place," Tetjana says. She believes Russia must withdraw from Ukrainian territory.

Drones buzz overhead as gunfire echoes across the market. "It's hard for our boys to shoot them down," Tetjana says. "But they're doing their best."

This article was originally published in Ukrainian.
