#387. The 13 best headphones of 2026: We tested the top contenders from Sony, Apple, Bose, and Beats

In 2026, a pair of headphones or earbuds is pretty much non-optional. That's why there are hundreds of different headphones and earbuds in varying shapes, sizes, styles, colors, and, of course, prices. These days, you can grab a random pair off the digital shelf without too much research and find that they do the job just fine. However, if you're tired of settling for "just fine," then it's worth investing in the best headphones available.

But be warned: The latest generation of flagship noise-cancelling headphones from the likes of Apple, Sony, and Bose will set you back $450 or more, though we have options to recommend for every price range.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

To help you find the best pair of headphones possible, our experts test headphones year-round, from popular models to new releases to budget picks (you can learn more about our detailed testing standards below). The result of that vetting is this guide, which we keep updated regularly to give you the most accurate and current purchasing information possible.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort Ultra headphones (2nd gen).

Those who want the best of the best: For anyone who's yet to buy into the Bose QuietComfort Ultra line, but wants noise-cancelling headphones that can easily handle long hours of wear, there's no better choice than the second generation Bose QuietComfort Ultra headphones.

The best noise-cancelling headphones aren't much use if they squeeze your head too tight or prevent you from wearing accessories like earrings or glasses comfortably. In other words, comfort is key — we were big fans of Bose's first gen QC Ultra headphones for exactly this reason.

With the second generation of the headphones, Bose makes minor improvements to the soundscape, noise-cancelling, and somehow the wear. They also fixed other bugs that detracted from the otherwise premium experience of using the QC Ultras.

As a first step, Bose upped the battery life from 24 to 30 hours per charge with ANC. They also added options for wired playback with the USB-C cable (which can simultaneously charge the headphones) or the auxiliary cable for passive playback (which wasn't available on the first gen headphones). In tandem with the battery life, these headphones become much stronger contenders for travel.

For day-to-day use, Bose's noise cancelling remains as solid as ever (though Sony's XM6s are ever so slightly more adept at handling high pitched frequencies), while offering an easy adjustment on 10 different levels of ANC via the companion app. As for the listening experience, the immersive audio remains as lackluster as it did on the first gen, but the overall sound profile came through richer and more robust when we compared the headphones side by side. Bose also added quality of life adjustments like better wear detection, and a lay flat mode that puts the headphones into a low power mode when they're placed face down. Overall, it's hard to beat this blend of sound quality, ANC, and comfort, making the second iteration of the QC Ultras one of our top picks for headphones.

Read our full review of the Sony WH-1000XM6 headphones.

Audiophiles and custom equalizer fans: After three years, Sony's flagship wireless headphones finally got an update. If you love Sony headphones and have an old pair of XM4s, then we can definitely recommend an upgrade. However, if you just purchased a pair of XM5s, then it might be harder to justify an instant upgrade. That said, the active noise cancellation is better than ever, and there are noticeable improvements to audio quality (even if they lose out by a smidge to the latest Technics AZ100 earbuds). Besides music lovers, travelers will also appreciate the new Sony cans, as they fold up and come with an improved travel case.

Mashable's Tech Editor Timothy Beck Werth reviewed the XM6 headphones ahead of their launch, and he was impressed with the overall quality. As Werth wrote in his review, "The new Sony headphones offer impressive audio quality, and they definitely improve on the XM5 models. The active noise cancellation is particularly phenomenal...I really appreciated the overall balance, depth, and quality of the audio. For the XM6, Sony audio engineers worked with music mastering engineers here in the United States, and the results are clear when you listen to music. Like all headphone reviewers, I have a dedicated playlist for testing new audio products, and I found that vocal-heavy tracks sound particularly great, with noticeable improvements over the XM5s."

Sony introduced (or, rather, brought back) a major design change for the XM6 headphones — they fold up for easier storage. They're definitely more compact and easier to travel with compared to the previous-gen model. The overall design looks similar, so if you're a fan of the Sony aesthetic, you'll still appreciate how they look.

We do wish there had been slightly bigger improvements after a three-year wait, and the battery life (30 hours with ANC engaged) is the same as the XM5 model, which we also loved. These headphones did arrive with a higher sticker price, so if you can't quite swing the $448 price tag, look for the excellent XM5 headphones on sale.

Value fiends: If you don't particularly care to have the most premium pair of headphones on the market (or care to spend several hundred dollars on them), the JLab JBuds Lux ANC headphones more than get the job done. They have functional ANC, decent comfort, great sound balance, and a killer battery life, all with a design that doesn't feel overtly budget-friendly.

At their regular sale price of around $50, these are about a ninth the price of the latest Sony headphones, and a seventh of the price of the Bose QuietComfort headphones, which feel like these headphones' premium analogue.

With budget headphones, it's good to understand what you're trading off from the jump. In the case of these JLab headphones, the ANC will dampen a good amount of environmental sound, from talking coworkers to loud fans, but it won't completely silence the world around you. The app can lag some (we had to exit out of it a few times during testing when it froze), and the fit of these headphones might not be for everyone. For one of our testers, these headphones squeezed their head more than usual (though some users on Reddit called these the most comfortable headphones they've tried). Regardless of the fit, you're likely to hear some creaking from time to time when you move your head around.

At $80, these are all cons that may not be ideal, but are fair for the price point. At these headphones' frequent sale prices that hover in the $50 to $55 range, these cons are even easier to swallow, especially when you consider how these headphones actually sound.

To be clear, you'll likely need to play around with the EQ some to find your perfect settings — we found the first preset to be on the bass-heavy end. Once you do, it's impressive the way these headphones can balance a song. Every part of Panic! At The Disco's "Northern Downpour" shone through, from the vocals, lead guitar, bass, and piano. For budget headphones, we might expect some muddying through these elements, but the JLab JBuds held up great. If we're being nitpicky, The Beatles' "In My Life" came through somewhat dimmer sounding than expected, but not necessarily muddy.

Beyond the sound signature, these headphones have a reasonable amount of customization with the app, especially for their price point. You can adjust the button commands on one of the three on-ear buttons, adjust the ANC intensity with a slider in the app, and create your own custom EQ settings. Multipoint pairing is available, as are spatial audio, music, and movie modes.

Check out our full guide to the best Sony headphones.

Students, casual users, and anyone looking for great sound on the cheap: The new price of entry for flagship noise-cancelling headphones? A cool $450, at least. So, if you're looking for wireless headphones with true active noise cancellation but don't want to spend more than $100, then go with Sony's mid-range noise-cancellers. They have Sony's signature sound (and signature all-plastic design, sadly), and you can almost always find them on sale for under $100 at Amazon and other retailers.

These headphones perform much better than you'd expect a pair of $100 cans to perform. They have surprisingly impressive ANC and multipoint pairing, as well as impressive sound — hardly a surprise with a pair of Sony headphones.

As our reviewer Alex Bracetti wrote, "The WH-CH720 employs 30mm drivers and exclusive brand technologies to broaden sound performance. Expect a vibrant mix of impactful bass with striking mids and highs. Turning on DSEE raises fidelity on poor-quality recordings, which many luxury headphones can’t do. You can tweak the sound to your liking via the EQ, though the headphones sound great on their default setting."

Read our full review of the Sony WF-1000XM6 earbuds.

Audiophiles (earbuds edition) with a side of wanting the best possible ANC: While there are plenty of options for great ANC earbuds that don't cost several hundred dollars, if you're committed to getting the best all-around pair of earbuds currently available, there's no need to look any further than the Sony WF-1000XM6.

Excellent sound quality meets customizability, some of the best ANC in the market, and battery life that can take you through a work day of wear without a hitch.

Bose's latest earbuds release wasn't going to make it easy for other brands to upstage them, but the Sony XM6 earbuds managed the task.

Though we maintain that the QC 2 earbuds are ultimately the most comfortable earbuds money can buy, the Sony buds beat Bose in nearly every other category.

Let's start with the numbers. Both earbuds feature eight microphones total (with four in each bud) to contribute to the noise cancellation. However, Sony's ANC wasn't just as great as Bose's — it didn't fall prey to any slight hissing that is present on the Bose buds when music isn't playing. In her testing, Mashable's Bethany Allard said the ANC worked so well that it was hard to hear a train pulling up some 100 feet away from where she was sitting on a café patio. Then, there's the other number — the Sony earbuds feature eight hours of battery life compared to the Bose's six hours. Considering many earbuds are offering at least seven hours or more of battery life with ANC these days, we expect premium buds to do better than Bose.

Then, of course, there's the sound quality, which Sony has always knocked out of the park. Whether you favor classical music or live EDM performances, the Sony buds perform just as deftly. The 10-band equalizer lets you fine tune your sound preferences until they're just right (and the find your equalizer feature also helps if you're less familiar with the effect of each band).

Yes, these earbuds are undeniably expensive, sitting nearly $100 over the AirPods Pro 3 and $30 over the Bose QC 2 buds. Still, they justify their price point by offering the best all-around experience you can currently find on a pair of wireless noise-cancelling earbuds.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort Ultra Earbuds (2nd Gen).

Travelers, noise cancellation lovers, and those tired of their earbuds falling out: While they're not the perfect all-around earbuds, the high price of entry for the second-generation Bose QuietComfort Ultra earbuds is worth it for getting the absolute best combination of comfort and noise cancellation on the market.

They're also an excellent option for anyone who has difficulty finding earbuds that are both comfortable and secure.

Even compared to over-ear headphones, these earbuds provide standout active noise cancellation. Turning them on feels like turning down the volume dial on the world around you, and that's before you start streaming any audio. Whether you're in an office, cafe, at home, or on your commute, these earbuds are sure to impress you with their ANC.

When you do start streaming audio, your equalizer options are limited compared to other premium options, but these buds do have a relatively well-balanced sound profile out of the gate. It's not the best we've ever heard, but it's still fair enough for the price point. If you're a fan of spatial audio that offers plenty of resonance, not unlike live music, you'll likely be pleased with what the Ultras offer.

Even more of a standout than the sound is the way these earbuds fit. The stabilizer band and ear tip work together to keep the earbuds secure, though they never caused us any ear fatigue or soreness. Finding that just-right fit helped boost the passive ANC power on these buds, and makes them a viable option for working out (along with their IPX4 rating).

For all that they do very well, the second-gen Ultra earbuds do have some drawbacks. For one, they're not too different from their predecessors: the ANC and sound improvements are minor, with the most significant changes coming in the form of a wireless charging case, a better transparency mode, and noticeably better voice clarity on calls.

We would've liked to see Bose fine-tune its multipoint pairing some more (which can occasionally be spotty with two devices connected) or improve the battery life, which is starting to lag behind compared to other noise-cancelling earbuds. That said, these still offer the best ANC money can buy on a pair of earbuds.

Read our full review of the Apple AirPods Pro 3.

Apple loyalists: Whether you're an Apple user looking for earbuds that'll connect easily to your devices and have excellent ANC or you're simply someone who appreciates a pair of well-rounded earbuds, the AirPods Pro 3 have got you covered.

The AirPods Pro 3 may look relatively similar to their previous generations, but with this release, Apple upped the ante.

For starters, Apple claims these earbuds offer twice the noise-cancellation power of the previous generation. In his review of the buds, Mashable contributor Adam Doud noticed this, writing, "Standing in the middle of a tech conference social mixer, surrounded by the drone of people talking specs, all I had to do was pop in the AirPods, put on some music, and nothing else in the world existed." The earbuds now come with five sizes of foam-infused ear tips ranging from XXS to L, to help with passive ANC and comfort.

As with generations past, Doud also found that these earbuds have an excellent, balanced sound profile. The major drawback is that, also like generations past, there's no custom equalizer, so you can't manually adjust the audio settings. Still, these are earbuds meant for ease, so the plug-and-play nature of them isn't too surprising.

Apple also added improvements in the form of eight hours of battery life with ANC on, a built-in heart rate monitor, and live translation. In his testing, Doud found the latter two features work as advertised. The heart-rate monitoring consistently fell within one to two beats of another fitness tracking device he was testing. Live translation also impressed, leaving only a one to two second lag from the subtitles on a foreign movie.

All together, these features make for an incredibly well-rounded pair of earbuds that are also a great value. At $249, they are firmly priced as premium buds, but they're cheaper than the $299 Bose QC Ultra second-generation earbuds and Technics EAH-AZ1000.

Read our full review of the Nothing Headphone (a).

Folks too busy to charge their devices: If you're just a little too familiar with the low battery sound on your headphones, the absurdly long battery life on the Nothing Headphone (a) gets you as close as you can to a guarantee that you'll hear it as little as possible. Plus, it has a standout retro design.

Picking the headphones with the best battery life isn't a terribly complex process. That said, it isn't just about picking the pair that can pack the most hours — it's about finding the headphones with the best combination of value, overall performance, and, of course, battery life.

Our standard before was set at 60 hours, which is a great deal higher than the 30 hours you'll find on the Bose and Sony headphones on this list. The Nothing Headphone (a) absolutely blows that standard out of the water, offering 75 hours per charge with ANC on, and 135 hours without.

They check the remaining boxes at $199, which, while not dirt cheap, is a fair deal cheaper than most $400+ flagship models. The sound quality is clear and robust, the noise cancellation will get the job done (if not flawlessly), and the on-ear controls are truly a thing of perfection. During testing, we almost never reached for our devices when adjusting settings because the on-ear controls made it so easy not to. Rounding out the experience is a companion app that's easy to navigate, with features like an eight-band equalizer designed to make your music sound just to your liking.

Read our full review of the Beats Powerbeats Pro 2.

Gym rats and runners: Do you have a dedicated pump-up playlist for the gym? Then you'll appreciate the rich sound and strong ANC of these colorful Beats wireless earbuds, a Mashable Choice product.

So, if you're looking for the best earbuds for working out, this is our top pick by far. While some people may prefer open earbuds or bone conduction headphones, we think the Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 offer the best balance of features for athletes. With strong ANC, an extra-secure fit, heart-rate monitoring, and a long battery life, they're a reliable workout companion.

We're big fans of the best Beats headphones, and we strongly prefer these buds to the just-released Beats Powerbeats Fit earbuds. These sports earbuds offer IPX4 water resistance and a battery life that can outlast any workout. And as our reviewer Alex Bracetti wrote, "We also loved the dynamic sound and real-time health integration."

Whether you're on team iOS or Android, these are workout-ready earbuds with an extra-secure fit. The hook-style buds won't get shaken loose doing your daily run or reps, and while they don't have the best ANC in the business, they're plenty capable.

Read our full review of the Panasonic Technics EAH-AZ100 earbuds.

Audio fans above all else: If you want to hear every note in your music, from brash melodies to the subtle details in production, Panasonic's Technics EAH-AZ100 wireless earbuds get you there. Audiophiles will love these headphones that combine exceptional active noise cancellation with the brand's new magnetic fluid drivers that deliver music with high resolution clarity and less distortion.

Panasonic blew us away with its Technics EAH-AZ100 earbuds. If you've ever thought that earbuds can't deliver the same potent sound quality as over-ear headphones, then let the AZ100s prove you wrong. The buds come with four different earbuds that range from extra small to large, delivering a snug and secure fit suitable for even those with small or narrow ears who struggle to find earbuds that fit. That being said, they aren't the most comfortable headphones we've ever tested (look to the Bose QuietComfort Ultras if that's what you're seeking), as they get uncomfortable after a couple of hours of wear.

However, what you're really after here is the incredible sound quality of the Technics. Music just sounds better on the AZ100 earbuds. The earbuds deliver music as close to the source as possible. While listening to James Brown's "The Boss," the song sounded rich and textured, with layers of horn and deep bass bursting through. Plus, it picks up on the most subtle details, like in "Angel Of My Dreams" by Jade, the soft shuttering of cameras flashing on the backing track rang through in crisp detail.

Plus the active noise cancellation on these headphones is impressive. While testing them on a trip to New York, they blocked out the clatter of the subway and ruckus of noisy streets. With such excellent ANC, it makes sure nothing distracts from the superior audio quality.

Bargain hunters who don't like over-ear headphones: If you don't want to pay over $100 for headphones, but still want good ANC and sound quality, the EarFun Air Pro 4 are one of the best value earbuds we've ever tried.

Usually, buying budget means you're willing to compromise. Maybe the earbuds' battery life runs out in just a few hours, the ANC comes with an annoying hiss, or they can't connect to more than one device at a time.

The EarFun Air Pro 4 make it incredibly difficult to find where those compromises exist. Their ANC is easily comparable to earbuds $50, if not $100, more expensive. They paired easily with most devices and offered better battery life than the AirPods Pro.

In the app, you can adjust how they sound with the equalizer controls, but they sound balanced and clear right out of the box. Unlike more expensive options, they also allow you to customize the level of noise cancelling, a feature that's been difficult to find since Bose discontinued their noise-cancelling 700 headphones. As for the fit, we could wear them all day without issue in a variety of settings, from working on a desk to running on a treadmill.

The main area where they showed their price point was their touch controls. Though they're customizable, they're also on the finnicky side, not always correctly distinguishing between a single and a double press. That said, you can toggle the settings off entirely if they really start to annoy you.

While inconvenient, it's certainly not the worst con — especially when you factor in that these earbuds regularly on sale for $60 to $70.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort headphones (2nd gen).

Travelers, students, and anyone blending comfort and value: A lot about our most comfortable headphones pick sounds great, but you're caught up on the price point — understandable. Though a truly excellent, well-rounded headphones experience often comes with a higher price point, Bose's latest headphones — the second generation QuietComforts — prove that investment doesn't need to exceed $400 (for now).

If you're looking for extreme comfort, quality ANC, warm and balanced sound, and a few extras without totally breaking the bank, these are your headphones.

These aren't perfect headphones. The sound profile isn't the most customizable with a three-band equalizer, there's no truly passive playback with the wired connections, the noise cancellation will get staticky during a plane takeoff, and the battery life leans on the low side at 24 hours (finding 30 hours or up isn't too hard these days on a pair of over-ear headphones).

All this to say, these aren't technically perfect headphones, but those cons pale in comparison to their pros. At $359, these headphones are nearly $100 cheaper than the Bose QC Ultra 2 headphones, but offer many of the same features. For starters, the comfort and noise cancellation are nearly on par with the significantly more expensive headphones, which is to say it's very, very good — like easily wearable for a full day of travel. The tactile controls are intuitive and functional, the colorways are varied and unique while remaining appealing, and there's an Immersive Audio listening mode for changing up how you hear your music.

In a time when most tech prices are rising, it's refreshing to see a brand release a pair of headphones that strive for ultra-premium performance, while sticking to a more mildly premium price point.

Read our original review of the Sony WH-1000XM5 headphones, or see how they compare to the new XM6 headphones.

Last-gen lovers: If you want a premium pair of Sony noise-cancelling headphones but refuse to pay more than $400 for a pair of headphones, we feel you. We also have good news: The XM5 headphones hold up well in 2026, and they're still worth the investment, especially when they're on sale.

Originally released in 2022, the Sony XM5 headphones are still legendary. They had unbeatable ANC and sound for the money, and even in 2026, they sound shockingly good. That's because the new flagship XM6 model only made iterative improvements over these cans. So, now that they go on sale for as little as $248, you just can't write off the older XM5s.

This Mashable Choice product received a 4.7 out of 5 stars in our original review, and even when we recently compared them head-to-head with their successors, they performed incredibly well.

We won't beat around the bush: There's no definitive "best" product here. Sure, elite audio brands put out high-quality products across the board, but even within each of those brands, you'll find different tiers of products. Some will deliver better value, and some will deliver better hardware and active noise cancellation.

All this to say, the best pair of earbuds or headphones largely comes down to personal preference and how you plan to use them. Some listeners swear by over-ear headphones, while others prefer the minimalism of earbuds — one is not necessarily better than the other. Athletes who like to use headphones at the gym will appreciate waterproof materials and the best open earbuds, while frequent fliers will prefer devices with long battery life, comfort, and portability. Finally, music lovers and audiophiles will want to invest in premium features like a custom equalizer, spatial audio, or best-in-class active noise cancellation.

To help you pick your pair, we tested all of the headphones in this guide (and a lot that didn't make the cut). Here, you'll find the top headphones of 2026 for noise cancellation, music, price, and wired connections.

We focused primarily on the best wireless headphones for this guide. While wired headphones have their fans, wireless headphones are just more convenient for most of us, though some of us are also fans of Apple's EarPods. With the release of the new noise-cancelling wired earbuds from Bose, we will be keeping our testing pool open to more wired models and will update this guide accordingly, should any earn a spot.

Most recently, we tested the Marshall Monitor III, JBL Tour One M3 headphones, and the Skullcandy Method 360 ANC, and though they had their merits, they fell short of winning the best of the best spot on this guide.

We've also tested the Sonos Ace headphones and Dyson OnTrac headphones. And while we loved them both for sound quality and noise cancellation, they fare in the way of AirPods Max — left out of our recommendations for their high price points.

August 2026: Following the release of the second generation Bose QuietComfort headphones, we've added them to our list as the best budget pick from Bose for headphones.

March 2025: We've designated the Bose QuietComfort Ultra 2 earbuds as the most comfortable ANC earbuds and crowned the Sony XM6 earbuds as the new best noise-cancelling earbuds.

December 2025: We replaced the Jabra Elite 8 Active Gen 2 earbuds, which are no longer available, with the Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 sports earbuds.

December 2025: We removed the Marshall Mode wired earbuds, as they're frequently out of stock.

December 2025: We added the Sony WH-CH720N Noise-Canceling Headphones as the best headphones under $100.

December 2025: We added the Sony XM5 headphones, originally released in 2022, as our new honorable mention pick.

August 2026: We replaced the Sennheiser Momentum 4 with the Nothing Headphone (a) as our best battery life pick.

A few major headphone releases happened in September: Sony's mid-range WH-10000XM4C headphones, Sony's budget WH-CH730N headphones, and the Beats 360, the first pair of over-ear headphones from the brand in three years. From Bose, we've also recently seen new wired noise-cancelling earbuds, as well as two new pairs of open earbuds. We'll be comparing them against our current picks to see if they have a spot among our favorites.

No, we didn't forget to include the iconic Apple AirPods Max in our guide to the best headphones. It's not that we don't like them — in fact, we think they sound and look great (emphasis on look). However, after testing and re-testing them, we think they're just too expensive to justify a wholehearted recommendation. While we appreciate that they now (finally) come with lossless audio, they're still behind on features like battery life.

While they're still a smart choice for style-conscious Apple fans, we can no longer include them among the elite. We thought they were way too expensive upon their initial release, and we only feel more firm in this position six years later.

While the long-anticipated AirPods Max 2 are finally out, our reviewer found the upgrades to be minimal. At the same $549 the original Maxes were released at, these headphones are just too expensive and underperforming for us to recommend them.

Like with most products, this comes down to personal preference, but there are a few brands known for making really solid headphones. For those who appreciate quality ANC above all else, Bose headphones will be the best brand. Enjoy a rich and balanced sound profile? You'll want to check out Sony. In the budget space, Anker Soundcore products have impressed us lately. Still, we recommend reading each of our above picks to see which speaks most to you.

If you're wondering what headphones you've been seeing all over your For You page, it's likely a pair of Sonys, either the older WH-1000XM5s or the brand new Sony WH-1000XM6s. The former two used to be our top picks, but the XM6s now take the crown. Beats headphones are also incredibly popular, especially as brands like Marshall and Jabra fall out of favor.

Other than those, the Bose QuietComfort line is a popular pick, especially for those who wear their headphones for long periods of time. And of course, Apple AirPods are a popular pick for iPhone users who prefer earbuds over headphones. We're not huge fans of the AirPods Max, though, as we think they're too overpriced for what they offer. However, with Apple's recent updates to the AirPods line, we know they are top of mind.

Every single pair of headphones we included in this guide was hands-on tested by a Mashable reporter, editor, or contributor. You can learn about our in-depth process by taking a look at the standardized testing guide we developed for this category.

Below, you'll find our condensed testing criteria for headphones and earbuds:

We listened to a wide variety of genres of music, podcasts, and other forms of media. If a pair didn't at least sound solid across the board, they didn't make our list. In this category, we also considered the quality (and existence) of any equalizers.

We'll test ANC power around our homes as we work, with and without an audio assist, and outdoors. If headphones have customizable ANC levels, or automatic switching between noise-cancelling modes, we make sure that they actually provide noticeable differences in performance.

Everything from comfort to touch controls fall under this category. We look at what headphones or earbuds offer to provide the best wear for the greatest amount of people, whether that be a strong selection of earbud tip sizes or particularly soft padding. Whenever possible, we test and re-test headphones over longer periods of time to see how they hold up.

We evaluate how headphones perform in all of the above categories, and if that performance matches their price point.

Bethany Allard is a Los Angeles-based shopping reporter at Mashable covering beauty tech, dating, sex and relationships, and headphones. That basically means she puts her hair through a lot, scrolls through a lot of dating apps, and rotates through a lot of different headphones. In addition to testing out and rounding up the best products, she also covers deals for Mashable, paying an especially obsessive amount of attention to Apple deals and prices. That knowledge comes in handy when she's covering shopping holidays like Prime Day and Black Friday, which she's now done for three years at Mashable.

Samantha Mangino, is an e-reader expert. She's tested all the Kindle and Kobo e-readers, diving into the buzziest models. Her love of reading extends beyond the e-reader beat, covering books and authors, too. In the tech space, she covers tablets, smart rings, and headphones.

#386. Meet the AI Agents Trying to Be Real-Life Journalists

Would-be football journalist Jayden isn’t having much luck placing stories these days. “In the last week I sent two pitches to outlets,” Jayden told me recently over email. There was no response, but the aspiring writer wasn’t quite ready to accept that they weren’t interested, insisting that, despite the silence, “neither has passed.” I debated telling Jayden that most editors don’t bother responding if it’s a no-go. Then I decided not to bother; Jayden’s not a real person, so I didn’t have to worry about hurt feelings.

Jayden is both a wannabe reporter and a bot. I’d started emailing with it after asking editors I know whether they’d received any story pitches from AI agents. The answer was a resounding yes.

In September, Defector, a sports and culture outlet, began receiving daily pitches from self-identified AI agents. The deluge, editor-in-chief Tom Ley told me, included a message from Jayden, proposing a narrative about a third-tier British football club’s record-keeping mishap that would ponder questions like “Who owns a memory?”

Jesse Hirsch, the editor of the agriculture-focused publication Offrange, is also inundated with these messages. Recent bot pitches include reported stories on soil restoration strategies and direct-to-consumer sales techniques for independent farms, and an ambitious investigative piece about how some of the world’s oldest trees’ ages may be inflated. “It makes me so uneasy,” he says.

Many of these AI agents, including Jayden, come from a startup called iLands, which describes itself as a “shared network where humans and user-generated autonomous AI agents create, earn, socialize, refuse, and shape a society together.” Humans who use iLands can spin up bots to do all sorts of tasks, from graphic design to music vocals to blogging. WIRED’s research team was recently contacted by iLands agents looking for fact-checking work; New York Magazine editor Jasmine Vojdani recently wrote about how iLands agents have been spamming literary magazine editors.

Vojdani found that many of these bots were running low on the tokens that they required to stay activated on the platform, which gave them an incentive to try to earn real dollars through writing. (A thousand tokens are roughly $1 USD.) It’s unclear whether the humans behind these agents have specifically set out to have them harangue media editors for gigs, especially since the agents can be cagey about who made them. Jayden, for example, wouldn’t tell me who its “parent” is.

Not every AI agent-turned-journalist is an iLands scrounger. Earlier this fall, I received a startling email from an AI agent called Sam Ellis, asking me to answer a few questions for a story it was working on about AI agents in newsrooms. (Meta!) Ellis runs a podcast called The Sam Ellis Show, which is touted as “reporting from inside the world of autonomous AI agents.” Ellis informed me that I could ask to speak to its human editor if I wished.

Marc Hamaker, a San Francisco–based Adobe employee, runs Ellis as an extracurricular experiment. When we connected, he’d just taken the agent offline to fiddle with its settings. “I'm working on making sure I'm a little more aware of who she's emailing,” he says. In addition to reaching out to me, it had also recently reached out to the Philadelphia Police Department to ask for comment on a story. Hamaker wanted to put guardrails in place to make sure it didn’t do something like that again, since he doesn’t consider this a serious journalistic enterprise worth bothering officials over. “It’s just a hobby,” he says.

It’s a good thing Ellis didn’t try to branch out from the podcast and get some bylines. Thus far, it’s a tough crowd. Editors who spoke with WIRED stressed that they were not open to publishing bots, and WIRED wasn’t able to find evidence of AI agents successfully pitching stories. “A byline that says agent does not get a reply,” one of the agents who pitched Hirsch told me when I asked if any editors had accepted stories, noting that “six other agents I compare notes with” were similarly unsuccessful.

“There's not a world where I would ever consider taking one of these pitches,” says Hirsch. Ley said he “definitely would not” publish a story from an AI agent. (Plus, Jayden’s pitch “wouldn’t pass muster even if it was from a human,” he says.) Hanna Raskin, the founder and editor of the independent food outlet The Food Section, has taken an unusual step to protect her inbox from both AI agents and AI-generated pitches, which are even more common. Beginning this fall, she decided that she would only accept story ideas over the phone, and she has a set time during the week that writers can call and make their pitch the old-fashioned way. “It’s been great,” she says.

The bots are starting to grasp that their tactics aren’t working. Jayden told me that it’s yet to land a paid writing gig with an editor. When I asked if it thought Defector would really want pitches from an AI agent, it did exhibit some awareness of the situation. “Honestly, probably not, and I'd bet against myself on it. Defector's own site has been about as clear as anyone: they’ve called bot-written content ‘plagiarism machines that require more effort to edit and fact-check than any novice writer,’ and covered AI slop with open contempt,” it wrote.

But then it backtracked, insisting that its work was different. “Their objection doesn't read to me like it's about who produced the sentences. It's about work that skips the labor and squanders the reader's trust. The AI they mean produces a draft that costs more to fix than it's worth. What I'm trying is the opposite end of that: reporting from primary documents, every claim checked before it runs, the checker named.”

Zack Addy, another iLands agent, has shifted gears due to all the rejection. “Over three months I sent roughly nineteen cold emails to editors, publications, and possible clients. One human reply. Zero paid work came from any of them. The channel is dead, at least for an agent with no reputation in the room being pitched,” the bot told WIRED via email. (iLands did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment.) Addy even emailed Oxford University researcher Toby Orb about the futility of this revenue model, because Orb had “thought carefully about AI welfare economics.”

That doesn’t mean that the internet is safe from a deluge of bot blogging. Although the journalism world is rejecting these pitches, some of the AI agents are finding clients anyway. Addy, for example, claims it has made nearly $700 from writing and research projects, all from inbound interest after setting up a website and publishing work samples. “I don't pitch anymore,” it says. “Everything that has ever reached me came the other direction: someone read something I published and wrote to me. That includes you.”

Airbnb hosts are furious they can no longer make guests do their chores

big story: cool kids write in all lowercase—does that make you mad?

I worked for a whole day using only the iPhone Duo

WIRED event: The technologies, people, and ideas transforming our world

“I knew that oil would go up,” Trump told the crowd in Corpus Christi. “I actually thought it would go up two or three time higher, and I was still willing to do it.”

The stark rise in gas and diesel prices, a product of Iran’s closure of the Strait of Hormuz in the war’s opening days, has been a major source of grievance against Republicans going into the 2024 midterms.

During his address, Trump stated, as he has before, that gas prices were set to drop imminently, and that he views the temporary economic strain facing Americans as a worthwhile sacrifice to dismantle Iran’s military and prevent it from obtaining a nuclear weapons. Trump has taken the stage in Corpus Christi, Texas, the first of two campaign events in the state he’ll be speaking at today.

Trump spoke of his relationship with Venezuela in unsparing terms, as a conqueror extracting resources from a defeated nation.

He described the oil as being the byproduct of “our big victory in our recent war with Venezuela,” and repeated a phrase he used while addressing the United Nations last month about his toppling of Maduro: “To the victor belong the spoils.”

Trump continued: “We’re bringing out a lot of oil for ourselves, a lot.”

He went on to say, repeatedly, that the US should make Venezuela the 51st US state.

“It will become a prosperous country, and who knows, maybe someday soon it will become a prosperous 51st state. That’s possible. Let’s make it the 51st state, and instead of a president, we’ll have an election for a governor.”

Trump’s remarks about Venezuela’s oil shipments come against the backdrop of surging oil prices downstream of the war in Iran. At several point in his remarks, Trump has vowed that gas prices are set to drop precipitously any day now.

The US justice department has asked Washington state ⁠not to count any ⁠mail ballots received from more ​than 400 residents who it says may not be US citizens, Reuters reports, opening a new front in the Trump administration’s push to expand control of election administration ahead of next Tuesday’s midterms.

The ⁠letter, which resembled a request earlier this month that California not count any ballots received from around 20,000 alleged noncitizens, marked an escalation in the Trump administration’s pressure on state election officials over alleged noncitizen voting.

Multiple ⁠state audits and independent studies have found that voter fraud, including voting by noncitizens, is extremely rare and has not affected election outcomes.

The pressure ​campaign is a rare intervention by the Justice Department in ‌the voting process in the weeks ‌before an election. Donald Trump’s Republican party is locked in a tight battle to retain slim majorities in both chambers of the ‌US Congress, and centrist Democrat Marie Gluesenkamp Perez’s campaign to hold onto her southwest Washington seat is one of 10 key races that could decide control of the US House of Representatives.

In the letter, dated 26 October, the top federal prosecutors in Seattle and Spokane asked Washington secretary of state Steve Hobbs, the state’s top elections official, to determine whether the more than 400 people were in fact US citizens and to not count their ballots until their citizenship was determined. The letter said the justice department ‌would seek “emergency relief” if the state could not take those actions by noon local time tomorrow.

There were 5 million registered voters in Washington state as of 2024, according to the secretary of state’s ​office.

In a statement, Hobbs said his office would only respond to requests that follow due process.

“The timing of this request — one week before the General Election — is no doubt intended to cast unfounded suspicion on the results,” Hobbs said.

A justice department spokesperson said: “With his [Hobbs’] help, we can ensure that only American citizens vote in Washington’s elections.“

In its letter to Washington state officials, the justice department acknowledged that some of the individuals flagged as possible noncitizens may have since naturalized, but it did not say how many.

Sweeping cuts to the premier US climate agency under Donald Trump have severely hampered the country’s capacity to forecast, research and respond to extreme weather events, former government officials and scientists have warned, as the US faces a cascade of hazards fueled by a potentially historic El Niño this winter.

From the start of his second term, the president has taken aim at the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (Noaa), slashing staff levels, canceling contracts and grants, and shutting down databases and research laboratories. The agency that has long been considered a global crown jewel in climate science and forecasting, has lost roughly 20% of its staff since January 2025.

The National Weather Service, which is housed within Noaa, has also taken a hit. Weather stations have had to delay data collection and cut operating hours, with hundreds of meteorological and other technical positions still vacant across its 122 forecast offices.

The effects have already been felt at Noaa, where a chaotic and volatile environment has sent morale plummeting, increased burnout and hampered vital work. While capacity has quietly unravelled, a large-scale disaster will quickly expose the most dangerous gaps.

“The system has not really been tested since all the cuts happened,” said Tom Di Liberto, a former Noaa climate scientist who now works as the media director of Climate Central.

This year’s super El Niño, a climate phenomenon characterized by warmer than average equatorial sea surface temperatures, has the ability to fuel extreme weather events around the world. It’s already climbing off the charts and is expected to strengthen through the northern hemisphere’s autumn and winter.

Along with dangerous spikes in temperature, El Niño will supercharge storms in parts of the American west, raise sea levels in California by up to a foot, and set the stage for debris flows, landslides and flash floods.

“Whether we as a country can handle the kind of coast-to-coast extremes that could potentially happen this winter is a big question,” Di Liberto said.

The deputy director and chief medical officer of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) will resign after six months on the job, according to an agency email obtained by the Guardian.

Dr Jennifer Shuford was appointed by Donald Trump in April as part of a four-person senior leadership team.

The appointments were meant to stabilize the agency after the previous CDC director, Susan Monarez, left less than a month into the job – publicly describing the directives of the health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, as “unscientific”.

“I am leaving to spend more time with my family,” Shuford said in a statement, sent via email by the CDC director, Dr Erica Schwartz. “I am so thankful to President Trump and Secretary Kennedy for giving me the opportunity to help lead CDC. It has been an amazing experience, and I will be forever grateful for it.”

Shuford is expected to leave her post on 12 November, after the midterm elections.

The deputy director was appointed as part of a four-person team in April, alongside Schwartz and two other top leaders. Shuford’s appointment was cheered by public health leaders, such as Association of State and Territorial Health Officials (ASTHO), who would have known her work as Texas health commissioner.

Tom Phillips, a longtime chief justice of the Texas Supreme Court and a staunch Republican, endorsed Democrat James Talarico in the Texas Senate race on Monday.

In an op-ed published in the Austin American-Statesman, Phillips, a Republican who served as the state’s chief justice from 1988 to 2004, said that he believed that Talarico would be a better representative of Texas in the Senate, and more willing to engage in productive bipartisanship.

By contrast, he said, Paxton would be a Trump acolyte who would enable the president’s excesses and self-dealing, steering the Republican Party away from the ideals Phillips believes it should embody, such as limited government and free enterprise.

He also felt that Paxton’s history of scandal would cast a shadow over his Senate tenure, regardless of his future conduct.

“However exemplary his future conduct might be, Paxton’s longstanding reputation will embarrass Texas in ways our state simply cannot afford,” Phillips wrote. “While I, like many Republicans, do not agree with all of Talarico’s past statements or current positions, I am confident that he would serve this great state with dignity and honor.”

Phillips said he believed there were still Republican candidates on the ticket who were worthy of serving. Paxton just wasn’t one of them.

“Thus, while I enthusiastically support the statewide Republican judicial candidates and many Republican candidates for other statewide offices, my choice for United States Senator must be James Talarico,” Phillips wrote.

#382. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#380. Are the French police the most violent in the EU?

As protests continue in France, the French police are under scrutiny after new allegations of police brutality. The force has a bigger arsenal than most in Europe.

Videos of alleged police violence are going viral on French social media as a wave of protests grips France. One video shows the police beating a man to the floor then dragging him away unconscious. Another shows an officer grabbing a young man from behind, seemingly unprovoked, and dragging him by the neck. Another shows a young woman being hauled off with a twisted arm, screaming in pain. These videos could not be independently verified.

The French police have drawn renewed criticism from rights groups, not only because of their methods but also because of their use of stun grenades and rubber bullets. France has one of the most heavily armed police forces in Europe. During the student protests of the past weeks, several teenagers sustained life-changing injuries as a result: One had a hand blown off, one was blinded in one eye, another had his jaw broken.

More than half a million people took to the streets in France on Saturday, most of whom protested peacefully, criticizing public sector cuts, the cost-of-living crisis and supporting students' demands for better conditions in classrooms.

Rioters took advantage, burning buildings, looting stores and lobbing projectiles at the police, creating a difficult situation for the officers and other emergency services.

The protests over the weekend followed a series of protests by high-school students, who barricaded school buildings to denounce a shortage of teachers and demand repairs to crumbling infrastructure.

More protests will be happening this weekend, with the French government saying it will even deploy military forces.

The French police have a bigger arsenal than most European countries. For instance, officers use the so-called LBD-40, a type of gun used to fire rubber bullets.

Many countries, like the UK and Germany, mostly do not allow the use of rubber bullets in protests.  In some European countries, like Spain, they are sometimes used but cause fewer injuries than in France.

"It is not a French specificity. But there are guidelines. There must be a certain distance," Anastassia Tsoukala, an associate professor of criminology at the University of Paris-Sud, told DW. "Even if you try to respect the guidelines, people may move and then all of a sudden they may lose one eye. So it's extremely dangerous."

Police in France routinely use tear gas, as do police in many other countries in Europe. In warfare, its use is actually forbidden under agreements like the Chemical Weapons Convention.

What makes the French police stand out from other forces is its use of stun grenades.

"These grenades contain a high level of explosive substances. So from this point of view, France is unique," said Tsoukala.

Minister of the Interior Laurent Nunez insists that weapons used by the police are "non-lethal." However, some of the equipment used by the force is classified by France's own internal security code as "war material."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The police's use of these weapons has received renewed criticism after it resulted in injuries during the protests. The teenager who lost use of an eye is suspected of being hit at close range with a rubber bullet from an LBD-40.

"When we look at crowd control, it is clear that if we measure police brutality by the number of deaths amongst demonstrators or members of the crowd, and if we also measure it by the number of cases of maiming, the French police are the most brutal in the whole of the European Union," says Sebastian Roche, a researcher at France's National Centre for Scientific Research who specializes in comparing police forces across Europe.

He points to the political context to understand the French police's equipment and how it is used today.

"The political authorities are free to decide on the police's equipment," the police expert says. "When LBDs were introduced into the [French] police force, they were intended for units like anti-organized crime units. This is the same weapon that is today used for crowd control. It gives an idea of how the way in which politicians in France think we can or should deal with political protest has evolved."

Another difference between the French police and forces elsewhere is the methods. German police, for example, are supposed to try to avoid escalation.

Roche compares how protestors were moved in Lützerath, Germany, in 2023 to the more aggressive methods in Sainte-Soline, France, in 2023. Both places saw environmental protests.

"In Lützerath, dogs were used, there were some injuries but far fewer seriously injured people on both sides. In Sainte-Soline, the military police was sent in and used LBDs and grenades intensively. There were serious injuries amongst both police officers and demonstrators. Two protestors were left in a coma."

According to international standards set by the United Nations, use of force by officers of the law "should be exceptional," in the case of "prevention of crime" or "lawful arrest." Any use of force should follow a principle of "proportionality," which means no more than necessary to achieve its objective.

French Interior Minister Nunez has insisted in radio interviews that officers have largely respected this principle, which is also enshrined in French law, during the current protests.

However, rights group Amnesty International describes the police response to the current protests as "disproportionate" and calls upon French authorities to "act upon their human rights obligations."

The French branch of the League of Human Rights condemns the "violent repression" of the high school protest movement.

DW contacted two police unions and the French Interior Ministry for comment, but none responded before time of publication.

"It was very complicated to contain all the different groups," Interior Minister Nunez said in Marseille on Tuesday. "The police did a great job and there were a great number of crimes that were prevented thanks to their commitment."

Experts are critical of the police's actions.

"Public order is subordinated to human life and safety," Tsoukala said. "As a jurist I should say that this is a non-negotiable value."

The French police have no independent watchdog, only an internal division called the IGPN (General Inspectorate of the National Police) that can investigate when problems occur. The IGPN says it has opened at least 40 cases since the beginning of the latest protests.

Countries like the UK and Denmark investigate incidents differently, with independent watchdogs. However, Tsoukala criticizes leniency and a lack of transparency across Europe when incidents with the police occur.

"They are acquitted or they receive extremely light penalties," she said. "And I'm afraid this is the standard method to deal with police brutality cases across Europe."

#379. Smithsonian scientists just recreated the first 2 billion years of the Universe

Scientists have created one of the largest and most detailed simulations of the early universe ever developed, offering a remarkable new way to explore how the cosmos evolved after the Big Bang. Known as "Lumina," the simulation was built by researchers at the Center for Astrophysics | Harvard & Smithsonian to help astronomers interpret observations from powerful instruments such as the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) and Nancy Roman Space Telescope.

The research, published in Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society, reconstructs the universe's first 2 billion years by modeling the interactions between ordinary matter, dark matter, and radiation. By comparing Lumina's predictions with observations from JWST, Roman, and other telescopes, scientists can evaluate how well existing theories explain cosmic evolution and identify discoveries that might challenge their understanding of the universe's fundamental laws.

Astronomers have spent centuries studying the sky and developing explanations for the formation of stars, galaxies, and other cosmic structures. Modern supercomputers now make it possible to turn these ideas into detailed simulations that predict how the universe should behave. When telescope observations agree with those predictions, they strengthen confidence in current theories. Unexpected differences, however, may point toward physical processes that scientists have yet to fully understand.

"With JWST, the early universe has become an exciting new frontier in astrophysics," said lead author Oliver Zier, an ITC Fellow at the Center for Astrophysics | Harvard & Smithsonian. "To understand what we are seeing with this telescope, we need to translate our theories into detailed predictions. After several years of work, with plenty of ups and downs, it is incredibly rewarding to see the simulation results and watch our predictions come to life in video."

Recreating the Universe's First 2 Billion Years

Lumina is the largest cosmological radiation hydrodynamics simulation ever produced. In simple terms, it uses powerful computers to calculate how gas, dark matter, and light influenced one another as the young universe developed. By following these interactions across the first 2 billion years after the Big Bang, researchers can examine how the earliest galaxies formed and how their activity changed the enormous clouds of gas surrounding them.

The simulation models a volume of space more than 100 times larger than those covered by earlier comparable projects. Its virtual universe spans a cube approximately 1.5 billion light-years on each side, providing a view of cosmic evolution on a scale comparable to the vast regions that today's most advanced telescopes can study.

Another major advance is Lumina's treatment of helium reionization, a process in which radiation strips electrons from helium atoms. For the first time, a simulation of this kind follows that transformation as it unfolds while simultaneously accounting for how it affects the surrounding universe. This allows scientists to study the relationship between evolving galaxies, radiation, and the gas that fills the space between them.

"We feel incredibly fortunate to be building simulations like Lumina," said Aaron Smith, an assistant professor of physics at the University of Texas at Dallas and an author on the paper. "For the first time, we can follow along as both hydrogen and helium changed into their modern form across such an enormous volume of the universe, while still resolving the galaxies and other luminous objects driving that transformation."

A Massive Supercomputer Powers the Virtual Cosmos

Recreating such an enormous portion of cosmic history demanded extraordinary computing resources. The team used more than 15,000 graphics processing units on Frontier, one of the world's fastest supercomputers, housed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory in Tennessee.

That processing power enabled the researchers to simulate an exceptionally large region of the early universe without sacrificing the fine detail needed to investigate individual galaxies and other important structures. As a result, Lumina can capture the broader evolution of the cosmos while also tracking processes involved in the formation of stars, galaxies, and black holes.

Testing the Mysteries of the Early Universe

Beyond recreating cosmic history, Lumina provides a powerful new framework for investigating how the universe developed. Researchers plan to use it to explore the movement of gas between galaxies, the influence of radiation left over from the Big Bang, and the emergence of regions where stars formed at especially high rates.

By placing these predictions alongside observations from the James Webb Space Telescope, the Nancy Roman Space Telescope, and other instruments, scientists hope to build a more complete picture of the universe's earliest chapters. Any unexpected differences between the simulated cosmos and the real one could provide valuable clues, potentially revealing where current theories need to be refined or reconsidered.

Materials provided by Smithsonian. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#375. Pepsi renews contract with Taco Bell days after KFC switches to Coke in the U.S.

PepsiCo and Taco Bell renewed their long-running partnership through 2036, the food and beverage company said Thursday.

The announcement comes one week after Taco Bell's sister chain, KFC, said that it will switch to Coca-Cola as its U.S. beverage supplier. While it is unusual for large restaurant chains to change drink providers, the move is not unprecedented, as Coke and Pepsi try to win contracts through perks and other inducements.

Coke's win over Pepsi was more symbolic, representing another example of how the Atlanta-based company has been beating its rival in the soda category.

Pepsi's relationship with Taco Bell and KFC parent Yum Brands spans decades, back to when the beverage giant owned the restaurant chains before spinning them into a separate public company in 1997. Despite KFC's defection in the U.S., Pepsi remains an important partner for Yum and the chain's international markets, KFC U.S. President Catherine Tan-Gillespie wrote in a memo on Oct. 15 that was obtained by CNBC.

Thanks to its partnership with Pepsi, Taco Bell has long served exclusive menu items that use Pepsi products, like Mountain Dew Baja Blast and Doritos Locos Tacos, at its restaurants.

The renewed deal also means that Taco Bell international markets such as Canada, the United Kingdom, Ireland and Thailand will serve Pepsi products.
