#374. The best laptops of 2026: Out of the 90+ models weve tried, these are our 9 favorites

The best laptops are fast enough for their primary use cases, well-made, and priced fairly for their specs. Ideally, they also have enough battery power to last a full workday (but often much longer). Whether your preferred daily driver is a sleek Apple MacBook, a versatile Windows laptop, or a simple Chromebook, a reliable PC is a solid investment that feels worth it for years to come.

After testing dozens of different models, we believe these are the top laptops for 2026 buyers.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Mashable has been testing laptops for over 10 years. Over the past two years alone, the team and I have researched dozens of models and tried over 90 of the most promising configurations available. We use a meticulous testing procedure involving real-world stress tests and artificial benchmarks to evaluate our contenders on the basis of performance, battery life, design/build quality, and value.

This regularly updated guide serves as our laptop trophy case, highlighting the best-in-class models we've tried across different subcategories. For even more recommendations, check out my dedicated guides to the best MacBooks, the best Windows laptops, the best cheap laptops, and the best Chromebooks.

Laptop prices have skyrocketed in 2026 for several reasons. One is the recent boom in AI data center construction, which has caused a global shortage of RAM and SSD storage chips (often referred to as "RAMageddon"). As a result, many consumer tech devices that use these components, including the best laptops, have become vastly more expensive than they would've been otherwise. Most PC makers have been affected, including Apple, Dell, Lenovo, and Acer.

The memory crisis is expected to worsen and could last into 2028, driving up prices even further and making good values in the budget and mid-range tiers even scarcer. On top of that, the Trump administration is reportedly mulling new tariffs on semiconductors that would apply to laptops and other electronics.

Further complicating things is that Microsoft reportedly raised its Windows 11 licensing fees by higher-than-usual percentages. In other words, both hardware and software prices have gone up for PC makers this year, forcing them to pass on the extra costs to consumers. Lucky us.

The Mashable team is taking the current state of the market into consideration as we assess and select laptops to recommend to our readers. Moreover, we've gone back and re-evaluated several models that have gotten more expensive since we first tested them.

At the time of writing, only two previous top picks have gotten so pricey that we think they're no longer worth buying. That would be the Lenovo Chromebook Plus 14, which jumped from an already-high $750 to $1,000, and the Acer Chromebook Plus 516, which went from $479 to $649. We're currently testing some other Chromebook options and will update this guide with a new top pick soon.

In a year when laptops became extraordinarily expensive, the MacBook Neo is a breath of fresh air. And for the average person who isn't running AI models locally or messing around with photo or video editing, it's a perfectly capable everyday computer.

Apple fans on a budget: The MacBook Neo is the best choice for folks who want the traditional Apple experience — that is, a premium design and a snappy, polished feel — without traditional Apple prices.

K-12 students and other younger users: The Neo can easily handle most basic tasks and comes in fun color options.

MacBook Air or Pro owners who want a backup device: Mashable's tech editor, Timothy Beck Werth, deemed the Neo the ideal "party laptop." That's the laptop you use for "streaming, music, casual browsing, and retail therapy" once you've clocked out of work for the day, he said.

Read Mashable's full review of the Apple MacBook Neo.

Even after a $100 price bump this summer, the MacBook Neo is still an unrivaled value in the budget category. Most cheap Windows laptops and Chromebooks have plastic components, but this little guy has the same luxe, all-aluminum build quality as any other MacBook. You miss out on extra fixings like keyboard backlighting, a haptic trackpad, and Touch ID (on the base model, at least), but you still get a pretty Liquid Retina display and great speakers. It's a little thicker than a 13-inch MacBook Air, but it weighs the same.

The Neo's A18 Pro processor is technically a pared-down iPhone chip, but don't be fooled: It's extremely impressive for a budget laptop CPU. In simple single-core workloads (web browsing, word processing), the Neo is just as fast as a last-gen M4 MacBook Air. In such scenarios, it feels snappier than most Windows laptops we've tried lately, including 10 that cost $2,000 or more.

The Neo lasted 14 hours and 50 minutes in our battery life benchmark. It lags behind its M-series peers there — our battery life median for MacBooks was 19 hours before it arrived — but it's not bad for its price range. Our median for most other sub-$1,000 Windows laptops and Chromebooks is just 10.5 hours.

The average MacOS user: The M5 MacBook Air inhabits the sweet spot in Apple's lineup. It has an elegant, portable design, versatile performance, and ample future-proofing, but doesn't go overboard on premium features (and thus stays reasonably priced). It's a smart buy for most professionals and college students.

Anyone looking for a great deal: Among mid-range laptops, the MacBook Air is still one of the absolute best overall values on the current market — even after its summer price hike.

Read Mashable's full review of the 15-inch Apple MacBook Air (M5).

The MacBook Air has always been an excellent laptop, but in 2026, it's become my standard bearer, my touchstone — the all-rounder I compare against virtually every other laptop that crosses my desk. Its M5 chip offers unrivaled performance for the price, whether you're using it for basic single-core tasks (like web browsing) or more demanding multi-core workloads (like occasional light video editing). Its build quality is also superb. Its 2.8K Liquid Retina display is bright, sharp, and vibrant; its speakers sound incredible; its Magic Keyboard is satisfyingly snappy; and its smooth haptic touchpad gives it a premium feel. It's actually very rare to see the latter at this price point.

While it's not what I'd call a battery life overachiever — see: some of the Windows laptops on this list — the MacBook Air still lasts well over two workdays per charge. We got almost 18 hours out of it in a video rundown test.

I think most people will appreciate the larger screen on the 15-inch MacBook Air, but if you'd prefer a cheaper, more portable option, there's a 13-inch size, too. They're almost identical; the 15-incher just has two extra speakers and a slightly better GPU in its base model.

The average Windows user: The 2026 Asus Zenbook A14 is the best general-purpose Windows laptop I've tested. It checks every major box — excellent performance, a marathon battery life, an attractive, ultralight design, and a sensible price. (I hold my breath every time I go to re-check its listings. It's one of the rare PCs that hasn't faced a price hike or iffy availability so far this year.)

People who aren't students or hardcore PC gamers: The Zenbook A14's ARM-based processor doesn't have native support for some specialty software and PC games.

Anyone looking for a great MacBook Air alternative: The Zenbook A14 is also a fast and fairly priced ultraportable with a powerful processor, but it has a much longer battery life and more ports than the MacBook Air (plus an OLED display).

Asus updated my beloved Zenbook A14 with Qualcomm's fresh Snapdragon X2 Elite processor for 2026. It's more expensive than the original version from last year, but it's definitely worth the upcharge. It used to lag behind same-gen MacBooks, but it's now one of the only devices that can keep up with both single- and multi-core tasks — so things like basic word processing and light video editing, respectively. In fact, in multi-core scenarios, it's almost 20 percent faster than the M5 MacBook Air. At the time of writing, it was the ninth-fastest laptop in our entire testing database, beating 91 percent of the competition; other stragglers include the latest MacBook Pro and many beefy PCs geared toward gaming and creative work. (This is just in the context of our artificial performance benchmark, by the way. The competition won't throttle you as much when you're running demanding software IRL, but the Zenbook A14's results are amazing nonetheless.)

That impressive performance looks even better when you factor in this 14-inch laptop's battery life test results. It lasted over 27 hours on a single charge, up from 22 hours in the previous generation. It's the fourth-longest-lasting laptop we've ever tried.

The Zenbook A14 sports a colorful OLED display and a satisfying keyboard. Its tan chassis is made of a magnesium-aluminum alloy called "Ceraluminum" that's durable, scratch-resistant, and deceptively lightweight. It weighs just 2.4 pounds, or 0.3 pounds less than the 13-inch MacBook Air. That doesn't sound like a huge difference, but I was shocked the very first time I picked one up. It still feels super sturdy despite its featheriness, fortunately, so I'd have no qualms about toting it to an office, airport, or coffee shop. (You'll want to show it off in public, by the way. That Ceraluminum chassis has a ceramic look and feel to it, and it's adorned with reflective gold Asus logos that remind me of jewelry. It's very "organic modern" and unique.)

Windows users on a budget: The newly revamped Dell XPS 13 is a wise pick for adult users with basic workloads and tight pursestrings (who don't want that reflected in their laptop's build quality). It's also a fantastic starter PC for kids and younger students.

Dongle bearers and wireless headphone users: The XPS 13 only has two Thunderbolt 4 (USB-C) ports. There's not even a headphone jack.

Anyone looking for a great MacBook Neo alternative: The XPS 13 has a longer battery life than the Neo, a nicer touchscreen display, and added keyboard backlighting.

Mashable's full review of the Dell XPS 13 is coming soon.

The new XPS 13 is the Windows version of the MacBook Neo: a hyper-affordable laptop with a deceptively high-end look and feel. It's made from full aluminum, with no plastic components to speak of, and it features a remarkably nice touchscreen that's much smoother and slightly sharper than the Neo's non-touch display. (Its 120Hz refresh rate makes it better for streaming movies.) What's more, its keyboard is backlit. It's very portable, coming in just as thin but half a pound lighter than the Neo.

The Intel Core 5 320 chip inside the base XPS 13 is a little pokier than the Neo's A18 Pro chip, but it's totally adequate for basic computer tasks like writing emails and surfing the web. Just be mindful of its memory usage: It starts with only 8GB of RAM, which Windows eats up quickly. On the plus side, you have the option of upgrading to a model with more RAM if your budget allows. Its storage and processor are also highly upgradable, though you'll venture out of "budget" territory with the mid-tier and upper-end variants.

Finally, it should be noted that the XPS 13 is the best budget laptop for battery life. It lasted nearly 19 hours in our video rundown test, outliving the Neo by four hours and our longest-lasting Chromebook by 45 minutes.

Chronic charger-forgetters: The HP OmniBook 3 16 with Qualcomm's base Snapdragon X chip hit a ridiculous 40 hours and 14 minutes in our video rundown test before dying. It's our battery life champ.

Casual users: This svelte and understated big-screened notebook is best-suited for light everyday use, not demanding creative work or gaming.

Read Mashable's full review of the HP OmniBook 3 16.

Many laptops boast an all-day battery life, but the OmniBook 3 16 has an all-days battery life of just over 40 hours. It's the longest-lasting laptop we've ever tested by a wide margin. (Our previous battery life champ, the OmniBook 5 14 with the Snapdragon X Plus chip, lasted 32.5 hours per charge.) Snapdragon-powered laptops are usually battery life freaks, in our experience, but this one's stamina is next-level. It lasts almost twice as long as the M5 MacBook Pro, our longest-lasting MacBook.

While the OmniBook 5 14 isn't as powerful, flashy, or premium-feeling as some of the other laptops on this list, it's still a sleek and solid laptop. Its island-style keyboard is snappy and easy to get used to. Its speakers are above-average and fine for most people's usage purposes. Its 1200p IPS display is colorful — and huge.

As a 16-inch laptop, the OmniBook 3 16 comes in at a relatively average weight of 3.65 pounds, but it measures just 0.58 inches thin. It's not as portable as the Asus Zenbook A16, but still pretty sleek for its size.

Frequent travelers and commuters: The ultraportable Acer Swift Edge 14 AI is one of the lightest and leanest laptops available. It's inconspicuous inside of a backpack, briefcase, or tote bag.

Value hunters: Though it's not a standout performer, this $1,500 laptop still punches above its price point with 32GB of RAM, a terabyte of storage, and a beauteous 2.8K OLED touchscreen. No wonder it's been tough to find in stock lately.

Read Mashable's full review of the Acer Swift Edge 14 AI.

Forget the MacBook Air: The feathery Swift Edge 14 AI makes Apple's ultraportable seem bulky in comparison. It weighs 2.18 pounds, or 0.3 pounds less than the 13-inch Air, and it only measures 0.37 inches at its slimmest point. The Air is 0.07 inches thicker despite having fewer ports. (The Swift Edge 14 AI has two Thunderbolt 4/USB-C ports, a pair of USB-A ports, and an HDMI port.) Its chassis is made from a magnesium-aluminum alloy, and while it feels a little plasticky, "I was surprised by how sturdy [it] felt," said Mashable contributor Sarah Chaney, our reviewer. It looks pretty cool, too: It comes in a pearl white finish with gold accents.

Additionally, you get quite a good display for the money here. The Swift Edge 14 AI has a 2.8K OLED touchscreen with a 120Hz refresh rate and a matte finish that makes it easy to see in almost any environment. "The picture is sharp, there’s great contrast with rich, true blacks, and colors appear vibrant and realistic, not overly saturated," Chaney said.

The Swift Edge 14 AI's last-gen Intel chip isn't an outstanding performer, but it has an above-average battery life of almost 21 hours per charge. That's probably more important for the on-the-go workers who will be drawn in by its unrivaled portability.

Professional creatives: Photo editors, video editors, and content creators — anyone who runs intensive apps on the reg, really — will be served incredibly well by the M5 MacBook Pro.

Apple users who game: Windows laptops are waaaaay better for gaming than MacBooks from hardware and compatibility standpoints, but the MacBook Pro can handle many AAA titles if you really need it to. Werth was able to play Cyberpunk 2077 on it in his testing.

People who want a long-lasting MacBook: This is the best Apple laptop for battery life.

Read Mashable's full review of the Apple MacBook Pro (M5).

The M5 MacBook Air is a beyond-fine pick for most use cases, but the M5 MacBook Pro offers some upgrades that are valuable for professional creatives — including content creators. (Jools LeBron of "very demure, very mindful" fame uses a MacBook Pro to edit videos and stay in touch with her fans.) It's got built-in fans that prevent thermal throttling when you're running heavy workloads for long periods of time, plus useful extra ports like an SDXC card slot, plus a much nicer display. Its crisp mini-LED screen has a 120Hz refresh rate and maxes out at an ultra-bright 1,600 nits for HDR content. You can further upgrade it with an optional nano-texture finish that minimizes distracting reflections.

Moreover, this is the longest-lasting MacBook we've ever tried. It ran for 21 hours and 17 minutes before dying.

High-earning business professionals: The Asus ExpertBook Ultra is a near-perfect, do-it-all ultraportable with fabulous performance, solid battery life, an absolutely gorgeous display, and an impossibly thin design. Its price reflects that.

Anyone who wants a really, really, really nice screen: I can't stay enough about this laptop's display. It's to die for.

On paper, ExpertBook Ultra seems like it shouldn't exist: A laptop that's much lighter than a MacBook Air and just as slim, yet as powerful as an entry-level gaming laptop? Not possible... so you'd think! The only catch is that it's quite expensive, which shouldn't be all that shocking given its specs.

The ExpertBook Ultra runs on Intel's new Core Ultra X7 385H chip, an upper mid-range CPU that offers above-average single-core performance and MacBook Pro-level multi-core performance — which is to say, it can easily handle the most demanding professional workloads. The neat thing about that chip is that it comes with the Intel Arc B390, an integrated graphics card (iGPU) that performs as well as a lower-end discrete graphics card. In my testing, the ExpertBook Ultra could run Cyberpunk 2077, an incredibly taxing AAA game, well above its 60fps minimum with AI upscaling tech turned on. What's more, it stayed impressively cool and quiet thanks to the wind tunnel-style thermal setup inside its base.

The ExpertBook Ultra weighs a mere 2.4 pounds and measures 0.43 inches thin at its slimmest point, making it incredibly sleek and portable. (It's 0.3 pounds lighter than a 13-inch MacBook Air and 0.01 inches thinner.) Its magnesium-aluminum body has a matte finish that isn't prone to smudges and scratches. It features a haptic touchpad, a snappy island-style keyboard with a UV coating that prevents its keys from looking greasy, and a surprisingly nice mix of ports, including double-sided Thunderbolt/USB-C charging ports.

But my favorite thing about its design is its display: It's a luscious 2.8K OLED touchscreen with a 30 to 120Hz refresh rate, 1,400 nits of peak brightness, and a matte finish that averts annoying glares. I've never encountered a nicer display on any laptop — PC, Mac, or Chromebook.

Mobile movie-watchers and multitaskers: The Asus Zenbook A16 is an impressively lightweight big-screener with mega-zippy, MacBook-beating performance. It's actually the lightest 16-inch laptop we've ever tried.

People who need a lot of RAM on a daily basis: Most configurations of the Zenbook A16 (including the model I tested) come with 48GB of memory. That, combined with its supercharged processor (which has a giant NPU), makes it apt for on-device AI workloads and similarly resource-heavy tasks.

It should come as no surprise that the 16-inch version of my top pick for Windows users is also very good. The Zenbook A16 doesn't last as long as its little brother, the Zenbook A14, but it offers way more power and adds an SD card slot in addition to a sharper, brighter, smoother OLED touchscreen. (Its battery life is still decent for a big, fast laptop, for the record.)

The Zenbook A16 runs on the high-end Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme chip, which keeps pace with Apple's M5 chip in single-core scenarios but squashes it in multi-core tasks. It's 38 percent faster than the M5 MacBook Air and 27 percent faster than the base MacBook Pro on the latter metric. It feels so, so peppy no matter what you're using it for.

To continue the MacBook comparisons, I think even the biggest Apple fans will appreciate the Zenbook A16's design. While it lacks the great speakers, crisp webcam, and haptic touchpad of a MacBook, it has way more ports, a nicer display, and a significantly lighter build. It weighs almost half a pound less than a 15-inch MacBook Air. (Who's airy now, huh?) As with the Zenbook A14, its rigid Ceraluminum construction is the reason it doesn't feel flimsy.

Topics Apple Dell HP MacBook Memory Crisis

Mid-September 2026: I've added the Dell XPS 13 as a new pick following our latest round of testing. It's now the best budget Windows laptop we've tried. I also checked all of our current picks for any new pricing and availability updates; there are none to report at this time.

Early September 2026: I added more information about the various factors affecting laptop prices right now. I also added a new section about some new laptops that shoppers can look forward to in the coming months. Finally, I updated three of our picks in the wake of recent pricing and availability changes. I removed the Acer Chromebook Plus 516 as our top Chromebook because of its value-zapping $170 price hike, and I'm now looking for its replacement. The Asus Zenbook A16 got a $300 price bump, but I'm keeping it as our best 16-inch laptop (for now) because it's still not a rip-off in the current market. Lastly, the Acer Swift Edge 14 AI was out of stock at the time of writing, but may not be sold out for good. I'll continue to monitor its listings over the next few weeks and will swap it for another model if it turns out it's actually been discontinued. In the meantime, a refurbished Swift Edge 14 AI is readily available.

July 2026: The Acer Swift Edge 14 AI is officially the most portable laptop we've ever tested: It only weighs 2.18 pounds and measures 0.37 inches thin at its slimmest point. I added it as a new pick.

June 2026: I made the Asus ExpertBook Ultra our new favorite business laptop and named the Asus Zenbook A16 the best 16-inch laptop. I also removed the Alienware 16X Aurora as our top gaming laptop because the configuration we tested has been discontinued. We're currently testing potential replacements.

In early September, PC makers showed off a slew of new Windows laptops at IFA, an annual tech trade show in Berlin. Among them are the ultralight Acer Swift Blade 14, the very repairable Acer Swift Vero 16, two new Lenovo creator laptops running on Nvidia RTX Spark, and a pair of colorful MacBook Neo competitors: the Lenovo IdeaPad Vibe and the pared-down Dell 14S. The IdeaPad Vibe is confirmed to arrive this October, starting at $699.99, but most other IFA debutants don't have set prices or release dates just yet.

On the MacBook side, Apple is rumored to be working on a refreshed MacBook Pro with an M6 chip (first seen in this summer's new Mac mini), as well as a higher-end MacBook Pro with an OLED touchscreen. The latter might be called the "MacBook Ultra," if the gossip mill is correct. The M6 model is reportedly coming this fall, while the Ultra could debut at the end of this year or early next.

Many great Windows laptops we've tried run on Qualcomm's Snapdragon X and X2 series chips, which use ARM-based architecture instead of standard x86 architecture like Intel and AMD chips. The Zenbook A14 and A16 are two great examples of outstanding ARM laptops.

These Qualcomm chips are great because they offer amazing performance and battery life, but they're not compatible with some specialty software and PC games. Having said that, ARM support is getting better and should dramatically improve in the near future. Right now, I would only avoid ARM laptops if you're a student or if gaming is the main thing you do on your PC. You can read more about this in my dedicated guides to the best Windows laptops and the best laptops for college.

A laptop with 512GB of storage and 16GB of memory (RAM) is going to be a solid daily driver and a future-proofed investment for most people. That said, if you're a PC gamer or someone who dabbles in creative work (like graphic design or photo and video editing), I recommend defaulting to 1TB of storage with 32GB of RAM to accommodate your heavier workload.

Many budget laptops start with just 256GB of storage and 8GB of RAM, which is workable for casual users who mainly browse the web, write documents, and send emails. Just know that it'll feel pokey if you have too many tabs open and may not be able to run certain software. You'll also probably have to supplement it with an external hard drive eventually, unless you stash most of your files in the cloud.

There are two huge pros to buying a refurbished laptop, one being that they're usually sold at a steep discount. By opting for "new to you" (instead of "brand-new, fresh out the box"), you can maximize your budget and score specs that would normally be out of your price range. Secondly, buying refurbished is just plain good for the environment. Every pre-owned laptop purchase keeps a still-usable device out of a landfill.

The key is to make sure any refurbished laptop you buy is in good-enough condition to last a few years and protected by a decent warranty. For more tips, read Mashable's guide to buying refurbished tech.

The HP OmniBook 3 16 with the Snapdragon X chip is the longest-lasting laptop we've ever tried. It held out for 40 hours and 14 minutes in our video rundown test. Coming in second place is the Dell 14S with the Intel Core Ultra 7 355 chip, which lasted 34 hours and 29 minutes on one charge. Meanwhile, the longest-lasting MacBook we've tested is the 14-inch Apple MacBook Pro (M5), which died at 21 hours and 17 minutes.

For more on them and other long-lasting models, check out our standalone guide to the best battery life laptops.

Mashable has been writing about laptops for over a decade, and I've personally been covering them since 2023. I also helped develop the rigorous hands-on testing process we currently use to review them. This methodology revolves around four criteria:

The laptops we review get put to work as our primary computers. This includes trying any unique software or use cases they support. We also subject all of our loaners to a multi-app/tab stress test and Primate Labs' Geekbench CPU test, which measures their performance in common tasks. Gaming laptops get put through additional graphical benchmarking.

To gauge a laptop's stamina, we conduct a battery rundown test that involves playing a looped 1080p version of "Tears of Steel," a short open-source Blender movie, at 50 percent brightness and 50 percent volume. Ideally, we hope to get at least 17.5 hours of battery life from MacBooks, 16.5 hours from Windows laptops, 11 hours from Chromebooks, and five hours from gaming laptops. (They're notoriously power-hungry.)

As we're using a laptop, we zero in on certain components to evaluate its build quality. These include the display, keyboard, touchpad, webcam, speakers, and ports. We also assess its overall aesthetic and portability.

We determine the ultimate value of a laptop by comparing its performance, design/build quality, and battery life to other laptops with similar pricing, specs, release dates, and use cases. We consider any accessories it comes with, any upgrades from its predecessor(s), and its future-proofing.

Haley Henschel is a Chicago-based Senior Shopping Reporter at Mashable who reviews and finds deals on popular tech, from laptops to gaming consoles and VPNs. She has years of experience covering shopping holidays and can tell you what’s actually worth buying on Black Friday and Amazon Prime Day. Her work has also explored the driving forces behind digital trends within the shopping sphere, from dupes to 12-foot skeletons.

#373. The Tragedy of Having a Wikipedia Page—and Then Losing It

What do you need to know—need to know—about Xavier Trudeau? The eldest son of former Canadian prime minister Justin, the teenage Trudeau has recently begun performing R&B under the mononym Xav. But, with all due respect to Xav, do those facts make him a person of note? Should you expect to be able to read about him on Wikipedia? What about Evanescence guitarist Tim McCord, or 2012 Yachtmaster of the Year Rainer Holtorff?

All three have had a biography on Wikipedia; all three of those biographies were considered for deletion this year on the grounds that the subjects were not notable enough to warrant a biography at all. Every day, dozens, sometimes hundreds, of Wikipedia biographies—of child actors turned LA realtors, retired ambassadors, soccer players who have played less than 10 minutes of professional soccer—are purged from the world’s free encyclopedia.

It’s not as though Wikipedia, which hosts over 7 million articles in the English language, doesn’t have the space. But Wikipedia has only so many volunteers to monitor its contents. To this day, Wikipedia editors are discovering hoaxes that have gone undetected for decades. The time it takes to verify the release dates of Xav’s three singles is better spent—the logic goes—by asking whether the American Civil War officer Nathaniel Lyon really drank mustard during battle, as was claimed in his Wikipedia biography for 17 years. Wikipedia removed the mustard claim, not because it was proven wrong, but because there was no reliable evidence for it. “We’re not really looking for what’s true,” Britta Gustafson, who has been editing Wikipedia since 2001, told me. “We’re looking for what’s verifiable.”

Five hauntings from the other side of technology.

Have a ghost story of your own? Share it in the comments below.

Wikipedia editors do not themselves decide that a person is not notable, only that there is a lack of reliable evidence to support the claim. One person who recently lost her Wikipedia biography on notability grounds is Kimberly Small, the CEO of a Nevada nonprofit that supports survivors of sexual assault. The debate occurred in a public page on Wikipedia itself; it’s in that space that the really tricky decisions about what is and what isn’t good enough for the encyclopedia are made. One editor raised a question: “How should we judge not-for-profit executives? By the nature of their job, the focus is, as it should be, on the organization and the work the organization does, not them.” Another editor replied: “I don’t think there is anything wrong with not-for-profit executives being less often individually notable. Wikipedia coverage is not a prize.”

Is it not? “Having an article says something,” Gustafson admitted. “It implies something.” For six weeks over April and May, I reached out to hundreds of people who were about to lose their Wikipedia page, almost all of whom were learning that information from me. “If you want to write something to save the page, I would appreciate it,” a Spanish filmmaker told me. A biography on Wikipedia might not be intended as a prize, but nobody knows its value like the people who’ve had one and then lost it.

Martina Dimoska is a scientist, originally from North Macedonia, who works at NASA’s Johnson Space Center in Houston. Specifically, Dimoska is an “analog astronaut”—she takes part in missions that simulate a space environment—and a “science communicator.” She posts often about her work in the can-do, aspirational register that is de rigueur on LinkedIn, where she cites her “recognition by … Forbes, Bloomberg, the United Nations … and global innovation networks.” A Facebook reel of hers, filmed during the crew announcement of NASA’s upcoming Artemis III mission, shows Dimoska alongside the text, “When you work hard so you belong in rooms where history gets proactively written.” On TikTok, she’s had paid partnerships with a makeup brand whose products are tested for effectiveness during spaceflight.

On April 29, Dimoska posted on Facebook, “Wait … I’m on Wikipedia now???” She went on: “A few years ago, building a career in space from the Balkans felt impossible. And now … somehow, that journey is part of the public record. I’m incredibly grateful that [the biography] is there … My hope is that through my journey, I will help a new generation to begin to see space as accessible, not distant.”

The next day, Dimoska’s page was nominated for deletion.

Anne Clin, who has edited Wikipedia since 2005 under the name Risker, warned me that it’s folly to celebrate becoming the subject of a Wikipedia article. “Especially if you’re what we call ‘borderline notable,’” she said.

The other taboo is editing information about yourself. You can’t do that—even if the existing facts about you are wrong—without disclosing a conflict of interest. Even then, it’s strongly discouraged. Jimmy Wales, the cofounder of Wikipedia, has been caught editing his own Wikipedia biography multiple times; that he did so is now part of his Wikipedia biography. In 2022, the novelist Emily St. John Mandel, unable to update her biography to reflect her recent divorce, sat for an interview solely to create a secondary source for that information.

Some high-profile Wikipedia editors and volunteers have bio pages about themselves. Clin is occasionally asked if she would want one. She doesn’t merely refuse; she actively sabotages the possibility. “You cannot write a proper biographical article about me, because I don’t give out enough personal information to do that,” she said. “Absolutely not. Absolutely not.”

Among the people reading Martina Dimoska’s Facebook post about her new Wikipedia page was Kiril Simeonovski, an economist in Skopje, Macedonia, and a Wikipedia editor since 2008. Simeonovski found the post self-congratulatory and the biography itself entirely promotional. He also doubted the inherent notability of an “analog astronaut,” a role that he later likened to a lab mouse.

A Wikipedia editor can tag an article for deletion, and unless satisfactory edits are made within a certain timeframe, the page will disappear. Simeonovski didn’t do this—he referred the article for debate among his fellow editors.

Arguing the case for deletion, Simeonovski described the Dimoska biography as “merely [a] chronology of overhyped personal achievements,” each meticulously deflated by Simeonovski, that did not credibly establish Dimoska as a notable individual.

More damningly, in his argument, was the timing of Dimoska’s Facebook post, coming “shortly after the article was approved.” To him, this was a smoking gun as much as anything. How could she have so promptly promoted the page if she were not somehow connected to it?

When an editor proposes a Wikipedia article for deletion, they append a caveat to the marked page: “If you created this page, please don’t take offense.” This courtesy is not extended to the subject of the article. When I reached out to these people, in the hundreds, to ask them how it felt to be considered non-notable, I was not surprised that the response rate to this question was about 1 percent. Maybe lower.

Matthew Songer, a Michigan orthopedist with three decades of experience, had his biography flagged for deletion (“Unlikely to be notable”) in April. Songer was among the 1 percent of respondees. He told me it’s not for vanity that he needs that page. He is often enlisted as an expert witness in trials, he said, because of the extra imprimatur of being on Wikipedia. The page “is a good reference for attorneys.”

Maneli Mirkhan, a French-Iranian adviser on international affairs, agreed that having a Wikipedia biography inspires confidence in her services. Her biography was called out by Wikipedia editors for reading like a résumé.

The biography of Andrew Wyant, a Phoenix-area executive who works in fitness and nutrition, was also deemed insufficiently notable, due to a lack of coverage from secondary sources. Wyant said he blamed himself for failing to “feed” his public persona through self-promotion. “What I know to be true is that I am largely responsible for the growth of the profession of personal trainers and fitness coaches by building the entire category over the last decade,” he told me. “I feel my accomplishments and impact are notable, but I don’t feel the need to promote them in media so I can have a Wikipedia page.”

Wyant’s biography was deleted. Songer’s biography was deleted. Mirkhan’s biography was deleted.

Of those, only Mirkahn’s reappeared, rewritten and with new sources, a few days later. I asked Mirkhan if she had been involved in re-creating the page. No, she said, but she wasn’t surprised. Mirkhan had been, and continues to be, sought out by the French media to comment on events in Iran, and more coverage means more demonstrations of notability. But there’s always that chicken-and-egg problem: Mirkhan thinks that what gave the media confidence in choosing her as a commentator was not just her career and her expertise. It was the fact that she was on Wikipedia.

Kiril Simeonovski did not lead with his suspicions that Martina Dimoska wrote her own Wikipedia biography. He argued, on Wikipedia’s public page for editorial debates, that the biography’s sourcing was weak, relying in part on posts from Dimoska’s LinkedIn page. It promoted Dimoska’s social media accounts, where she posted things like—and this Simeonovski found objectionable enough to cite in his argument for deletion—a picture comparing herself to NASA software engineer Margaret Hamilton. Published three separate times, it became one of her most popular posts, engendering sexist mockery and vicious defense.

To Simeonovski, self-promotion like this suggested that the case for Dimoska’s notability was regularly oversold by Dimoska herself. Though he could not prove a connection, it was difficult to imagine the Wikipedia biography as anything but an extension of that project.

That afternoon, a new Wikipedia user added comments to Simeonovski’s public arraignment, noting three existing articles about other individual analog astronauts. A fair contribution, tempered by a shot across the bow at Simeonovski: The user’s name was “Kiril’s mom.” The user was not Simeonovski’s mom, and the account was subsequently blocked. Hours later, another new user asked Simeonovski, rhetorically, “If I publish a blog post about someone and he finds it amazing and celebrates it does that mean that he/she created it?” Their username was a coded reference to the actual name of Simeonovski’s mother. They also got blocked.

Nine days later, another anonymous user—whose IP address, according to Simeonovski, placed them in Skopje, North Macedonia—said Dimoska’s biography had improved “considerably,” with new sourcing from publications like The Telegraph. The revised article, the user claimed, was superior to “85%” of Wikipedia articles about living Macedonians.

Three days later, Simeonovski dismantled each of the 32 new sources in turn. It wasn’t really the British newspaper The Telegraph that had been cited, for instance, but the Albanian news website Telegrafi. “I fail to find a single independent and reputable English-language source,” he later wrote.

The next day, the anonymous user rebutted Simeonovski point by point. They said it was “strange” that Simeonovski could not seem to find a single appropriate source for Dimoska’s biography but had found “10 sources from non-independent websites” when he wrote an article about the former governor of the National Bank of North Macedonia. The National Bank of North Macedonia, the user added to Simeonovski: Isn’t that where you work?

At one point, Simeonovski said he would report his detractor to the Wikimedia Foundation legal department.

Of Rosemary Bush, a professor at Northwestern University in Illinois, a Wikipedia editor recently wrote: “It will be many years until she qualifies as notable.” Bush herself agreed, to a point, that the Wikipedia page about her did not make a strong case, though she found the biography itself to be strange, possibly AI-generated, and not wholly representative of her career. “The world is fine without a Wikipedia page on me,” she said, but added, “I do think the comment proposing deletion is a bit rude.”

A little rudeness is unavoidable in this line of work. Sonia Jones, a Welsh singer best known for the theme song from the 1979 film Monty Python’s Life of Brian, is indeed best known for the theme song from Monty Python’s Life of Brian; therefore, Wikipedia editors decided that her Wikipedia biography should be deleted and henceforth redirect to the article about the song. Jones, when I asked her for a comment, did not agree that her achievements since 1979 do not merit documentation. “No, I do not want them to remove my page,” she said. “Why would they?”

“Canada has a national dodgeball team? Who would have thought?” wrote one editor in their case to delete the biography of Savannah Burton, the first transgender athlete to compete for Canada’s national dodgeball team. “Even though dodgeball is not considered a mainstream sport, competing at the highest level in any sport is a significant accomplishment,” Burton told me. “Given the ongoing attempts to erase transgender people, along with infringements on our rights and our ability to live normal lives, this action is not surprising.”

There is, when you look for it, a careless and cocky tone that can creep into the verdicts of Wikipedia editors who have been doing the job for a long time. “This guy is a nobody. Delete him.” “I’ve got more followers on Instagram than he does.” “All we have here are stand-up comedy, opinion columns, being a guest or collaborator on some non-notable podcasts … This is more or less every twentieth person who lives in NYC.”

Rosemary Bush’s biography was deleted. Sonia Jones’ biography was deleted. Savannah Burton’s biography was retained. “It was easy to find more sources,” wrote an editor who came to the page’s rescue. Editors’ participation in this process is not mandatory. Articles, once marked for death, can be deleted simply because nobody showed up to debate the matter. That anyone would volunteer their time to save an undeserving article from oblivion is a kindness: a good deed in a rude world.

Kiril Simeonovski never did refer the anonymous editor of Martina Dimoska’s biography to the Wikimedia Foundation’s legal team. Instead, he and his antagonist continued their debate on sourcing and semantics for days, and then weeks.

Per her Wikipedia page, Dimoska’s achievements included the command of two analog astronaut missions in Poland, but Simeonovski argued that while this information was verifiable, the missions themselves did not meet the threshold of notability and therefore neither did Dimoska. The anonymous user pointed to Dimoska’s inclusion in a Macedonian book for schoolchildren about 50 Macedonian women: “The book was subsequently recommended by the Bureau for Development of Education as optional reading material for ninth-grade students nationwide, which gives the subject recurring exposure within the educational system rather than temporary media attention.”

“You’ve apparently regressed to using children’s books to support the notability of the person we’re discussing,” said Simeonovski. “I expect [you] to continue gaslighting with multi-paragraph comments, but that won’t change the observation that the person isn’t notable [enough] for a Wikipedia article.”

“Count your words and then mine … and see who is the one gaslighting with multi-paragraph comments.”

“You seem to mix up ‘prominence’ and ‘notability.’”

“Your argument relies heavily on personal anecdotes and assumptions about how Macedonian media operates.”

Other editors with an eye on the debate pleaded for colleagues to lend their perspective. Most supported Simeonovski, but they didn’t linger long. “At this point the most visible thing about this individual seems to be [this] article,” one said, before bouncing.

As this unfolded, I asked Dimoska what she made of it. “The whole thing has become quite challenging to track,” she said, “and I’m already behind on my social media.” She denied editing the page herself or having any knowledge of the people defending it. She added: “Watching strangers debate your life, work, and credibility in such an intense way is definitely a strange experience to process.”

On April 25, a user named “DavidfromArizona” took to Wikipedia to announce that his friend Sheldon Marks—“the world’s leading vasectomy reversal expert,” per Marks’ own website—wished for his Wikipedia biography to be removed.

“It is inaccurate and does not truly represent his achievements,” David wrote. “[His] ego is hurt by having such a page. He either wants a detailed page or wants this one deleted.” A request from the subject of a Wikipedia article is not grounds for deletion in itself, although it can tip the balance if there is no clear consensus. On examination, Wikipedia editors found the biography lacking in sources that demonstrated notability.

On May 6, a Wikipedia user who 17 years earlier had created a biography for Sophie Aston, a British landscape artist, said that Aston had recently asked that the page be deleted. “She has retrained as a psychotherapist and wishes to forget her career as an artist.”

Having seen how much a Wikipedia page matters to certain people, I asked Marks and Aston why they wanted theirs removed.

“A kind and enthusiastic man made my Wiki page as a surprise for me about 17 years ago,” Aston told me. “We’d been on two dates. I found it a bit embarrassing but sweet. I didn’t see him again; I wasn’t into him. The info on the page was never [very] accurate and is now very out of date.” It was “ostentatious,” she added, for Wikipedia to present her as an artist of note, not least because for the past 10 years she has been a professional psychotherapist. The biography, she said, “makes it sound like I am an important artist, and I am not—it’s inappropriate.”

The Wikipedia editors could not treat Aston’s request with undue favor, but given the chance to cast fresh eyes over the article, they found her biography lacking. “The request to delete her article is not relevant,” one wrote bluntly, “but I don’t view her artistic career as meeting [notability] guidelines regardless of the request.” Aston’s biography was deleted.

Sheldon Marks didn’t respond to me. After receiving my email, however, he fled straight to the debate unfolding on Wikipedia. “I did not request deletion of this article,” he wrote. The statements made by DavidfromArizona, whom Marks did not know, were apparently fabrications. Marks wanted to keep his page. He admitted it could be improved but maintained that he was a notable individual: “I am the author of Vasectomy Reversal: Manual of Vasovasostomy and Vasoepididymostomy (Springer, 2018), translated into Chinese by Springer Nature in 2025; the surgical instrument known as the Marks Vas Cutting Forceps is named for me; I have served as Course Coordinator for the AUA post-graduate course on vasectomy reversal; and I have been a contributor to WebMD since 2005.”

The Wikipedia editors briefly conferred, then replied that they had already voted to delete.

There’s a final twist in the biography of Martina Dimoska: She knew Kiril Simeonovski—the guy trying to get her deleted.

“I had interacted with him on social media before and had crossed paths through mutual friends,” Dimoska said. “One recent example I remember clearly is seeing his post after he was injured by a cyclist who had left the scene.” Dimoska recalled the post in detail, though she had not responded or reacted to it at the time.

Simeonovski said he didn’t remember ever speaking with Dimoska in person but admitted the possibility of “virtual interaction.” Both are active on Facebook, and though they do not follow each other, Simeonovski said they have enough mutual friends in North Macedonia that their respective algorithms often put them in each other’s path. Just as Dimoska saw Simeonovski’s post about his accident, so Simeonovski came across Dimoska’s celebration of inclusion in Wikipedia, which led him to discover Dimoska’s earlier Instagram post comparing herself to Margaret Hamilton, then to a much older Reddit thread asking if Dimoska was the smartest Macedonian woman. (“Isn’t it just all hype about that girl?” one Redditor replied.) Simeonovski didn’t comment on any of those posts. But in Wikipedia he had found an opportunity to contest her notability on surer ground.

“It was somewhat surprising to me, perhaps because I have become accustomed to professional environments where people tend to be more openly supportive of one another’s work,” Dimoska told me. “At the same time, having grown up in North Macedonia, I am aware that recognition within a small community can sometimes generate more scrutiny than one might expect.”

One month after Simeonovski nominated Dimoska’s biography for deletion, he lost. The page was kept. “Given the bludgeoning, canvassing, and sockpuppetry evident here, this is a no consensus closure without prejudice against speedy renomination,” wrote an editor in a determination. “The arguments to delete here are stronger on the merits, but sufficient reasoned opposition exists that I do not see a consensus for deletion.” Obstinacy won the day, though Dimoska’s page now carries a disclaimer that it contains “promotional content.”

Wikipedia pages, for better or worse, matter to people. To be included is a validation of a life. If it were not so desirable, then one of the very first things Kiril Simeonovski did upon joining Wikipedia in 2008 would not have been to create a page about himself. Which he did do. He “wasn’t aware about the notability policies” back then, he told me.

The page was promptly deleted. “If your achievements, etc., are verifiable and genuinely notable and thus suitable for inclusion in Wikipedia,” an editor chided Simeonovski at the time, “someone else will probably create an article about you sooner or later.” To date, nobody has.

#372. Russell Brand told police he led a ‘very promiscuous’ life, trial hears

Comedian accused of sexual offences against six women said consensual encounters were ‘unfavourably reframed decades later’

Russell Brand told police he had led a “very promiscuous” life and consensual sexual encounters had been “unfavourably reframed decades later”, his trial has heard.

The 51-year-old comedian blamed the allegations on “defamatory and fictitious” media reports. He said in a statement that he had had sexual encounters in countless locations and scenarios and kissed hundreds of people in public while trying to be “friendly, consensual and warm”.

Brand is accused of seven offences against six women in allegations spanning 10 years from 1999. He denies all the charges.

Wearing a grey suit and pale blue shirt, he sat in the dock on Tuesday as his statements to police were read to Southwark crown court in London.

The jury was told Brand was interviewed four times between November 2023 and July 2025, when he read prepared statements in which he insisted he had “never engaged in any non-consensual sexual activity”.

“I led a very promiscuous life in the past, which I have spent years openly denouncing,” he said in his first statement on 16 November 2023.

Referencing joint reporting by Channel 4, the Times and the Sunday Times, Brand added: “On 16 September 2023, a defamatory and fictitious documentary was broadcast in conjunction with the publication of two national newspapers containing false, anonymised allegations which led to unprecedented media reporting.

“Subsequently, both the media and the police publicly invited additional allegations to be made, which resulted in further encounters being invented or reimagined.”

Addressing an allegation that he had touched an MTV worker’s breasts before dragging her into a toilet and raping her in 2004, he said he did not recall meeting the woman.

“At no point would I have touched [her] breasts unless it was consensual,” he said. “I have had consensual sex in toilet cubicles during parties. I have never forced anyone into a toilet and I have never forced my penis into anyone’s mouth.”

In a statement on 14 December 2023, Brand said he did not remember the women who had come forward to accuse him.

But he added: “It is entirely possible that I may have encountered them and that we may have engaged in consensual sexual activity or physical contact that has since been reimagined.”

Brand described aspects of his past celebrity lifestyle, saying: “My engagement with interested members of the public was friendly, consensual and warm. There was often physical contact. At live shows, press events, junkets and book signings I did my best to be accessible, non-discriminatory and loving. I spent a lot of time at these events.

“I have shared kisses with hundreds of members of the public during press events and photos of many such kisses, in front of hundreds of people, are available online. Everything I did in public once I was famous could be photographed or recorded and end up in the tabloids – I was aware of this.

“I routinely made provocative jokes or comments both with members of the public and during live performances and broadcasts.”

He also went on to detail his promiscuity, saying: “I did on occasion find reasons for leaving, or for asking partners in casual sexual encounters to leave, shortly after the experience had concluded.

“I had sexual encounters in countless locations and scenarios often with people I had just met. The way of life that I have described, the magnitude of the promiscuity, deceptively rendered in recent media attacks, accompanied by advertisements for complainants, have caused consensual encounters to be unfavourably reframed decades later.”

Brand gave similar statements to police in his subsequent interviews on 17 January 2024 and 17 July 2025. In response to police questions after each statement, Brand said: “I have nothing further to add at this time.”

Brand is accused of three counts of rape and three counts of sexual assault. On Tuesday, an allegation of indecent assault was changed to a charge of attempted indecent assault. He has pleaded not guilty to all charges.

#370. Albania's student protests are only getting bigger

As police try to suppress anti-corruption protests by young Albanians, Prime Minister Edi Rama is claiming that "antifa" is behind the unrest. Few are buying that explanation.

The fallout from the October 13 protests was still visible when students and faculty from the University of Tirana gathered outside the Education Ministry in Albania's capital on Tuesday to express their continued discontent. Smashed windows have yet to be replaced; the walls are still black with soot from fires that broke out during the protests.

Albania has seen nearly five months of peaceful anti-government protests, sparked initially when plans were made public that Jared Kushner — a son-in-law of US President Donald Trump — and associates intended to build a luxury hotel complex within a nature reserve.

Since then, anti-government and anti-corruption protests have escalated. Now, college and high school students clash with police forces almost daily, with the fiercest clashes so far occurring last week.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Protesters denounce corruption, mismanaged universities and the rising cost of education in Albania — and call for the resignation of Prime Minister Edi Rama, who has been in office since 2013.

Wearing a black hooded jacket and a scarf across her face to conceal her identity, a woman who asked to be called Sara because she fears repercussions for speaking to the press told DW that protesters "communicate via text messages — that's the only way."

She cut the interview short as some protesters began flinging stones at the Education Ministry. Police pushed demonstrators and journalists out of the way, using water cannons to disperse the crowd.

"Social media is the most important public space today — more so than physical spaces," Besmir Semanaj, a prominent Albanian cybersecurity advocate told DW. "Young Albanians no longer organize through traditional structures such as political parties, associations or public appeals. Instead, they organize themselves where they spend much of their daily lives: on their cellphones."

"The most important communication channels include Instagram, WhatsApp groups for academic-year cohorts, departments and dormitories, Telegram channels for general announcements, and TikTok for sharing videos," Semanaj said. He added that people who are especially concerned with security use the Signal messaging app.

"This is a horizontal organizational structure, without individual 'leaders' and without a central command structure," Semanaj said. "That is exactly why the government is having such a hard time understanding and controlling the situation."

Semanaj said the Telegram messenger app had proven particularly useful for protesters. "Telegram channels allow you to reach tens of thousands of people at once," he said. "Notifications are sent out within seconds, and external control is more difficult to establish than on traditional platforms."

Rama has called the youth uprising a "hybrid war." He said protesters thought they could "make Albania a better country through TikTok and without even being able to string together more than seven coherent words." Though not all are terrorists, he said, he believes that "they are hostages to a certain mindset, a new ideology that has only one goal: to destroy, to turn everything upside down, to raze everything to the ground."

Over nearly five months, Rama has accused various "true" masterminds of organizing the anti-government and anti-corruption protests. He has alleged that the governments of Greece, Russia and Iran are stirring up dissent.

Most recently, Rama said it was "antifa" — or a decentralized movement of global antifascists — that had stoked anti-government and anti-corruption sentiments in Albania. "These are young people who have been radicalized and ideologically indoctrinated and who are acting under the pressure or influence of a new, dark force that has emerged in Europe and the United States, a force that the President of the United States has designated as a terrorist organization: Antifa," Rama claimed.

Redion Qirjazi, a security affairs researcher who focuses on violence prevention and conflict resolution, told DW that Rama's claim was not likely to prove true. "That is a serious accusation — one that requires equally compelling evidence," Qirjazi said. "To date, no verifiable evidence has been presented to prove the existence of an organized Antifa structure in Albania, let alone its involvement in coordinating the protests."

Qirjazi said Rama's reference to Trump's calling antifa a terror group was problematic. "Antifa is not a unified international organization with a centralized structure or leadership," he said, "but rather an umbrella term for various movements and groups united by the fact that they are, at their core, anti-fascist."

Sara said she rejected the "terrorist" label that Rama had attempted to slap on her and her fellow protesters. "There are groups that are willing to use violence — that's true — but I have nothing to do with them," she said. "As soon as I see violence, I walk away; I'm against it."

Arlind Qori, the chairman of the Together Movement, a social-democratic opposition party with just one seat in Albania's parliament, said youths had taken to the streets out of dissatisfaction with the status quo rather than any particular ideology. "Young Albanians are outraged by the current socioeconomic conditions: the poverty, the fact that many of their parents have emigrated because they couldn't support their families, the decline of the education system, the lack of postgraduation prospects," Qori said. The students' central demand is for Rama to resign, he added  "not a socialist revolution."

"People aren't demanding anything radical," Qori said. "They merely want a corrupt head of government to resign — that's a fundamental and moderate demand. But Edi Rama continues to meet this demand with police violence and arrogance."

Qirjazi said Rama's allegations of foreign interference could be a strategy. "When a government faces protests and public discontent, linking demonstrators to extremism and foreign influence may serve to divert attention away from the actual causes and toward a national security narrative," he said.

"This can serve several political purposes: to delegitimize the protesters, to weaken their support among the public, and to justify a tougher crackdown in the name of law and order," Qirjazi said.

Semanaj said this was a common pattern in governments' efforts to dismiss dissent. "Protests are portrayed as acts of violence," he said. "Young people are cast as manipulated, and their legitimate concerns — conditions at universities, tuition fees, corruption and a lack of prospects — vanish from the news agenda."

#367. James Webb discovers a scorching lava planet with a “giant sunscreen”

Astronomers have discovered surprising evidence that a scorching planet covered in molten rock may be protected by a reflective layer of clouds that acts like a giant sunscreen. Scientists previously believed that the planet's extreme conditions would make it nearly impossible for an atmosphere to survive, but new observations suggest otherwise.

An international team of researchers, including Dr. Anjali Piette, an astrophysicist at the University of Birmingham, reported the findings in The Astronomical Journal. Using the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST), the scientists monitored a rocky exoplanet called TOI-561 b, located approximately 280 light-years from Earth. Over 37 hours, they observed nearly four complete orbits around its host star.

The observations indicate that TOI-561 b may be surrounded by a substantial atmosphere, although its conditions would be far too hostile for life as we know it. This atmosphere appears to help the planet reflect much of the radiation coming from its star. One possible explanation is a layer of highly reflective silicate clouds that prevents the surface from reaching the extreme temperatures expected on an exposed rocky planet. The atmosphere also appears to move heat from the permanently illuminated dayside toward the nightside, further reducing temperatures on the star-facing side.

A Lava Planet With an Atmosphere That Should Have Disappeared

Scientists still do not know exactly which gases make up the atmosphere of TOI-561 b. One possibility is a thick layer of carbon dioxide, resembling the atmosphere of Venus but under much hotter conditions. Another is an atmosphere dominated by sulfur dioxide, a gas released by volcanic activity on Jupiter's moon Io.

Dr. Anjali Piette, Assistant Professor in Astronomy at the University of Birmingham, said: "Our discovery challenges current theories. We would expect a heavily irradiated rocky planet like TOI-561 b to have lost its atmosphere billions of years ago.

"Instead, the evidence suggests it still has a significant atmosphere today. This raises major questions such as whether the planet started with an unusually large supply of gases or if the ocean of molten rock covering its surface, acts as a planet-wide volcano that is constantly feeding its atmosphere."

James Webb Reveals Why the Planet Is So Reflective

Earlier measurements revealed something puzzling about TOI-561 b. Its dayside was significantly cooler than researchers predicted for a dark, rocky planet orbiting so close to its star. That unexpected temperature suggested that much of the incoming energy was either being transferred to the nightside or reflected back into space before it could heat the surface.

To investigate, the team observed the planet throughout its orbit, allowing them to examine its nightside for the first time. Because the same side of TOI-561 b always faces its star, the nightside receives no direct starlight. However, the measurements showed that this darker hemisphere was not warm enough to explain all the missing heat.

The findings instead indicate that TOI-561 b reflects approximately 75% of the starlight reaching it, a remarkably high proportion comparable to Venus.

To determine what could produce such strong reflectivity, the researchers compared the telescope measurements with computer models of the planet's climate. Their simulations suggest that clouds containing silicate minerals, including silicon dioxide (SiO2) and magnesium silicate (MgSiO3), may form over the dayside. If present, these clouds could reflect incoming radiation before it reaches the surface, functioning as a natural sunscreen.

Professor Lisa Dang, who led the project at the University of Waterloo in Ontario, Canada, said: "We measured how the planet's brightness and heat changed throughout its orbit. This technique, known as a phase curve, revealed that the planet reflects a significant amount of light from its star, and that heat is being redistributed around the planet instead of only on the sunlit side. Together, these point to the presence of an atmosphere."

Could a Global Ocean of Lava Keep the Atmosphere Alive?

The new findings strengthen earlier evidence gathered by Professor Lisa Dang, Dr. Anjali Piette, and their collaborators that TOI-561 b has a thick atmosphere that may be continually replenished by gases escaping from its molten interior.

That possibility could help explain how the planet has maintained an atmosphere despite enduring intense radiation from its star for billions of years. Rather than simply losing its gases to space, the planet's vast reservoir of molten rock may be constantly releasing new material into the atmosphere.

Researchers now hope to determine whether TOI-561 b is more like an extremely hot Venus or a much more intense version of the volcanic moon Io. Additional observations with JWST at longer wavelengths could reveal important details about the atmosphere's chemical composition and help resolve the mystery of its long-term survival.

Professor Dang added: "The next big question we will seek to answer is whether TOI-561 b's atmosphere resembles Venus or an extreme version of lo. Observing the planet at longer wavelengths with JWST could give us insights and help explain how a planet this hot has held on to an atmosphere for billions of years."

Materials provided by University of Birmingham. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#365. Lykyn Review: A Mushroom Garden Worth Showing Off

If your online algorithm detects your having even the slightest interest in growing mushrooms, you will, at some point, probably see an ad for the Lykyn. It looks like something out of a science fiction movie—a clear box with theatrical lighting, fog, and a giant mushroom displayed like an otherworldly specimen.

After trying the Lykyn myself for a month, I can confirm that it really does look like that in real life, and as someone who has tried many different mushroom-growing methods, I was impressed by how well the Lykyn worked as a legitimate fruiting chamber. The thing you’ll have to decide for yourself is whether it’s worth $300 to showcase a process that lasts only a week or two.

Even though the company is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, Lykyn manufactures all fruiting chambers in Turkey and ships them straight from Istanbul for free. I worried about a delay, but my unit arrived in just 11 days, with none of the duty fees or other customs weirdness that seem to plague many overseas shipments these days. The speed was even more impressive given that I had the custom wood handle engraving add-on ($15), which puts your own text on the handle instead of “Lykyn.” (Mine had my name.)

The 13 x 13 x 15-inch unit, available in white or black plastic, arrives mostly disassembled—you will need to peel the protective film off both sides of each acrylic panel, slide them into plastic tracks on the base, add the corner posts, and fill the unit with distilled water. One panel doubles as a sliding door, and installing it was fiddly on my unit; I had to open and close it a few times to loosen it, and even now it still takes two hands to wrench it open. The Lykyn must be connected to 2.5-GHz Wi-Fi and a power source with its 5-volt USB-C cable (there is no provided brick), and the light—a little LED pad that sits on top—plugs into the base.

The only troubleshooting I had to do was with the ultrasonic humidifier, which emits an occasional cloud of fog (and is audible, but no louder than a typical humidifier), with extra humidity being pulled out by a fan. Despite my following directions, the mister produced no mist, and I kept getting notifications from the Lykyn app that the unit was out of water, despite the reservoir being visibly full. I eventually had to push the preinstalled wick farther down into its slot and “prime” the membrane on top with a little bit of water. (I later verified the Lykyn app’s humidity reading against my own hygrometer; the readings were similar within a couple of percentage points.) Other than refilling the water reservoir, the Lykyn needs no maintenance.

The Lykyn is a “smart” fruiting chamber, which means the humidity, airflow, and light schedules are controlled by settings for your specific species in the easy-to-use Lykyn app. There are also options to control these variables manually. (Note also that though the app shows the temperature, the Lykyn is not able to heat or cool.) While you can use any brand of fruiting block in the Lykyn up to 6 pounds, I tested my unit with the brand’s own 5-pound shiitake and pink oyster blocks, which came separately—no fruiting block is included with your Lykyn purchase. There are also no instructions on or with the blocks themselves, so be sure to check online for how yours needs to be prepared—some, like the shiitake, need to be taken totally out of their packaging, while others simply need an X cut into the side of the bag.

Having the block in versus out of the bag had a dramatic effect on the overall aesthetic of the Lykyn, which sat front and center in my dining room during the test. The bare shiitake block looked like a moon rock or quirky scientific specimen under the colorful lighting (which is customizable, but the default indicates the mushroom's “emotions," from green for “happy and thriving” to red for “stressed and worried”), while the black-bagged oyster mushroom block looked like … well, a black plastic bag. With the precisely controlled lighting and humidity, both mushroom blocks fruited within several days, compared with several weeks with other methods I’ve tried. This is especially impressive for shiitake, which are notoriously fussy in their requirements.

Both blocks produced about a pound of mushrooms each with their first flush, which were notably more flavorful than their grocery store brethren. However, the Lykyn is more of a hobby/decor setup rather than one designed for volume. Lykyn founder Ibrahim Berber told me the company wanted the chamber to “read more like an aquarium than an appliance.”

“The mycelium-to-fruit turn is one of the more visually alive things that can happen in a home,” he said. “Something that earns a spot on the kitchen counter, not a place to hide in a closet.”

Lykyn says most blocks should have two to three flushes, but only my shiitake block has had a second flush so far, and it was about three weeks after the first (and less voluminous), which meant most of the time I was displaying what looked like a moldy compressed-sawdust brick. Still, I love the Lykyn and absolutely plan to kit it out with a new fruiting block next time we have people over—a centerpiece you can eventually sauté is hard to resist.

#362. Parents inflicted fatal physical and sexual abuse on their baby, Warwick court hears

Michal Stasiak and Kinga Wydrzynska pleaded not guilty to 11 counts relating to death of 13-month-old Isabella Stasiak

A 13-month-old girl was subjected to physical and sexual abuse at the hands of her parents before she was fatally injured by her mother, a court has heard.

Michal Stasiak, 28, and Kinga Wydrzynska, 26, appeared at Warwick crown court on Tuesday, where they pleaded not guilty to 11 counts related to the death of Isabella Stasiak.

Wydrzynska is charged with Isabella’s murder, while both parents have been charged with one count of causing or allowing the death of a child, four counts of causing or allowing serious physical harm to a child, two counts related to the sexual assault of a child and three counts of child cruelty.

The court heard both parents were known to social services and the police.

Isabella died in hospital after sustaining a series of injuries including a fractured skull and ribs, a broken arm and a burn to her entire face, the court was told.

In her opening statement to the court on Tuesday, Annabel Darlow KC said emergency services were called to the Wydrzynska’s home in the early hours of 7 January 2025 where Isabella was found “grey and lifeless”.

Medical experts later concluded Isabella died “because she suffered injuries consistent with a forceful direct impact to her head against a hard surface”, Darlow told the court.

Darlow said Wydrzynska was the only adult present at the home when Isabella was found by police and the injuries were the “devastating culmination of systematic physical abuse”.

“The prosecution say, by the time Isabella died, there was an entrenched pattern of abuse, neglect and mistreatment of the little girl of which both parents were entirely aware,” Darlow told the court.

“Before she died, Isabella was subjected to physical and sexual abuse at the hands of both her mother and father,” Darlow added. “Neither defendant has ever provided any explanation for those injuries to their daughter.”

Jurors were told Isabella was born in December 2023 and immediately taken into the care of social services. After being placed in emergency accommodation, Isabella was looked after by foster parents for four months before she was returned to her parents in April 2024 – nine months before her death, the court was told.

Members of the jury were also told Stasiak was arrested by police for common assault after a report was made by Wydrzynska on 14 December 2024, less than a month before Isabella’s death.

Wydrzynska made repeated attempts to withdraw the report, jurors were told. However, Stasiak was placed on police bail and prohibited from living at the family home or contacting Wydrzynska.

In addition, the court was told police officers had attended the family home after a complaint was made by a neighbour in October 2024.

Officers attended the property and were only responded to when the police threatened to force entry, Darlow said. Officers spoke to the parents and saw Isabella and “did not have any immediate safeguarding concerns” after the visit, the court was told.

#358. Serbia: Tens of thousands rally ahead of elections

After two years of student-led protests, Serbia will soon vote in what observers describe as a referendum-like snap election pitting strongman Aleksandar Vucic against the student movement.

Over a hundred thousand of Serbians took to the streets in support of Serbia's protesting youth movement on Thursday, in what was the final campaign rally ahead of a pivotal election challenge to the Balkan nation's populist strongman, Aleksandar Vucic.

Crowds poured into Belgrade's central square from all directions and filled up several blocks around the area. Independent crowd-monitoring group Archive of Public Gatherings estimated about 150,000 people were at the rally.

The optimistic crowds chanted "victory" ahead of Sunday's crunch vote which comes after two years of student-led protests against Vucic. It was triggered by a 2024 collapse of a canopy at the newly renovated Novi Sad railway station which killed 16 people.

The anti-corruption movement, led by university students, has since snowballed from demands for an independent investigation into the incident, into calls for early elections.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Student List, a leaderless movement of student-backed candidates, is the veteran leader's biggest threat.

Thousands supporting it flocked to Belgrade's streets on Thursday.

"I came to support the Student List because young people finally gave me hope that it is possible to live in a normal country," 47-year-old Marija Nedeljkovic Radic told the French AFP news agency as she arrived for the students' rally.

"Really, for the first time in my life, I believe that it is possible," she said.

"We are putting an end to 14 years of humiliation, chaos and betrayal, and to decades of putting personal and party interests above yours," university professor Jugoslav Gasic, one of 250 candidates on the Student List, told the crowd who chanted "Victory, victory!"

"You deserve better, and we know how to deliver it. Students are winning!"

Vucic has dominated Serbian politics for over a decade now, either as prime minister or president of the Balkan country.

As pressure from the protests mounted, he announced a snap election last month. He has resigned as president, but many argue it is a tactical move so that he could run for prime minister.

This is now the biggest electoral challenge his party, the Serbian Progressive Party (SNS), has faced since it was elected into power in 2012.

The leader, meanwhile, has regularly accused the students movement of being a foreign-funded coup.

"Let us never forget how they tried to bring down our state. Let us always protect and defend it," Vucic said to a sports hall full of supporters on Thursday. "Let us go out and defeat them! Defeat them decisively!"

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Days before the vote, Serbian civil societies warned that conditions for "free and fair election" were lacking.

"This assessment is not based on isolated incidents, opposition claims or political disagreement with the government," a joint statement by 24 organisations said, arguing it was supported by documented findings.

Analysts warn that a close election result could lead to post-election instability, if both sides declare victory. Vucic, who controls the Serbian police, has said he would quash any unrest after the vote.

While several minor parties are also in the race for premiership, observers say that there is a referendum-like atmosphere between Vucic and the students' movement which could sideline other parties in the 250-member parliament.
