#357. Common drugs may help dangerous viruses spread through the body

Medications are designed to treat diseases and relieve symptoms, but some can also produce unintended effects that put patients at risk. While side effects are common with many treatments, researchers have identified a particularly concerning possibility involving drugs used to control inflammation.

Scientists at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU) have discovered that a widely used class of anti-inflammatory medications may make it easier for certain viruses to infect cells and spread throughout the body.

"We have found a surprising and potentially serious side effect of a class of anti-inflammatory drugs. They can help viruses spread more easily in the body," says Denis Kainov, a professor at the Department of Clinical and Molecular Medicine at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

Common Anti-Inflammatory Drugs May Help Viruses Spread

The medications at the center of the findings belong to a class called Janus kinase inhibitors, or JAKs. These drugs are widely prescribed to control inflammation, but their effects on the immune system may also leave the body more vulnerable to viral infections.

"Among the viruses that more easily affect the body with the help of these drugs are Rift Valley fever virus, influenza A, adenovirus and SARS-CoV-2, the virus that causes COVID-19," Kainov says.

These viruses can cause significant illness, making any treatment that potentially increases their ability to spread a serious concern.

Why Arthritis Drugs Can Weaken Antiviral Defenses

In a new study published in NAR Molecular Medicine, Kainov and his colleagues investigated how JAK inhibitors influence the body's ability to defend itself against viruses.

"JAK inhibitors are often prescribed for autoimmune diseases and inflammatory conditions such as rheumatoid arthritis," says Erlend Ravlo, a PhD research fellow at NTNU and first author of the article.

Although these medications can be highly effective at reducing inflammation, their benefits come with an important trade-off. By suppressing certain immune responses, they can also interfere with the mechanisms that normally protect the body from viral infections.

"In particular, the drugs suppress an important immune signalling pathway that helps protect healthy cells from viral attacks," Ravlo said.

This pathway plays a central role in coordinating the body's response when viruses attempt to infect healthy tissue.

Scientists Discover How JAK Inhibitors Disrupt Immune Protection

The research team found that JAK inhibitors, including the medication baricitinib, can reduce the activity of genes involved in fighting viral infections.

"These genes play a crucial role in the body's response to viral infections," said Aleksandr Ianevski, an NTNU researcher and coauthor of the new paper.

When JAK inhibitors interfere with this immune signaling system, they weaken an important layer of antiviral protection. Without the usual defensive response, viruses may find it easier to establish an infection and spread between cells.

To investigate these effects, the scientists combined advanced virology methods with gene expression analysis and organoid technology. Their experiments included cells from the lungs, eyes and brain, along with miniature organ models grown in the laboratory.

These approaches allowed the team to examine how the drugs affect antiviral defenses across different types of human tissue.

"Although JAK inhibitors are effective in the treatment of inflammation, this shows that they can pose a hidden risk for patients who have latent or active viral infections," says Ravlo.

What the Findings Mean for Patients and Future Treatments

The results highlight the importance of carefully considering the risks associated with JAK inhibitors, particularly when patients may already have a viral infection or when outbreaks are occurring.

Researchers suggest that healthcare professionals should exercise caution when prescribing these medications during periods of increased viral transmission.

However, the same immune-suppressing properties that create potential risks for patients could also offer advantages in certain research settings. Under controlled laboratory conditions, these effects may help scientists develop vaccines or evaluate potential antiviral medications.

The researchers hope their findings will encourage greater awareness of how JAK inhibitors influence the immune system and support further investigations into their safest and most effective uses.

"We recommend further studies to better understand how the drugs can be used optimally, especially during virus outbreaks or pandemics," says Ianevski.

Professor Magnar Bjørås of the Department of Clinical and Molecular Medicine provided significant scientific expertise and secured most of the funding for the research. Additional financial support came from the Central Norway Regional Health Authority, the South-Eastern Norway Regional Health Authority, the Research Council of Norway and the Swedish Research Council.

#356. LG B6 OLED TV Review (2026)

It’s amazing what can happen in just one year. The LG B6 is the cheapest LG OLED model around (priced lower than the LG C6 and LG G6), but it also has some stiff competition. New art televisions like the Amazon Ember Artline (which costs under $1,000) use a matte display that renders masterpiece paintings in convincingly realistic glory. RGB televisions like the LG Micro RGB Evo and those from Samsung and Hisense have also burst onto the market, making a convincing argument for choosing an RGB screen over an OLED one.

Last year around this time, we reviewed the LG B5 and ranked it a solid 8/10, but there was less competition. The LG B6 is still a good budget OLED TV, it’s just beset on all sides by better, brighter options, including from LG’s own LG C6.

The first thing that struck me while setting up the LG B6 is that it’s incredibly thin—just 1.8 inches at the base and about a quarter-inch at the top and around most of the bezel. The OLED panel doesn’t need any backlighting like most LED or RGB televisions, so it can be extra thin. The lower portion of the TV is thicker because of the four HDMI ports (all of which support a 120-Hz refresh rate), an optical audio out, two USB 2.0 ports, and one LAN port. The TV only supports Wi-Fi 5, certainly not the latest standard.

The B6 comes with two legs that are easy to install (two screws each), though I generally prefer a single pedestal stand design. The 65-inch model I tested fit comfortably on two 38-inch IKEA SPIKSMED TV benches set side by side that measure 76 inches long.

Like all LG TVs, setup involves LG’s own WebOS interface or ThinQ app, not built-in Google TV or Fire TV. It was easy to complete the setup using the LG ThinQ app on my iPhone—in minutes, I was loading my usual assortment of apps like Prime Video, Disney+ and Apple TV+.

The B6 is packed with features too. A few of my favorites include a gaming mode that helps improve low latency gaming, AI picture enhancements that actually work and make a discernible difference, an artwork mode called LG Gallery+ that costs $5 per month, and an LG music app in case you don’t like Spotify or other music apps. It also has settings for audio dialogue sync, similar to what you can do with powered speakers like the Klipsch The Fives II. A desktop mode means you can mirror your laptop screen and even run a Gmail app.

I have mixed feelings about WebOS, though. My main gripe is that you cannot disable the pointer on the LG remote, and it jumps around a lot. When you press the center dial on the remote, it turns off temporarily as though LG user interface designers are admitting it isn’t that great. I also have mixed feelings about the remote—it’s hard to use in the dark by feel. The Home button is offset so it’s hard to find, and shortcuts for Netflix and other apps are situated at the bottom, but not backlit.

Based on the pricing, it’s obvious LG has positioned the B6 as the entry-level OLED in their line-up. That became crystal clear once I started testing movies and TV shows.

In the official specs, LG only lists “wide color gamut” and not a percentage for the specific BT.2020 spec, nor a specific brightness rating. This is where the benchmark testing comes in handy, and what I tested right away.

One win for the B6 is blacks and grays, which have great texture and clarity, a surprise given how many midrange televisions make dark, shadowy scenes look washed out. Scenes from Awake and The Creator, very dark movies, were clear and detailed, after tweaking a few picture quality settings (namely changing to Vivid mode).

Contrast and brightness are more of a mixed bag. I watched House of the Dragon and Game of Thrones (yes, the infamously dark season eight, episode three) to stress test these specs, and the B6 struggled. On better TVs (and home theater projectors), you can see trees in the background of a scene where a character emerges on a frozen lake. On the B6, they are completely black—e.g., a contrast problem.

Color saturation and luminance could be better too, especially compared to RGB TVs I’ve tested recently. The Super Mario Galaxy Movie, with its various blooming effects, wasn’t exactly terrible looking, but certainly not as crisp as on something like the LG Micro RGB Evo. (The less technicolor The End of Oak Street fared better.) During a demo reel benchmark, green grass by a fence in winter looked a bit too brown for my taste (again, color saturation issue), but skin tone variation was surprisingly good.

LG is not the first brand I think of for playing video games (it’s Sony), but the B6 deserves some extra credit here. The 120-Hz refresh rate screen does its job, and made all my movements playing Crimson Desert on PC crisp and responsive. Resident Evil: Requiem looked almost photo realistic, with gleaming statues and surprisingly good color quality, even zooming in on crumpled leaves.

Watching sports and news didn’t yield any surprises—a Baltimore Orioles baseball game looked rich and textured, but not very bright.

LG B6 may be an entry-level OLED, but, like an entry-level BMW, it has a lot of the bells and whistles of a flagship, with plenty of ports, features, and premium finishes. My main issue with it is brightness and contrast, both could be better. And LG’s WebOS is still not my favorite interface in the world.

The main issue might actually be price. At the time of writing, the LG B6 is on sale for $1,300. The superior midrange LG C6 is also on sale for $1,500. That’s only $200 more for a much better, brighter TV (both 65-inches). That’s not even discussing buying an older (but still brighter) model like the LG C5 (which I found on sale for less than the B6). It all depends on how much you want to spend.

#355. We found the best robot vacuums for every floor, budget, and level of laziness

There's definitely something to be said for the crisp handheld control of a good cordless stick vacuum. But if you don't consider cleaning cathartic, the best robot vacuums can cross that chore off your to-do list for good.

And after testing more than 40 robot vacuums on my own personal floors (my apartment is very clean), I can confidently curate a handful of robot vacuum recommendations that'll make your life easier — and fit your budget. Here are the best robot vacuums to buy in 2026.

I test new robot vacuums on a regular basis and compare them to the top performers from previous months, with all my evaluations taking place in my own home on various rugs, tile, and hardwood floors. As I test each robot vacuum and tweak its settings for optimal performance, I keep notes on its overall suction power vigor, scrubbing efficacy (if it's a robot vacuum and mop combo), pet hair pickup, the convenience of its automatic emptying and self-washing mopping pads, accuracy of smart mapping and small obstacle avoidance, and the overall user experience. But the final boss here is cost efficiency — I'll be the first one to point out when a cheap robot vacuum actually works well for the price, or when a high-end robot vacuum is actually worth the splurge or not.

Read my full review of the Dreame X60 Max Ultra Complete.

The Dreame X60 Max Ultra Complete is an incredibly well-rounded premium robot vacuum that would excel in a variety of homes. Its market-leading 35,000 suction power and livestream pet camera make it the top contender for homes with pets. Its dual spinning mopping pads are more precise around edges than a flat mop or a roller mop, speaking to anyone who'd cringe watching a robot vacuum completely ignore a corner or wall edge. (Hi, I'm "anyone.")

Measuring just over three inches tall, it's also easily the best slim robot vacuum out there — ideal for homes with low-clearance furniture, or expectations for cleaning under cabinets.

It happened: The Roborock Saros 10R has finally been dethroned as the best robot vacuum for corner mopping. Dreame should enjoy its reign while it can before Roborock's 2026 flagship comes out this spring: The Saros 20 Sonic matches the X60 Max Ultra Complete's suction power and introduces an interesting new method of edge mopping. The conversation about the best robot vacuum of the year is about to get interesting.

After Dreame's release of a flagship roller mop robot vacuum in September, I appreciate its return to dual rotating mopping pads in its CES 2026 star, the X60 Max Ultra Complete. The double pad design just offers so much more precision along edges and in 90-degree angles. Like the Roborock Saros 10R, I've watched the X60 Max Ultra Complete scoot the vacuum's circular body as close to a wall as possible, then extend a spinning mop right up against the wall, along table legs, or against kitchen counter edges. At just 3.1 inches tall, the X60 Max Ultra can even reach the sticky floor under the overhang of my fridge.

The Dreame X60 Max Ultra Complete's mopping capabilities are intensified by the use of hot water during mopping. (The Saros 10R washes its mops with hot water, but doesn't actively mop with hot water.) That has been key in melting stubborn dried stains on hard floors, like the massive dried puddle of dirty snow slush and salt that was accumulating for days near my front door. Water stains were a struggle for most 2-in-1 vacuums I've tested, but the X60 Max Ultra Complete left the hardwood gleaming.

The X60 Max Ultra Complete's carpet performance has also stood out to me. It didn't flinch at the war zone of spilled sprinkles and cat hair that I created on a furry high-pile rug, leaving behind just a few minuscule sprinkles and a few ultra-fine hairs. Its pickup rate for rice, powdered sugar, and kitty litter on hardwood and tile was just as reliable, though bits of debris hiding near the lip of my kitchen Ruggable were missed on multiple occasions. I can just pull the rug back an inch in that case, though such intense suction power shouldn't get tripped up by such a negligible change in plane.

Read my full review of the Narwal Flow 2.

The Narwal Flow 2 is the robot vacuum to splurge on if you take the cleanliness of your hard floors seriously — it takes a certain confidence in cleanliness to walk around in bare feet at home. Between the sturdy XL roller and hot water mopping, the Flow 2 has the elbow grease to soak up large liquid spills and melt away sticky or greasy droplets without smearing.

If your household is prone to spills, perhaps from kids, pets, or frequent guests, the Flow 2's accurate obstacle and mess detection technology will come to the rescue for those big in-the-moment messes.

If you've sworn off mopping robot vacuums after a cheap one streaked your floor with dirty water, a roller mop robot vacuum should be your next move. The Narwal Flow 2 is my top recommendation halfway through 2026, mostly because it never really missed a spot in the several months that I was testing it.

Roller mop robot vacuums mitigate smearing in a few ways. The bath towel-like material is already thick and absorbent, and constantly rinses itself to keep the spill from being dragged across a clean floor. If the roller gets too soiled during a bigger cleanup, the Flow 2 automatically returns to the dock mid-cleaning to wash the mop with hot water. The Flow 2's roller actually has flat sides like a conveyor belt, which Narwal says gets more direct surface coverage than the sliver of a cylindrical roller mop hitting the floor at any given point.

In my testing, the Flow 2 consistently mopped up spills of various consistencies, from true liquids like red wine and milk to sludgier splatters like pancake batter, chunky pasta sauce, and most impressively, globs of dried syrup. The Flow 2 is one of just a few robot vacuums that mop with hot water, making it able to dissolve the syrup without leaving the floor sticky. Hot water loosens the bonds that make grease slimy and hard to lift off hard surfaces with force alone, so the Flow 2 handles oily or greasy liquids better than robots mopping with room temperature water. During dry vacuuming, the Flow 2 did a commendable job with cat hair and small debris on rugs and kibble, cat litter, dirt, and crushed dried flowers on hard floors. I appreciate that the app allows up to three cleaning passes in the same session.

The Flow 2 is also such a thorough cleaner simply because it knows what kind of mess it's dealing with — its AI spill detection is the most intuitive I've seen in 2026. The Flow 2 snaps a "before" photo when it approaches a mess that's bigger than a few crumbs or a single droplet. After cleaning the rest of the area, the Flow 2 comes back to the dirty area for intensive zone cleaning. My Narwal app was also full of photos of extension cords, phone chargers, shoes, and random cat toys that the Flow 2 saw in my apartment while cleaning, even if the object wasn't directly in the way. No one has time to tidy up before sending their robot vacuum out every single day, so it's comforting to see such direct proof that the Flow 2 is, in fact, reacting to obstacles in real time.

The Eufy C28 is an awesome find for shoppers who can spend more than a few hundred bucks, but don't want to toe the $1,000 line (which also puts it in great gift territory). Its roller mop is targeted toward the cleanup of large liquid spills, easily making the C28 the best budget robot vacuum mop combo for fast-paced homes and in-the-moment cleanup. The AI-powered small obstacle avoidance will come in handy if you don't always have time to tidy up before sending the vacuum out.

This particular Eufy robovac also has some of the best battery life on the market (even on its highest suction setting), making it an even easier choice for homes with lots of square footage or multiple stories.

Before the Eufy C28 came out in February 2026, a budget in the $600 to $800 range meant you were strictly relegated to hybrid robot vacuums with dual spinning mopping pads. Though their precision has its advantages, the thin pads aren't overly absorbent. The C28 finally answers the call of spill-prone households who need something more heavy-duty (and less likely to smear). The HydroJet system rinses the mop nine times per second as it's mopping to prevent smearing.

The Eufy C28 nearly doubles the 8,000 Pa suction power seen in the Eufy X10 Pro Omni (my budget mopping pick for the past two years). I've been consistently wowed by the C28 fuzz and debris pickup on various rug piles. From catnip flakes to tufts of dryer lint to handfuls of crushed chips, I really had to squint to find a few tiny pieces that the C28 left behind. The Eufy C28 gets bonus points for its slow, deliberate vacuuming when spot cleaning the tricky area where a rug changes to hardwood — its crumb pickup around rug edges has actually been more reliable than premium Roborock and Dreame models.

Its performance on hardwood and tile has been even more stellar. The damp roller presses down firmly to wipe up dust and pet hair on hardwood that would stick to the floor during regular vacuuming. The laser on my Dyson PencilVac proved that the C28 left the floor around both litter boxes essentially spotless on multiple occasions.

If you're set on spending less than $300 on a robot vacuum, there are virtually no reasons to choose any model other than the 3i G10+. Between its ability to maneuver around clutter and its dust-compacting, space-saving design, the 3i G10+ is the best robot vacuum for small spaces with limited floor storage.

This niche pick also eclipses any other cheap robot vacuum in the suction power department. While it's a strong budget pick for homes with pets or a lot of carpeting, its basic mopping skills should rule it out for homes with intense mopping needs.

This was the first robot vacuum I've ever tested from 3i, and it became one of my most recommended robot vacuums of the year. While its dust-compacting element is cool because it saves you the space a self-empty dock would take up, it's hardly the G10+'s only monumental feature.

For such an inexpensive robot vacuum, I was thoroughly impressed by the G10+'s perceptive navigation around my apartment. Its initial mapping run of the rooms was almost 100 percent accurate — it even recognized beds, credenzas, and toilets all on its own, and automatically remembered where all of the rugs were. The 3i G10+ is also an incredibly rare budget option with small obstacle avoidance that can steer clear of cords or socks, plus pet waste (I tested this with a pile of brown sugar). But small obstacle avoidance isn't the only high-end feature 3i squeezed in here — there's also a livestream pet camera. That was firmly a $1,000+ robot vacuum feature before the G10+ hit the scene.

All of that brainpower isn't just fluff, either. The G10+ is a beast at cleaning. Its 18,000 Pa of suction power is super high for this price range, and you can tell that the G10+ is working hard as it moves extra meticulously when carpet is sensed, or during zone cleaning.

Read my initial review of the Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow.

The Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow is the most reliable roller mop robot vacuum you'll find with a budget capped at $1,000. Its sturdy self-rinsing roller mop and flawless small obstacle avoidance will be great to have on call in hectic households, whether that's to deal with spills or clean without running over random toys or socks on the floor.

The Curv 2 Flow's rounded, white self-empty dock is shorter and cuter than most, making it an easy option if you're limited on floor space.

The Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow was the first to convince me that not all roller mops are a wash at edge mopping. When it senses a wall, the Curv 2 Flow's roller pops out past the vacuum's body to mop where the circular body wouldn't fit well otherwise. The roller rescinds pretty early when the Curv 2 Flow has to change directions in a 90-degree angle. So while true corner mopping isn't the most precise, the Curv 2 Flow was able to scrub closely along my baseboards and the tiled wall around my bathtub.

If you want to talk precision, the Curv 2 Flow's obstacle avoidance is some of the best I've ever experienced. In the few months that I had the Curv 2 Flow at home, we didn't have a single incident with random junk on the floor: Extension cords, socks and shoes, the cats' crinkle balls, and even skinny iPhone chargers hanging out of the wall (those are usually a problem at least once for most premium robot vacuums). I could see each correctly-identified obstacle on the map in the Roborock app.

The Curv 2 Flow was a pro at preventing smearing when cleaning up splatters of liquid as well as thick condiments. The roller is constantly rinsing itself with clean water as it's mopping, but if it gets too soiled, the Curv 2 Flow automatically returns to the dock mid-cleaning queue to wash the mop with hot water. Its fluffy bath towel texture is also more absorbent than the cloth on spinning mopping pads in general. I entrusted it to tackle a variety of liquids in my apartment, from wine and Bloody Mary mix, to chunky queso, to a thick pudge of leave-in conditioner that exploded on my bathroom floor. Those spills would have taken a ton of paper towels, but the Curv 2 Flow wiped each up without leaving residue behind.

Its 20,000 Pa suction sounds mediocre compared to the 30,000 Pa to 35,000 Pa of other 2026 flagship vacuums, but the difference in pickup rate has been negligible in my testing. It has consistently picked up 95 to 97 percent of various kitchen and snack crumbs, layers of cat hair as well as larger tufts, long human hair, and powders like pancake mix on both hard floors and multiple rug piles. The Curv 2 Flow has been such a beast at keeping up with the crystal cat litter that gets tracked onto my bathroom floor. Any dust specks or fine hairs that are too small to be sucked up get lapped up by the damp roller.

The percentage of homes that would benefit from having both a robot vacuum and a manual stick vacuum is probably 100 percent. In a perfect world, right? If you don't have the space, budget, or patience for that, the Eufy E20 3-in-1 is a really strategic way to maximize a mid-range budget. The E20 would have serious daily usability if your idea of a clean home goes past the floors to crevices like windowsills, car seats, staircases, or the pit of despair between couch cushions.

In a sea of $1,500+ vacuums, Eufy was genius to take the "bang for your buck" approach with its CES 2025 contender. At $649.99, the Eufy E20 3-in-1 isn't all that steep for a self-emptying, smart mapping robot vacuum, let alone the bonus cordless vacuum it brings along. Here, 3-in-1 refers to the robot vacuum, cordless stick vacuum, and handheld modes. Switching between these modes just involves pulling the motor out of the robot vacuum body and clicking it onto one of the included attachments.

The Eufy E20 definitely isn't the most powerful robot vacuum I've tested, nor is it as powerful as my favorite Dyson vacuum, which I should note has an identical price tag. We already know that its real power lies in its range, but for the value, its robotic and handheld cleaning capabilities would actually suffice for any casual cleaner. At 8,000 Pa, it vacuums with the same amount of suction power as 2024's Eufy X10 Pro Omni, which also impressed me during testing. Its pickup rate of fine debris along edges and long hairs on rugs could be better, but the E20 reliably took care of all visible crumbs and loose tufts of pet hair on most rugs, hardwood, and tile.

While the Eufy E20 can avoid items like toys over 15 millimeters or pet food bowls, its obstacle avoidance doesn't extend to flatter items like cords or socks. Its navigation to specific rooms and zones, however, was nearly perfect in my apartment. It surveyed the three bedrooms and bathrooms extremely accurately on the first try, and after I added an extra border or two, the E20 reliably made its way to selected spots on a daily basis — and without getting stuck on a bath mat or under a bed.

This Roborock robot vacuum is ideal for those who don't want to spend much more than $500, but who also don't want to settle for the most basic cleaning possible. This 2025 entry-level model is the rare cheap robot vacuum and mop combo with legitimate pressurized scrubbing — a nice upgrade for hardwood floors or entryways with high foot (or paw) traffic. Its self-empty dock barely takes up two feet of floor space, and its noise level is quite low, making it a great robot vacuum for small spaces.

Unlike iRobot, Roborock has always been a brand that gives ample attention to the budget-friendly half of its product line. So it's saying something that I think the Roborock Q10 S5+ is the best bang for your buck near $500 that Roborock has whipped up in a long time. It has reliably completed all missions in my apartment for weeks, and isn't nearly as obnoxious when self-emptying as most other robot vacuums I've tested — it barely reaches 70 decibels, which isn't much louder than competitors while they're just cleaning.

The Roborock Q8 Max+ has probably popped up in your research for the best robot vacuum and mop combo on a budget. Roborock itself says that the Q10 S5+ is the direct successor of that model, and there's no reason not to upgrade: The Q10 S5+ nearly doubles the suction power — 10,000 Pa versus the Q8 Max+'s 5,500 Pa — which makes it just as powerful as the high-end Roborock Qrevo Master that I loved in 2024. While I do prefer the spinning mopping pad design found on more premium Roborocks, the forceful vibrations of the Q10 S5+'s singular mopping pad provide more thorough scrubbing than many cheap 2-in-1 vacuums. The Q10 S5+ successfully wiped up a variety of spills and splatters in my kitchen and near the cat bowls, though it doesn't mop very close to edges.

Roborock has consistently shown me the most user-friendly app experience. The Q10 S5+ supports the same color-coded home map with the same straightforward room and zone cleaning options, and never once got lost in my apartment when navigating to a particular spot. Unlike the Shark Matrix Plus 2-in-1 (which was previously in this spot on our list), the Q10 S5+ automatically lifts the mop when carpets and rugs are detected. Small obstacle avoidance is also a bonus at this price point.

UPDATE: iRobot filed for bankruptcy on Dec. 15, 2025, and has been acquired by Roomba manufacturer Picea. While Roombas are still available to buy as usual, I would recommend deprioritizing a Roomba in your robot vacuum search. There are robot vacuums with better features at better prices out there, and with iRobot's rocky future now layered in, even the best Roomba vacuums aren't worth it.

If you absolutely must have the iRobot name under your roof, the Roomba 105 series vacuums are iRobot's most advanced and fairly-priced entry-level robot vacuums to date. They'd be ideal for the crowd who is totally fine with settling for a not-amazing-but-decent clean if it affordably lightens their chore load. It'd be a fine addition to an apartment or small space with low foot traffic and relatively simple layouts.

It's also nice that you can customize your choice to the features you need in your house — and how much you want to spend. There are four versions of the Roomba 105: a standalone mopping and non-mopping version, and a self-emptying mopping and non-mopping version. They all have huge discounts quite often, dropping both standalone models below $200 and both self-emptying models below $300.

To be real, any random Roomba you could pick out is probably being upstaged by a different brand's robot vacuum with the same price tag. But if you insist on trying a Roomba, the 2025 Roomba 105 series vacuums definitely offer better bang for your buck over any of the low-budget Roombas.

The most impactful upgrade from the i5 to the 105 lies in suction power: The Roomba 105 offers 60 times the suction of 600 series Roombas like the Roomba 694 or Roomba 675, while the Roomba i5 offers just 10 times the power of the 600 series. This translates to 7,000 Pa in suction power for the 105. On my apartment's hard floors, the 105 had a commendable track record for the price — it sucked up most tracked kitty litter and spilled protein powder, and grabbed all but one or two heavy pieces of dry cat food. On rugs, most loose crumbs and visible fuzzies were taken care of, though matted-down cat fur required a second pass from a stick vacuum.

iRobot also finally shifted to LiDAR-based smart mapping with its 2025 Roombas, which ultimately just means faster, more precise room mapping. The Roomba 105 successfully made it to the selected rooms and zones in my apartment 95 percent of the time, despite the new Roomba Home app being significantly more basic than the old one.

July 2026: I added the Narwal Flow 2 as the new pick for best roller mop robot vacuum overall, bumping the Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow to the best roller mop robot vacuum under $1,000. The Narwal Flow 2 has stronger suction power and more advanced AI spill detection.

March 2026: I added the Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow as the best roller mop robot vacuum, replacing the Dreame Aqua10 Ultra Roller. Though the Dreame has more powerful suction on paper, the Curv 2 Flow demonstrated better edge mopping and comparable vacuuming. I also replaced the Eufy X10 Pro Omni with the Eufy C28 and shifted it to my budget roller mop pick. The X10 Pro Omni is two years old at this point, and the new C28 offers more advanced cleaning and obstacle avoidance.

January 2026: I added the Dreame X60 Max Ultra Complete as the best robot vacuum right now and shifted the Aqua10 Ultra to the best roller mop robot vacuum. The Roborock Saros 10R, which was listed as the best option for corner cleaning, has been removed from the list. It costs nearly the same amount as the X60 Max Ultra Complete, which has near-identical corner mopping abilities and much stronger suction power.

December 2025: I added a disclaimer to the pick for best Roomba value in light of iRobot's bankruptcy news.

October 2025: I added the Dreame Aqua10 Ultra Roller as the best robot vacuum right now and shifted the Roborock Saros 10R (previously named the best robot vacuum right now) to the option for best corner cleaning.

July 2025: I removed the Shark Matrix Plus 2-in-1 robot vacuum from this guide. While it was a solid budget mopping robot vacuum for its first year or so on the market, it's now a little bit too basic compared to other newer budget robot vacuums.

Most robot vacuum run times range between 120 and 180 minutes, which translates to about 500 and 2,600 square feet covered on one charge. Some ultra-efficient models can clock over 200 minutes on one charge, which would only really matter in homes exceeding 3,000 square feet. Larger spaces or homes with multiple stories obviously require more time to clean, and how much you prioritize battery life depends on how annoyed you'll be if your robot vac has to take a time out during its queue to charge.

You'll notice that we left Dyson's robot vacuums off of this list. We've tested the Dyson 360 Vis Nav robot vacuum and the Dyson Spot+Scrub Ai robot vacuum and were not impressed with either. Though we love Dyson's stick vacuums, we think there are other brands making better robot vacuums at better prices.

I've been testing robot vacuums for Mashable since 2019 — most recently in a three-bedroom, two-cat apartment.

All of these vacuums were tested for at least four weeks. I put them through a series of standardized tests that cover the four main pillars of robot vacuum competence, while also considering overall bang for your buck.

Each vacuum tackles various spills, debris, levels of pet hair, and floor types. I also tested hybrid vacuum and mop combo models on various stains and spills.

I keep tabs on navigational accuracy when it comes to smart mapping my home, finding specific rooms and zones, and identifying small obstacles using LiDAR or cameras.

Your new robot vacuum should be a polite houseguest. I make sure each vacuum isn't too loud, an eyesore, or generally a pain to use.

Many robot vacuums automate their own dust bin emptying, mopping pad washing, and more — the more self-sufficient they are, the better.

Her ever-evolving experience in these categories helps her make thoughtful recommendations for how to spend your money during shopping holidays like Black Friday, which Leah has been covering for Mashable since 2017.

#354. Rosebank oilfield would not breach UK emission targets, say government insiders

Emissions from exported oil would not count against carbon budget, insiders say – reasoning likely to draw dismay from climate campaigners

The giant Rosebank oilfield can be approved without breaching the UK’s emission targets, according to government insiders, in a boost to those in the cabinet who are pushing for it to be given the go-ahead.

The Guardian revealed last week that Miatta Fahnbulleh, the energy secretary, was leaning towards rejecting the project, but her position has put her at loggerheads with cabinet colleagues, including the chancellor, John Healey, who are lobbying for an approval.

Government insiders have told the Guardian the project can be approved on a strict reading of Britain’s climate obligations, in part because once the oil is exported, the emissions from burning it no longer count against domestic targets. “Miatta has more latitude than you might think,” said one.

A decision to approve the oilfield would be met with dismay from climate campaigners and some Labour backbenchers who view it as a harmful step away from prioritising net zero – and may see the shift of emissions from one country to another as a trivial distinction.

On Tuesday, Greenpeace and Friends of the Earth wrote to the prime minister urging him to consider the cumulative impacts of big decisions on North Sea drilling, Heathrow expansion and grants for heat pumps – rather than viewing each in isolation.

But sources say the Treasury has already accounted for billions of pounds’ worth of tax revenue from Rosebank and so would be faced with a shortfall should it be rejected.

“The climate case against was never the direct emissions,” one source said. “Instead it was the leadership position … the power of a G7 producer seeking to put that into action is the climate point, not the direct emissions.”

Greg Muttitt, a climate researcher at University College, London, said the government was wrong to approve the Jackdaw gasfield last week and would be wrong to approve Rosebank, which would generate around seven times the emissions.

But he added: “It is true that the UK government is not going to find itself in any international court if it approves Rosebank. Approving it is not inconsistent with what the UK has committed to firmly, because most of the oil will be exported and so will count towards other countries’ emissions.”

Fahnbulleh has been forced to make a decision over Rosebank because of a legal judgment that the previous environmental consent was incorrectly given by the Conservative government at the time.

A judge ruled in 2024 that ministers should have accounted for emissions not just from drilling for oil at Rosebank, but also from those which will be generated by burning it.

Given most of that oil will be burned abroad, however, the resulting emissions will not count against the UK’s carbon budgets. Experts estimate that Rosebank could emit 1m additional tonnes of carbon dioxide by 2042, against an overall limit of 90m tonnes.

Fahnbulleh is under an obligation to take into account Britain’s commitment under the Paris agreement to take action to prevent global temperatures rising more than 1.5C.

In her decision to approve the Jackdaw scheme last week, however, she noted Jackdaw would account for about 0.01% of global greenhouse gas emissions created by the oil and gas industry in 2035.

Some experts believe Rosebank could be approved without meaningfully adding to global temperature changes. But others insist that any new oil and gasfields are harmful to the goal of staying within safe carbon budgets.

Downing Street insiders insist the decision rests with Fahnbulleh, rather than with Andy Burnham. However supportive ministers have interpreted the prime minister’s promise to take a “pragmatic” approach to oil and gas as a signal he would support giving the green light to Rosebank.

He has also talked of “reindustrialisation” in a speech in Motherwell, North Lanarkshire, this month, language often associated in Scotland with the North Sea. Scottish Labour MPs are believed to be overwhelmingly in favour of approving the oilfield.

Fahnbulleh told the Guardian last week that while she had approved the Jackdaw gasfield on the grounds of national energy security, the same concerns did not necessarily apply to Rosebank.

Her allies say she is leaning away from approving it because the climate damage it would create, while arguably not enough to violate legal limits, would not be overriden by the potential benefits, which those who support its approval argue includes tax take, jobs and investment.

In recent days, however, people close to the chancellor have said he wants Fahnbulleh to approve the project.

The Guardian has learned that part of the reason for this is the Treasury has already accounted for the tax benefits, which Adura, the company operating the field, estimate would be £500m a year by the end of this parliament.

A spokesperson for the Department for Energy Security and Net Zero said: “Any decision will be made through the quasi-judicial process, taking into account all relevant evidence, including environmental assessments and public representations received as part of the regulatory process.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#353. Moderna shares plunge after cancer vaccine data leaks — despite positive results

Moderna shares dropped as much as 25% on Thursday following the leak of partial data on its closely watched cancer vaccine developed with Merck.

Shares of both drugmakers fell even though the data, released in full later Thursday following the leak, appeared meaningful for patients and positive relative to Wall Street expectations.

The personalized vaccine, known as intismeran autogene, paired with Merck's immunotherapy Keytruda reduced the risk of recurrence or death in certain melanoma patients by 42% compared with Keytruda alone.

The data also showed that the shot along with Keytruda reduced the risk of cancer spreading to distant parts of the body or death by 38%, compared with just the immunotherapy.

The companies said about 82% of patients who received the personalized cancer vaccine alongside Keytruda were alive without their melanoma returning at two years after receiving the shot, compared with 70% of those who received Keytruda alone.

"This is a huge development for our patients," said Dr. Hussein Tawbi, a melanoma expert and trial investigator at the University of Texas MD ​Anderson Cancer Center, about the results.

He said that the added benefit of the vaccine also comes with much milder toxicity, or side effects like injection-site pain, compared to other cancer treatments.

The detailed phase 3 trial results were set to be presented at the European Society for Medical Oncology conference this weekend.

"Whenever you put a therapy on top of something that works really well, like Keytruda, sometimes it's harder to show additional benefit. We have no problems of showing additional benefit here," Dr. Dean Li, Merck Research Laboratories president, said in an interview ahead of the results.

The benefit appeared even larger after the six-month vaccine regimen, with a separate analysis showing a 58% reduction in risk of recurrence or death. The companies say it is still too early to determine whether the treatment helps patients live longer overall.

Several analysts acknowledged investors' elevated expectations ahead of the release, but noted that the data is overall positive.

"We assert this relative reduction in recurrence/death is clinically meaningful," BMO Capital Markets analyst Evan Seigerman said in a research note on Thursday following the leak. "Such a [stock] reaction likely reflects inflated expectations into the full data release, rather than a true indictment on the efficacy of the combo therapy."

Oncologists and investors see the targeted vaccine as a possible breakthrough treatment, with the potential to be used to treat other forms of cancer in the future. Merck and Moderna are studying the vaccine in several trials across other tumors, such as non-small cell lung cancer, bladder cancer and renal cell carcinoma.

But Li acknowledged the companies have more work to show the product can be effective across tumor types.

"I can dream of creating a log cabin, but we got some wood chops, and we've just knocked down one tree," Li said. "Knocking one tree does not give you a cabin or a village."

For now, the vaccine is not far from potentially entering the market for the first time and reaching melanoma patients. In an interview ahead of the results, Moderna CEO Stéphane Bancel said the company is finishing a filing with regulators to get the drug fully approved next year.

"It's a very, very happy moment for the field, for patients," he said.

The shot is designed to target the specific mutations in each patient's tumor rather than using a one-size-fits-all approach. The idea is to train the immune system to recognize and kill those unique cancer markers and then pair that response with Keytruda, which helps the body attack the cancer more effectively.

It could also offer Moderna a critical additional commercial product in its pipeline, following vaccines for Covid-19, the flu and respiratory syncytial virus.

In a research note Thursday, UBS analysts said "this is overall a strong data set" and gives more confidence in upcoming studies of the vaccine in kidney and bladder cancers. They added the stock reaction may be due more to massive expectations than disappointing data.

Against the backdrop of those high expectations, Moderna shares have spiked more than 530% year-to-date as of Thursday afternoon.

Shares of Merck, a much larger drugmaker, fell by about 1% on Thursday. The company's stock has climbed nearly 41% this year.

Since the vaccine is personalized, the complicated manufacturing process can take six weeks. But Merck CEO Robert Davis told CNBC last week that the company believes it can shorten that timeline in the future, potentially allowing the shot to reach more patients more quickly.

#349. Embarrassing name? How we live with names we don't like

What happens when your name invokes laughter or ridicule? Research points to negative life outcomes, but some people learn to embrace unfortunate names.

As a child growing up in Australia, Louise Pain hated her surname.

"People used to tease me; I was called things like 'Pain in the a*s,'" she told DW. "I remember actively hating my surname when I was a child and wanting to change it."

The jokes have followed her throughout her life, even after she moved to Germany. But one thing did eventually change: Pain's feelings about her own name.

Names are not only a fundamental part of how we identify ourselves — they can, beyond teasing, also influence how other people treat us. So, what happens if we dislike our own name, or if it is perceived negatively by those around us?

"A person's name is vital to their sense of identity," psychoanalyst Mikita Brottman told DW. "When someone has an embarrassing name, that embarrassment can be incorporated into their identity."

Research on name bias has linked negatively perceived names with romantic rejection,as well as lower educational outcomes, lower self-esteem and more frequent smoking.

This suggests that names could influence our life trajectory, namely through self-esteem and opportunity.

But a person's relationship with their name is not necessarily fixed. Pain's lived experience, and that of another woman with the first name Tiny, shows how people can find ways to reclaim, and even come to value, names that once triggered ridicule and rejection.

Names can act as "signals" when you first meet someone, personality psychologist Jochen Gebauer told DW. Those signals may lose importance as people get to know each other, but Gebauer said their effects could also accumulate over a lifetime, potentially contributing to "real measurable advantages and disadvantages."

Research led by sociologist Anthony Lee found that a name can evoke inaccurate assumptions about intelligence, competence, trustworthiness, sociability and attractiveness. For Pain, other people's reactions have extended beyond jokes. In high school, a teacher refused to believe that 'Pain' was her real surname and reprimanded her in front of the class.

"Your name couldn't possibly be Pain. Write your real name!" she recalled the teacher saying.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In Lee's UK-based research, less familiar names tended to fall along a dimension partly characterized as "weirdness," which may help explain why difficult-to-pronounce or culturally unfamiliar names receive more negative evaluations.

Tiny, now 54, encountered the same questions about her unfamiliar name growing up in Greece.

Her parents had adapted "Tiny" to "Tinie" to make it sound more Greek, but people would repeatedly ask what her real or baptismal name was.

Even relatives have refused to accept it. Recently, two elderly aunts introduced her to someone as "Haritini," a conventional Greek name.

"I didn't even bother correcting them at this point," Tiny told DW.

Tiny's relationship with her name has also evolved.

"The jokes [from others] got repetitive and boring pretty fast, so at least I was left with the sense that I have something unique with my name going on."

Pain, now 40, discovered how much cultural context could change the reaction to her surname when she began learning her father's native French as a teenager. In French, 'pain' means bread.

"People started calling me 'Bread! Louise Bread.'"

Later, after moving to Germany, the jokes became mostly bread-related rather than the more scathing ones she had experienced in Australia. "That helped me to feel better about my name," she said.

As they got older, both Pain and Tiny developed coping strategies to pre-empt people's reactions to their names.

Tiny sometimes introduces herself with: "Hi, I'm Tiny. It's my name and not a statement," or, "I'm Tiny. Not a difficult name to live up to."

"This usually stops people from asking awkward questions."

Pain also began to embrace the humor surrounding her name.

"I was convinced that I should become either a doctor or a dominatrix, or perhaps both, to become either 'Doctor Pain,' or 'Mistress Pain.'"

"Names can conjure that whole spectrum of shame and pride," psychoanalyst Melissa Daum told DW.

Pain now finds that people tend to see her surname as "funny and endearing… it's become something that is more of a funny talking point."

But the childhood discomfort has not entirely disappeared. When Pain thinks about having children, her feelings about the name become more complicated.

"I don't really want to pass on my surname ... so there is a deeper wound there."

But Pain also believes her father would be deeply hurt if future children had a different name. The surname she once hated connects her to him and to her French heritage.

"Something that once feels like an inconvenience or a source of self-consciousness can become a marker of distinctiveness, history, or belonging," Daum said.

"My parents seemingly made an unfortunate choice, but by now I have embraced both the name and the sense of uniqueness that comes with it."

That does not mean she uses Tiny everywhere. She avoids it on dating apps because "Tiny might give off the wrong message," and does not use it in her email address when she wants to appear professional.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Pain, meanwhile, uses a Germanized version of the French word for bread as her social media surname.

Online, people have more control over what name they present to others. As Daum put it: "Choosing one's own name can feel like an act of mastery, even a refusal of that original passivity: I will decide what I am called and how I am encountered."

But accepting a name does not necessarily mean embracing it in every situation.

Pain once wanted to change her surname. Tiny considered using her middle name in her thirties. Neither ultimately abandoned the names that had caused them difficulties.

For Tiny, at least, a name that once made her feel awkward has become something she can imagine carrying for the rest of her life.

"It might be cute to eventually be a granny called Tiny." Edited by: Richard Connor

#348. The Hottest New Side Hustle Is Stripping Copper Wire and Posting About It

A woman wearing blue overalls and a pink tank top sits on a bench in a workshop lined with tools. Holding a broken copper wire in one hand and a small mechanical tool in the other, she looks at the camera and smiles. “Let’s make money stripping …” she says, pausing for effect. “Wire!”

This play on words is common in a specific corner of TikTok where users document the process of stripping copper wire in order to sell it for extra cash. Most of these creators don't appear to collaborate or use a common hashtag, and they haven’t coined a new term ending in -maxxing. But many of their videos have a similar, visually striking setup: A complex appliance is broken down into bits, or an industrial wire is split like ropes of licorice, and in the end, what’s left is a bundle of glistening amber.

The participants are diverse: Some are hobbyists or electricians looking for extra income. There are also people who claim to be saving for a big purchase, and others who appear to be cash-strapped teenagers. They speak a wide range of languages, including English, French, Spanish, German, and Romanian. At least one has an Etsy shop for selling hand-cast copper bricks, also known as ingots, which are made by pouring molten metal into molds.

Selling scrap copper is nothing new. The metal is relatively scarce, a strong electrical conductor, and fully recyclable—qualities that have made it a particularly valuable scrap metal for centuries. But copper prices reached an all-time high this year, making it an opportune moment for scrappers. In September, according to International Monetary Fund data, copper was worth about $14,473 per metric ton, or about $7.23 per pound, more than double what it was worth in September 2020.

While there’s no reliable data on how often people sell scrap copper, rates of copper theft—often involving metal taken from vehicles, plumbing, building roofs—may offer some clues. Craig Depken II, chair and professor of economics at the University of North Carolina, Charlotte, who has studied the phenomenon, says it tends to increase under two conditions.

The first is when the price of copper is increasing, much like it is now. The second is when people’s level of “economic distress” is high enough to make them believe the potential monetary benefits of stealing copper outweigh the risks of getting caught. (It’s legal to sell lawfully obtained copper everywhere in the US except Minnesota, which requires a state-issued license.)

On TikTok, it’s easy to find copper-themed accounts with hundreds of thousands of followers. On TikTok Shop, beginners can even buy their own wire stripper—the small tool that eats a cord and separates copper from its exoskeleton. The most popular option has sold more than 20,000 units globally at the time of writing.

The final price of recycled copper depends on a few factors, including its quality, grade, and whether it’s still “insulated” in plastic or rubber. Insulated copper can go for a fraction of the price of stripped copper, so many resellers remove it themselves to earn more. But even with a wire stripper, the process can be tedious. For some, sharing content about it appears to be a way to make the repetitive task more bearable.

People have posted about hosting a wire-stripping “girls night,” “movie night” and even “date night.” In one video from August, the creator @Payciencu.u strips wire on a bed near a man in a red sweatshirt. “How down bad are you?” reads the text overlay. “Well I’m stripping wire just to hang out with him sooo.”

Other creators treat copper scrapping as a full-on side hustle or hobby, posting time-lapses, tutorials, and lists of the “worst cables to strip for copper.”

Benjamin Stickle, a criminal justice professor at Middle Tennessee State University and author of the 2017 book Metal Scrappers and Thieves, identified three main types of legal scrappers: those trying to make ends meet, workers selling scrap metal they obtained on the job, and people raising money for a charitable cause.

Electricians, who are plentiful among wire-stripping TikTokkers, neatly fall into the worker category. Other creators are harder to classify, especially if they’re also earning money from TikTok itself.

The creator @Courtney2291, who did not respond to WIRED's inquiries, began documenting her efforts to strip enough copper to save $20,000 for a three-bedroom home earlier this year. She sourced wire and old machinery from family, her scrap yard, and Facebook Marketplace, stripping it almost daily. On October 10, a little over six months after she started, she announced she had reached her fundraising goal.

However, most of the money didn’t come from selling the copper itself. According to a scrap yard receipt Courtney shared, she earned just $99 selling 30 pounds of copper stripped over six months. One TikTok video, meanwhile, generated $1,446 after racking up 6.8 million views, according to an earnings screenshot she displayed in another video.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, Courtney says she plans to keep posting. “Once the house is mine, and it’s all set up and ready to be lived in, then I’ll continue pretty much the same kind of content,” she said. Her new goal will be to pay off the loan on the two acres of land where her home will sit.

Other popular copper creators, including King of Copper and Queen of Copper take a different approach, emphasizing scale and spectacle over personal storytelling. Their videos show them buying crates and trunks with hundreds of pounds of old wire, and then, little by little, stripping every piece

Neither claims to be affiliated with the other, despite their similar names and videos, but they occasionally repost each other's content. Both have amassed hundreds of thousands of followers, though how much they earn from TikTok and YouTube is unclear.

King of Copper is currently more than 70 days into a self-described quest to obtain, strip, and sell 1 ton of copper. In early September, 44 days into his journey, he claimed to have collected 271 pounds.

In one recent video, King of Copper spent five hours stripping wire, melting the copper in a black furnace, and then pouring it into six-ingot molds, producing what he claimed was about $90 worth of metal. In another clip, he spent two hours meticulously dismantling a 110-pound air conditioner and extracted roughly 7 pounds of copper, which he valued at $47.

His videos are mesmerizing to watch, but it’s unknown how much he might earn from them, and King of Copper did not respond to inquiries from WIRED.

Another way copper accounts can earn revenue is by marketing wire strippers and other goods for sale on TikTok Shop. When viewers purchase products through links in their videos, creators can earn a commission on each sale. Courtney, for instance, has advertised her own wire stripper. But many of these salespeople, like the creator @Dragonslayer_Industries, don’t speak much about copper outside of their ads.

“If you’ve got any scrap wire laying around, you need this wire stripper right here,” Dragonslayer Industries says in a January video before feeding a bright green wire through a machine linked for purchase. “This thing is basically just gonna print money for you. Probably pay for itself in the first hour you use it.”

“It's crazy, I only got this because my neighbor was stripping wire by hand,” he said in a June comment on one of his videos. “I got the free sample and thought l'd do 2 videos and give it to him. I thought it was such a niche thing that they probably wouldn't sell. It's been my biggest seller since l started doing affiliate videos. I can count on 5-10 of these a day selling.”

In May, he claimed to have helped sell thousands of wire strippers. It’s unclear how much he’s earned, since TikTok Shop merchants set their own affiliate commission rates. Dragonslayer Industries did not respond to WIRED's inquiries, but in a recent video he noted that it's not in his “best interest” to devote much time to wire stripping since he makes “more money” from doing other things.

But human salespeople like him face plenty of competition from AI-generated ads on TikTok Shop. Even in the specialized market for wire strippers, there’s a huge number of videos featuring AI-generated imagery and voice-overs, some using remarkably similar scripts about making “eight grand a week” from selling scrap copper.

Online, copper scrapping can seem like a solitary pursuit. Offline, however, scrappers sometimes form the kinds of communities that are less apparent on TikTok. Stickle tells WIRED that scrappers he spoke to often worked in small teams, split their profits, and looked out for one another. People struggling to get by, especially those who were experiencing homelessness, sometimes received donated metal from their fellow scrappers.

Stickle adds that they also would lean on friends who had trucks for help transporting large hauls. “If they came across a large amount [of metal] that they couldn't just dolly back to a place,” Stickle says, “they had somebody they'd call.”

big story: cool kids write in all lowercase—does that make you mad?

I worked for a whole day using only the iPhone Duo

#347. The 5 best smartwatches of 2026: Comparing the latest models from Apple, Google, and Samsung

The best smartwatches make it easy to monitor your health, track your workout progress, and check your notifications on the go. Top brands like Apple, Garmin, Google, and Samsung have all launched new smartwatches in recent months, so whether you're team Apple or team Android, you have excellent choices for your wrist. Now that we've researched the biggest releases, tested a handful of them ourselves, and drawn upon the expertise of our Ziff Davis colleagues at CNET and PCMag to fill in the gaps, we're ready to name the best smartwatches of 2026. Spoiler: You really can't go wrong with any of the latest flagship models.

In just the past few months, Samsung, Apple, Garmin, and Google have all rolled out new smartwatches. Later this month, Google will also debut the brand-new Fitbit Edge with a bright display and advanced fitness features. (The Venn diagram of the best smartwatches and the best fitness trackers is basically a perfect circle.) The competition intensifies when you factor in other wearables like smart rings and sleep trackers.

Price-wise, most of our favorite smartwatches cost somewhere between $250 and $400, though they're often on sale for much less. However, you don't have to spend very much to get a kick-ass smartwatch. In fact, we have an excellent option for folks with budgets capped at $100.

So, which watches deserve a spot on your wrist? (Say that five times fast.) Here's our definitive guide to the best smartwatches for 2026 shoppers.

If you're an iPhone user, we think you'll be very happy with Apple's latest smartwatch, the Series 12. Mashable's Stan Schroeder is in the process of testing it right now, and he said that while it may look similar to last year's model — other than some new ceramic case options — "it does bring a number of big improvements inside."

The Series 12 has a new S11 chip that adds support for Audio Intelligence tools. Moreover, it boasts "an entirely new electrical and optical heart sensor," and offers more battery life during workouts (plus faster charging), Schroeder said. He added, "The star of the show is the heart sensor array, which allows for far more frequent measurements and opens the door for new apps such as Readiness and an overhauled Vitals app."

It's worth pointing out that the Apple Watch Series 12's chip and sensors are the exact same ones you'll find inside the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4, which costs twice as much. The Ultra 4 is awesome in its own right, especially if you prioritize battery life and durability, but this means you don't need to splash out on Apple's most expensive smartwatch to use its most advanced features. The mid-tier option has never been a smarter buy or a more worthwhile upgrade from an older model.

Apple hasn't updated its cheapest wearable for 2026, but we're not complaining. Last year's Apple Watch SE 3 is still an outstanding budget pick with all the necessary features you're probably looking for in a smartwatch, like a gorgeous always-on OLED display, heart rate tracking, sleep tracking, gesture controls, and a water-resistant design. (For all intents and purposes, it's a last-gen Series 11 with a shorter battery life and no blood oxygen monitoring.) The new Series 12 is the "better" iOS-friendly smartwatch overall, but it's obviously much pricier and might be considered overkill if you're not especially health-conscious.

CNET's wearable expert Vanessa Hand Orellana tested the SE 3 and deemed it a great option "for anyone new to the Apple Watch world or upgrading from a much older model (think Series 6 or earlier)." It would also make a good starter device for a kid, she noted, letting parents "hold off on a full-fledged smartphone while maintaining a direct line of communication with their child."

The extra-nice thing about the SE 3 is it's much easier to find on sale than Apple's newest smartwatches, since it's technically an "older" model. It's regularly marked down to $219 these days, and on Black Friday, we expect it to dip under $200.

Former Mashable senior reporter and marathon runner Christianna Silva gave the Google Pixel Watch 4 a 4.4/5 rating last year, calling it "a true runner's watch." They loved its immersive display, clear audio, repairable design, custom workouts, and "solid" battery life. It used to be our favorite Android smartwatch — until Google released the Pixel Watch 5 this summer. It justifies a $50 price bump with a faster processor, double the storage, improved GPS route tracking, faster charging, and better sleep tracking, including a new breathing emergency detection feature. It can also now monitor blood pressure and insulin resistance trends.

Those who are already using Google's Gemini AI assistant on their Android phones will appreciate its integration into the Pixel Watch 5, which is pretty extensive. Gemini "acts as a proactive companion" within the device, "surfacing your flight details on the way to the airport or suggesting a phone number while you text about dinner plans," writes PCMag's Andrew Gebhart. Plus, certain Gemini features now work offline.

All things considered, we think the Pixel Watch 5 is the best Android smartwatch for most people — though if you manage to find its predecessor in stock for way cheaper, it's still a stellar choice.

Mashable readers went a bit gaga for this budget smartwatch when it was announced last July — and after testing it out for ourselves, we get it. Mashable contributor and reviewer Lauren Allain gave it a 4.6/5 rating (earning it a Mashable Choice Award), writing that it "looks, feels, and functions like a smartwatch that costs hundreds more." If you're looking for the best smartwatch under $100, nothing else comes close. (It's priced at $99, but often available for $79 these days.)

The British brand Nothing is known for its unique designs, which somehow look both retro and futuristic at the same time. CMF is the company's budget line, but the Watch 3 Pro doesn't perform like most cheap smartwatches. For starters, it offers direct ChatGPT integration, which even the Apple Watch can't do. Samsung and Google are eager to make you use their own AI tools, but if you prefer ChatGPT, then you'll love this watch.

Other spec highlights include blood oxygen monitoring, stress tracking, precise heart-rate monitoring and location tracking, and an extra-long battery life. For a smartwatch this cheap, that's an impressive roster of features. (Remember: The cheapest Apple Watch still lacks blood oxygen monitoring.) Allain said it best when she wrote that the CMF Watch 3 Pro "feels like a pricing mistake."

The Samsung Galaxy Watch 9 is another great Android wearable packed with Gemini-powered features. While we prefer the Pixel Watch 5 a bit more, mainly because its battery life is much better, this is a sensible purchase for those who are already in the Samsung ecosystem. Its feature set includes a new Heart Health score, a Fitness Index score, a Daily Cardio Load metric, and a tool that monitors your vitals overnight. (Aptly, it's called Vitals.)

On the aesthetic side, Samsung retained "squircle" design of the older Galaxy Watch 8 — which really isn't as ugly as some people say, in our opinion. CNET's Katie Collins said it has a "relatively subtle look, which is useful for an accessory you’re just as likely to be wearing to the gym as into the office." She also found it quite comfortable to wear all day, even in the larger 44mm size. "I can’t imagine ever feeling the need to take this one off, except when it needs recharging," she wrote.

The Galaxy Watch 9 also marks a price hike over its predecessor, but only a small one ($30). The older Galaxy Watch 8 remains a good alternative if you find it at a steep discount.

Haley Henschel is a Chicago-based Senior Shopping Reporter at Mashable who reviews and finds deals on popular tech, from laptops to gaming consoles and VPNs. She has years of experience covering shopping holidays and can tell you what’s actually worth buying on Black Friday and Amazon Prime Day. Her work has also explored the driving forces behind digital trends within the shopping sphere, from dupes to 12-foot skeletons.

As a writer for GQ, he covered everything from bull-riding competitions to the best Legos for adults, and he’s also contributed to publications such as The Daily Beast, Gear Patrol, and The Awl.

#346. RAF Fairford plot ‘coordinated by handler working on behalf of Iran’

Two men charged under National Security Act were directed by handler believed to have been working for Iranian intelligence, court told

A group of men were directed to drive vans loaded with petrol to Fairford RAF base by a handler known as Ali, who police believe was working on behalf of Iran, a court heard on Tuesday.

One of the five men tasked with driving to the base called police in the early hours of 27 September after the handler warned the group about a bomb in the vans, prosecutors told Westminster magistrates court.

Two men, Mohmed Abdulkarim, 24, and Nouman Essghir, 24, both appeared in court on Tuesday charged under the National Security Act 2023.

Abdulkarim, of London, was charged with assisting a foreign intelligence service. He is accused of buying the vans used in the Fairford incident on the instruction of Ali.

Essghir, also of London, was charged with entering a prohibited place for a purpose prejudicial to the UK, after initially being arrested at the scene near the Gloucestershire base on 27 September and then re-arrested on 20 October. He is accused of visiting RAF Fairford and filming around the facility on a video call with Ali before returning a month later in the convoy of vans.

Abdulkarim has been released on bail under conditions including the surrender of his passport, and Essghir has been remanded in custody. They will appear at the Old Bailey on 20 November.

Prosecutor Louise Gray alleged the defendants were in contact with an individual known as Ali who is now believed to be abroad.

She said that during the events of 27 September at the RAF Fairford base, five men who were arrested travelled in vans, parked and got out “and were told to do so by someone known as Mr Ali”.

One of them, she said, had claimed they were told there was a bomb in the van and called 999.

The court heard that the person calling stated that they were from London and that he was one of a group of five males. The caller told the police that they had been tasked to drive vans to the location for the purpose of a new business venture. When the vans arrived at the location, the organiser was in communication with them over the telephone.

Gray alleged he then told them to park the vans and to walk away from them. After they did that, the organiser told them to run as he had planted a bomb in the van. The caller indicated that he and his colleagues had run away from the vans and were now calling the police for help.

The defendants are believed to have known Ali for “a number of years”, she said.

Essghir is accused of travelling to RAF Fairford on 22 August and contacting Ali.

Abdulkarim is accused of buying mobile phones and collecting cash for Ali, as well as buying five vans including the three that were driven to RAF Fairford.

The court heard he had known Ali for 10 years and it was claimed the latter had been linked to a drone incident at the Israeli embassy.

Counter Terror Policing said they believed the foreign intelligence service involved in the case was Iranian.

The assistant commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of national counter-terrorism policing in the UK, said: “We have seen a range of incidents in recent months that we believe have been directed by the Iranian state, and as the director general of MI5 said just the other week, we are working together to intervene, identify and respond to that threat.

“The public can help us with this, and as always I would urge them to report anything that they’re worried about – those reports can and do make a difference.”

A 25-year-old UK-Iranian dual national who was also arrested on 20 October on suspicion of aiding Iran’s intelligence service has been released without charge but remains on police bail after being arrested on 1 October on suspicion of preparation of terrorist acts.

Five other men arrested during the investigation remain on police bail.

Frank Ferguson, the chief crown prosecutor for the CPS special crime and counter-terrorism division, said: “The Crown Prosecution Service has decided to prosecute two individuals with National Security Act offences following a Counter Terrorism Policing investigation into a high-profile incident involving RAF Fairford. We will continue to work closely with our partners as this complex investigation continues.”
