#343. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

Scan the QR code to download the CNN app from the Apple Store.

#340. Fewer people are flying as airfare soars. Here's why airlines aren't worried

Fewer people are flying this year, but with airfare this high and climbing, airline executives say that's just fine.

At the New York Hilton in Midtown Manhattan on Friday night, the aviation industry held its annual Wings Club gala, honoring Ed Bastian, CEO of profit-leader Delta Air Lines.

North American airline CEOs and other executives at the black-tie bash told CNBC that demand trends aren't worrying them, but persistently high, profit-crimping fuel prices are.

The number of people screened at U.S. checkpoints is on track for its first annual decline since 2020. Major U.S. carriers have said capturing higher fares matters more than fully filling planes or flying record numbers of travelers.

"We're actively prioritizing yield versus load factor and really happy as we see it. It fits right in with the premium investments that we're making," American Airlines Chief Commercial Officer Nat Pieper said on the company's earnings call Thursday.

American's load factor, a gauge of how full planes are flying, fell to 83.4% in the third quarter from 86% in the same quarter of 2025.

Plane ticket prices have skyrocketed as airlines grapple with a spike in jet fuel prices, their biggest expense after labor, and pass more of those costs on to customers.

Fares in September were up more than 22% from a year earlier, according to the latest inflation read. Fare tracker Hopper said it expects this holiday season to come with the most expensive ticket prices in years.

The average Southwest Airlines fare in the third quarter, for example, was $231.69, up close to $50 from a year earlier. That doesn't include Southwest's new fees, like its extra charges for checked bags or a seat closer to the front of the plane.

The airline's revenue passenger count, however, fell 3.2% from a year ago to about 33.5 million people.

Still, both fares and the passenger counts exceeded Southwest's expectations.

"Passenger fares came in about $3 above what our forecast was and the more important thing is that passenger volumes also exceeded our expectations," Southwest CEO Bob Jordan said on an earnings call Thursday.

Since April — when airlines began warning in earnest about profits taking a hit from the spike in jet fuel — through Thursday, about 9.3 million fewer people were screened at airport checkpoints than in the same period of 2025, according to the Transportation Security Administration. That's down less than 2% from last year, but it follows years of growth.

With jet fuel prices more than double year-ago levels — as seen in U.S. Gulf Coast spot prices at $4.63 a gallon, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration — airlines have focused on recovering as much of the fuel spike as possible.

Despite some drops, airline executives said during their earnings calls that overall travel demand is robust, especially for profitable premium cabins like business class.

And not every carrier is seeing a decline. United Airlines flew 2.4% more passengers in the third quarter than a year ago and said its unit revenue rose 16%, the biggest increase since the pandemic.

"I've been pleasantly surprised at the industry's ability this year to raise yields and pass through much of the increase in fuel prices," CEO Scott Kirby said on the company's earnings call Wednesday. "So it's possible that airlines will continue to focus on yields at the expense of load factor and that alone could get us to double-digit margins next year."

Most airlines expect fuel costs to keep biting, but executives say they're confident in their pricing power.

United, for example, said it expects to pay an average of $4.80 per gallon for fuel in the fourth quarter, up from $3.90 per gallon in the third.

American, United, Delta and Southwest all cut their 2026 profit forecasts this month when they reported earnings. American is the only one of the four that expects to post a loss this year.

American and others have also reduced growth plans or warned the industry could dial back its capacity expansion goals, which could keep fares elevated. Reduced capacity can make bookable seats scarcer, driving up prices even more if demand holds.

The carriers said demand has been particularly strong for premium seats, which command hundreds, if not thousands of dollars, more than coach tickets.

"Today, we find ourselves yet at another inflection point for our industry and the world. ... Geopolitics affecting our business, driving the price of jet fuel to historic highs," Bastian said in his award-acceptance speech, adding that artificial intelligence makes it hard to predict months or weeks ahead.

"It causes many people to question yet again the durability of our industry. But I'm not worried because I know the people in this room, and I know your resilience and how you've demonstrated that time and time again," he said.

Smaller airlines set to report results in the coming weeks, starting with JetBlue Airways next week, followed by Frontier Airlines and Allegiant Air, have traditionally focused on more price-sensitive customers, which could show if those travelers are pulling back at all.

One factor that could also affect air travel is the demise of Spirit Airlines, which went out of business in early May.

"Some of its customers have likely stopped flying," Bank of America said in a research note Thursday.

Many of the problems that plagued the airline industry for years are starting to abate.

In some cases, aircraft deliveries are still years behind schedule. But Boeing, whose CEO, Kelly Ortberg, attended on Friday night, and its main rival, Airbus, have ramped up production. Boeing will update investors on its production outlook when it reports quarterly results Tuesday.

The Federal Aviation Administration has approved Boeing's Max 7, a key smaller jet for Southwest Airlines, and the Max 10, which Alaska Airlines and United Airlines have been waiting for for years.

Pratt & Whitney parent, RTX, and GE Aerospace say they're improving engine durability and shop visit turnaround time, though customers say more improvements are needed.

#339. Germany sit out FIFA Under-15 World Cup as Russia return

Germany is one a handful of FIFA's 211 nations to refuse an invitation to play at the inaugural Under-15 World Cup. The tournament will also mark Russia's return to the FIFA fold.

With nearly 1,000 matches and 189 teams, the Under-15 World Cup in Azerbaijan is bloated even by FIFA's usual standards. It marks a change in its attitude to children's international tournaments, which have not existed on anywhere near this scale before.

For Germany, it is too much too soon. The German FA (DFB) told DW that it had "consciously decided not to participate." The statement from the organization suggested the DFB — whose development of young players is under scrutiny after a run of poor performances by the men's national team — felt such a tournament was not appropriate for players so young.

"At this age level, our identification and development process is still underway, and we therefore do not believe it is appropriate to nominate a fixed squad for an international tournament at this stage," it read.

"More broadly, the DFB's approach to talent development is based on the principle of supporting young players in a long-term and age-appropriate manner. We carefully assess at which stage international competitions and development measures are beneficial for each age group.

"In our view, young talents should not be exposed too early to the accompanying aspects of professional football. These include increased expectations and pressure, public evaluation of their performances, and early interest from agents and the football market."

The refusal to play in the tournament, which began this week, is consistent with Germany's more cautious approach to youth development. While in some leagues, like the Premier League in England, there is no regulation about youths playing professionally, in Germany's top flight, the Bundesliga, players must be 16. In fact, before the 2020-21 season, players could not be granted a pro license before they were 17 years and six months old.

That change enabled Youssoufa Moukoko to make his Bundesliga debut for Borussia Dortmund a day after his 16th birthday in 2020, becoming the league's youngest player in the process.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

But, some would argue his is a cautionary tale. Hyped wildly due to his goalscoring exploits at youth level, Moukoko was fast-tracked by Dortmund and Germany, who called him up for the 2022 World Cup. Now, still just 21, he is struggling for game time and goals on loan at Corum, in the Turkish Super League, from Danish side Copenhagen.

Players under 16 are also forbidden by FIFA from having an agent. But this is poorly regulated in some regions of the world. That led Bundesliga club St. Pauli, then in the second division, to announce they would no longer work with player agents or private agencies in their youth system in 2023.

"We want to focus on partnership and dialogue with the players, their families and their relatives," explained the head of the club's youth academy, Benjamin Liedtke, as St. Pauli officially positioned themselves "against the commercialization of youth football."

Germany are one of 22 nations to refuse the invitation to compete in the tournament, 18 of whom are from UEFA, Europe's confederation. The USA are also one of the prominent refusals alongside Ukraine, whose FA told German public broadcaster Sportschau that they initially refused on sporting grounds before claiming that "other countries indicated that they would compete but did not want to play against Russia." DW contacted a number of FAs to clarify this point but is yet to receive a response.

FIFA President Gianni Infantino, who has become a notorious figure thanks to a number of scandals over the past year, told Sky in February: "We have to [look at reinstating Russia], definitely, because this ban has not achieved anything. It has just created frustration and hatred. Having girls and boys from Russia being able to play football games in other parts of Europe would help."

Eight months later, he has his wish. The ongoing tournament, billed as the "FIFA U-15 World Cup & Festival" comprises eight-a-side games with 20 minute halves, a smaller pitch and a number of other technical tweaks. FIFA say the 2027 edition will be for girls teams only.

"Young players need meaningful matches, different opponents and different football experiences in order to develop. Talent exists everywhere, but the opportunities available to young players are not always the same," said FIFA Chief of Global Football Development, Arsene Wenger, likely referencing that teams from Bhutan and Seychelles to the Bahamas and Vanuatu are present.

"This tournament gives players from across the world the chance to compete, to learn and to grow."

FIFA are yet to respond to a DW request for further comment.

The round robin stage, according to FIFA, has placed teams in "groups that provide diverse sporting and regional representation" but also appears to have, at least initially, kept apart nations in conflict with each other.

For example, Israel and Palestine or Kosovo and Serbia are not in the same groups but could, in theory, meet in the later stages of the tournament. This would not be allowed in senior football, thanks to various bans and workarounds.

FIFA are vague about how the tournament works after the round robin, saying only that it will consist of "four to five additional matches per team, determined by results and subsequent tier placement. [There will be] one winner for each of the six tiers." It also said that the winner of the top tier will be crowned champions but all teams will play matches until the tournament concludes on October 30.

But, given that they have been banned from international football since invading Ukraine in 2022, it is the presence of a Russian national team at a FIFA tournament that will likely heap the most controversy on to young shoulders. Though a Russian presence was not the reason for German refusal, not attending for sporting reasons may also turn out to be a smart piece of sport politics.

#338. The 7 best Amazon Echo devices for 2026

Among Amazon's flagship gadgets, like Kindles and Fire TVs, Echos are the stars of the show. These multitasking smart home devices do a little bit of everything in nearly every room in the house. Plus, with the rollout of Alexa+, they're now supercharged with extra AI features.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Whether you need a smart speaker, a smart alarm clock, or a hub for all of your smart devices, there's an Echo to help you out. Amazon's list of devices is ever-growing, and we've tested as many as we could to find the best Echo devices for your smart home.

Amazon might have some new competition this year, as Apple announced a collection of smart home products, a new venture for the tech giant. The Apple HomeView is like an Echo Show for Apple enthusiasts, while the HomePod mini 2 acts as an Echo or Echo Dot counterpart.

If you're ready to invest in an Echo, you don't need to buy these devices at full price. They always go on sale during events like Prime Day and Black Friday, so it's worth holding out until the next big sale, which happens to be Black Friday on Nov. 27, 2026.

Thanks to the global memory crisis, widespread price increases are plaguing tech. As of August 2026, major price increases came to Amazon devices. This included Kindles and, unfortunately, Echos, too. Here are all of the Echo devices affected:

Echo Dot Max — was $99.99, now $119.99 (+$20)

Echo Spot — was $79.99, now $109.99 (+$30)

Echo Show 5 — was $89.99, now $99.99 (+$10)

Echo Show 8 — was $149.99, now $199.99 (+$50)

Echo Show 11 — was $219.99, now $249.99 (+$30)

Echo Show 15 — was $299.99, now $349.99 (+$50)

Echo Show 21 — was $399.99, now $499.99 (+$100)

Read our full review of the Echo Dot (5th Gen) here.

Most people: Not sure where to begin with Echo devices? The best starting point is the Echo Dot. It's an easy-to-use smart home speaker, able to fill rooms with music from your preferred streaming service. It responds to voice commands and is even Alexa+ compatible.

The Echo Dot is the best entry point to Amazon's smart home devices. The Echo Dot is a squat little speaker, measuring 3.5 inches tall, suitable for any countertop, even when you're cramped for space.

Now in its 5th generation, the Echo Dot boasts more vibrant sound with clearer vocals and deeper bass, though it does not have Dolby audio like the Echo Dot Max.

The Echo Dot has a built-in mic and in-app privacy controls, as well as a temperature sensor and motion detector to help automate other smart home devices. Amazon takes smart home functioning quite seriously with its Dot: If you enable sound detection routines, for instance, you can program a particular sound (dog barking, oven dinging, door opening) to trigger a certain Alexa Routine.

You want a smart speaker for the bedroom: Whether you want an Echo smart speaker in every room or just need a smarter alarm clock, the 2024 Echo Spot is made for your bedside. It has programmable routines to queue your morning wake-up and evening wind-down. Plus, it responds quickly and clearly to Alexa commands, so you can play music from the speaker without opening your phone or ask for a weather report as you're getting dressed.

The 2024 Echo Spot improves on the previous models' shortcomings, creating an easy-to-use smart alarm clock that's both useful and good-looking. It's bigger than both the Echo Pop and Echo Dot; at 4.5 inches by 4.1 inches by 4.4 inches, it takes up just the right amount of nightstand space without overcrowding.

Slanted at a slight angle, the upper half of the clock face features a small touchscreen. While we think it's a little too small, it still has vibrant colors. The bottom half is a 1.73-inch front-firing speaker, the same one that comes in the Echo Dot. The speaker produces clear audio that doesn't sound tinny; however, it's meant for small spaces — don't plan on using it for your next party. Alexa also answers quickly to questions and commands, making it easy to turn on a podcast to kick off your morning.

The alarm function works like any daily alarm clock. Snoozing is easy: Tap the top of the device with more than one finger to get another few minutes of rest. Meanwhile, turning off the alarm is slightly more annoying, requiring you to interact with the touchscreen. While it works fine, it would feel more natural to have a "Stop" button on top.

The touch screen is rather minuscule and limited to the small rectangular space on the half-moon top of the clock face. Another big drawback of the Echo Spot comes when you're playing music or listening to a podcast. When audio is playing, the time disappears — a hindrance if you need to know what time it is.

Otherwise, the Echo Spot is a worthwhile entry into the Echo line of devices, offering a well-designed smart alarm clock.

Smart home beginners: Amazon's Echo devices don't just take the form of compact orb-like speakers. The Echo Show line of devices adds displays, and they're perfect for the person who wants to be able to take video calls, watch recipe videos, or check the calendar hands-free. The Echo Show 8 is a great starting point with a redesigned screen and upgraded chip for better performance.

Breaking beyond just audio, we're now jumping into the Echo devices with visual displays, also known as the Echo Show lineup. First on our list is the Echo 8 with its 8-inch HD touchscreen and 13-megapixel camera. It can act as a sous chef in the kitchen, serving up recipes while keeping your hands free, and it can just as easily make video calls to keep up with friends and family or stay productive while WFH (though our reviewer SaVanna Shoemaker noted some issues with Zoom functionality with the previous-gen model). You can also "Drop-In" on any other Alexa devices in your household, including video chat with other Echo Shows.

That's just the tip of the iceberg when it comes to the Echo Show 8's visual feats, though, and the gorgeous display really opens up the door to an incredible amount of enhanced Alexa functionality. The Echo Show 8 can even function as a security camera, and you can check in on what it sees via the Alexa app on your phone while you're away. That said, you can close the camera shutter and shut off the mic for more privacy.

If you're looking for the same capabilities in a smaller size for the bedroom or office, however, the Echo Show 5 is an equally nice choice. Note that the smaller-screened Show 5 won't do as good a job with streaming, pulling up ingredient lists on recipes, or other tasks that require a large display. Check out our full in-depth comparison of the two for more details.

Audiophiles: If you're only going to use your Echo Dot for talking to Alexa, then go with the classic Echo Dot. However, if you also want to listen to music, then it's worth paying a little more for the new Echo Dot Max, released in fall 2025. It has stronger bass and better sound quality if you want to fill your space.

In September 2025, Amazon released a whole new line of smart home products, including a new range of Echo Dot devices. That included the Echo Dot Max and Echo Studio, which feature the most advanced speakers ever in the Amazon Echo lineup. Technically, the pricier Echo Studio has more powerful speakers, but the Echo Dot Max gets our recommendation.

So, why are we recommending the Echo Dot Max? Its $119.99 price tag is a little more reasonable than the $219.99 Echo Studio. (You can buy some amazing portable Bluetooth speakers for that price.)

The Echo Dot Max is compact like the classic Echo Dot, but it offers upgraded bass and speakers thanks to a redesigned tweeter and woofer.

Kiddos who need an age-appropriate speaker: Looking to get a speaker for the kids' rooms? The Amazon Echo Dot Kids speaker is great since it responds to voice commands while keeping parental controls in place. Not to mention, it comes in adorable designs, too.

In addition to a built-in mic off button for privacy, the Echo Dot Kids is also packed with parental controls: Like only giving kid-friendly responses to any questions asked, filtering out explicit songs when playing music, and only allowing calls to parent-approved contacts. It also allows Alexa's voice to be changed to that of an owl- or dragon-inspired character, making the whole experience more fun for little ones. And, it comes with a two-year worry-free guarantee that allows for replacement if it breaks (which is a huge step up from the Echo Dot's 90-day limited warranty).

Read our full review of the Echo Pop (1st Gen) here.

Budget shoppers: If you want a smart home device, but aren't ready to drop a lot of money right now, then the Echo Pop is for you. It starts at $39.99 but is often on sale for under $25, an extremely good deal considering it comes with lossless audio. But word of warning, it's often out of stock so grab it when you find it.

Editor's note: The Amazon Echo Pop is unavailable on Amazon's website. We've reached out to Amazon to confirm if the product is currently out of stock or being phased out. We'll update this piece when we've received a comment from Amazon.

Mashable contributor SaVanna Shoemaker reviewed the lil' Pop in July 2023 and praised it for its ability to (inexpensively) fill up a living space with great-quality music. Besides being a handy and cheap smart speaker, the Echo Pop also boasts "nearly all of the same functionality" as other Echo devices on the market — including that signature smart home compatibility and Alexa voice control. Note that, unlike the 5th Gen Echo Dot, the Pop doesn't have temperature sensors or multi-device pairing.

Shoemaker was quick to mention (and bust) the myth that the Echo Pop is equivalent to a Bluetooth speaker. You cannot, in fact, use it to stream audio directly from your phone. The Alexa app must serve as a handy intermediary in order to do so.

Commuters: Want to bring all the perks of a smart home device to your car? Do exactly that with the Amazon Echo Auto. Your car needs to have a USB port to operate it but that's it. Once plugged in, it will play music through your car's speakers at your command.

PSA: Amazon has an Echo device just for your car, in case you weren't aware. While other Echo devices are designed mostly around the idea of being a hub for smart home devices, the Echo Auto brings that very intelligence to your vehicle.

As of now, you likely don't have a ton of smart tech to control while in your car, but the Echo Auto allows you to access Alexa's hundreds of thousands of skills hands-free while you drive. You can control your music, podcasts, or audiobooks, or make phone calls and send texts.

You can also remotely control your devices at home from the road, such as turning on your lights and bumping up your thermostat for a warm welcome when you walk in the door. You can even contact roadside assistance, all with simple voice commands.

The Echo Auto comes with an adhesive mount, USB-A cable, and AUX cable, and it's built with privacy controls, such as a "mic off" button.

All Echo devices utilize Amazon Alexa's AI superpowers — it's just a matter of which form you want your Echo to take. The Echo and Echo Dot are smart speakers that you can use for music, phone calls, calendar reminders, or news alerts. Amazon Echo Show devices, on the other hand, can do all of the aforementioned audio stuff, but they also boast colorful displays for making video calls, streaming entertainment, or managing your day. Think of Echo Shows as stationary, more affordable tablets, essentially.

Most Echo devices are designed to act as smart hubs within your home, but some Echo devices can also be taken on the road. Echo Auto brings hands-free Alexa functionality to the car, while Echo Buds and Echo Frames can travel with you anywhere.

Alexa has an answer to almost anything you ask. Just by shouting out, you can play music, adjust the volume, and set an alarm or timer. And that's just naming a few options. On devices like the Echo Show, you can also ask Alexa to pull up recipes or answer questions that come up while cooking. With Alexa+, the AI agent doesn't just respond to commands but can actually engage in conversation.

Here's a short list of what Alexa+ can do:

Provide a highlight reel of the day's security footage

Control your smart home technology from one central hub

Get answers to any question you can think of

Make phone calls (and video calls with an Echo Show)

Stream music (and video on an Amazon Echo Show)

There are more than 100,000 Alexa skills that you can utilize via an Echo device, and Amazon is always adding more.

Alexa gets supercharged when other smart home tech comes into the picture. If you have a smart thermostat or smart light bulbs, as long as they're compatible with the Alexa system, you can create routines through your Echo. That can be as simple as telling Alexa to turn on the lights in one room or programming Alexa to turn the thermostat down before you come home.

Regardless of the Echo device you choose, its built-in access to Alexa’s artificial intelligence allows you to do so much. But how does Alexa work? Primarily through voice commands. When you call out to Alexa, similar to Apple's Siri, your device will respond to execute the task.

Amazon also has an Alexa app that connects your smartphone to your echo devices. So if you prefer to control your Echo through your smartphone over voice controls, that's another option. However, the Alexa voice controls is super helpful if you need to go hands-free.

It should also be noted that Alexa and Echo devices are built with many layers of privacy protection these days. You can turn the mic off if you don't want Alexa listening, and you can also view and delete your voice recordings. If your Echo device has a camera, the latest models also include a shutter to cover it up when not in use.

Before we try to make sense of all your different options, let's just define what an Amazon Echo device is, was, and has become. When this line of devices first launched, the Echo was a tall cylinder that was essentially a speaker that responded to your voice commands, thanks to the artificial intelligence of Alexa. This is still the core of today's Amazon Echo devices, with some still offering just audio and others offering both auditory and visual content. Today's Echo devices also now come in many different shapes and sizes, and Alexa's capabilities have greatly expanded.

Many people tend to use the terms "Echo" and "Alexa" interchangeably, but even though their functions are deeply intertwined, they're actually quite different things. Amazon Alexa is the artificial intelligence system (kind of like its "rival" Google Assistant) that responds to your questions, acts on your voice commands, and does all the myriad other things that Alexa can do — but we'll get to that in a minute. On the flip side, Echo devices are simply the conduit for bringing that service to you. That's why Echo devices take on so many different forms, from spherical speakers to video screens to earbuds you can take on the go.

Echo devices are some of Amazon's flagship products, so you can expect to see their prices drop accordingly during Amazon Prime Day events, as well as other big shopping holidays like Black Friday and Cyber Monday. In 2024, many Black Friday Echo deals continued well into late December.

You can find Amazon Echo devices and accessories for sale at retailers other than Amazon, such as Best Buy, Target, Staples, and even The Home Depot. However, you may not have access to as wide of a selection of styles and colors as you would if you shopped directly through Amazon. You're also not likely to score the best price when shopping from a store other than Amazon, mostly because of all the aforementioned sale periods (and not just Amazon's signature Prime Day) when prices drop dramatically.

Possibly one of the best perks of purchasing an Amazon Echo device is that there's no monthly fee to use it. It's your entry point into Alexa's incredible AI and — aside from the purchase price of the Echo device itself — she and her ever-growing list of skills are totally free to use. The only reason you might encounter a paywall while using your Echo device is if you're asking Alexa to access another service, such as a streaming music service. (And yes, that includes Amazon Music; you may be prompted to start a Music Unlimited subscription if you try to access a song that's not free. Can't win 'em all, I guess.)

According to Amazon, there are more than 30,000 smart home devices marked with the "Works with Alexa" badge — and if something works with Alexa, it works with Echo too. There's a whole host of smart home items you can connect to your Echo device, including light bulbs, switches, locks, thermostats, fans, and smart plugs that can turn pretty much any electronic device into a “smart home” device.

Amazon's own brands (like Blink and eero) make a ton of smart home devices that will link up seamlessly, but Echo devices — especially those with a built-in Zigbee smart home hub — should be able to connect to any brand of smart home device fairly easily with the command "Alexa, discover my devices." (To be clear, though, Zigbee isn't required for connectivity)

Echo devices also work exceptionally well with each other. You can link as many of them together as you want, and you can even have them play the same music at the same time for a whole-home audio experience, or act as an intercom to let your family know dinner is ready.

While we haven't had the opportunity to conduct hands-on testing with every single one of these Echo devices, we did put forth hours of careful research into Amazon's current lineup of Echo devices and analyzed our own writers' in-depth reviews and personal experiences. We included many of the newest Echo devices in our guide, comparing their features to determine the best Echo devices for a variety of different use cases.

To make our selection, we took some key factors into consideration:

We included as many different types of Echo devices as we could, including the audio-only original and Dot lineups as well as the more visual Echo Show options. We then compared and contrasted specs and selected those devices that rose to the top.

Echo devices can vary greatly in price, so we made it a point to include budget-friendly options as well as more extravagant, "splurge-worthy" choices.

Amazon's smart home ecosystem goes beyond Echo devices, and also includes Ring and Blink products. We chose Echo products with modern smart features, such as the ability to control and sync with other smart home gadgets.

#337. Some iPhone Duo Models Are Breaking. iFixit’s Teardown Might Explain Why

The first batch of Apple’s first folding phone may already have some screen issues.

The iPhone Duo is a strong first foldable from the company, but multiple Reddit reports say the phone’s inner screen cracks when customers open the device. Some apparently happen very soon, like during device setup. (Others might be from influencers trying to break the thing, like YouTuber JerryRigEverything.) People are already calling this “crackgate.”

The issue affects a small number of units—most iPhone Duo models aren't affected. If there is a problem, it crops up quite quickly after usage, not days after unboxing. (WIRED’s review unit hasn’t had any issues after more than two weeks of daily use.)

“The response to iPhone Duo from early users has been incredibly positive, and we are seeing exciting things from developers who are rapidly updating their apps to take advantage of its unique form factor,” an Apple spokesperson tells WIRED. "We are aware of an issue impacting the inner display on a small number of iPhone Duo units. If users experience issues with their iPhone Duo display, they should contact AppleCare for a replacement.”

This is familiar territory for foldable phones. A hinge means strain on the screen and the components inside. It’s also a place for dust to seep in and break the phone, as with Samsung’s original Galaxy Fold in 2019, forcing the company to pause sales and fix the issue. Some devices handle it better than others, and as the product category has grown, those issues have largely diminished. But fewer problems don’t mean easier fixes.

On Sunday, repair company iFixit released its teardown of the iPhone Duo, including a video detailing the specific parts inside and what to look out for if people need to fix their $1,999 phone one day—maybe sooner than they think.

The culprit behind the screen breaking hasn’t been officially verified yet, but iFixit has a theory. Along the hinge, a display cable passes across the center of the hinge. In iFixit’s stress test simulation, the center support bar along the hinge fails to get out of the way as the screen folds, making a stress point right in the middle of the screen that could be leading to those cracks.

The phone got a provisional repairability rating of 5/10, though that score may be updated to factor in how Apple supplies replacement parts. A 5/10 doesn’t sound great, but it’s actually the highest repair rating iFixit has given any foldable phone.

“The state of foldables today is one of the hardest engineering challenges across a number of disciplines out there in the market today,” iFixit CEO Kyle Wiens said at an event the day before Apple announced its folding phone in September. “It is the pinnacle of material engineering and materials science. It is ludicrously difficult to accomplish this.”

The iPhone Duo earned good marks on some repair fronts. The battery and back panels are straightforward to remove and replace. The outer trim comes off in one piece. The 5.4-inch front screen comes off easily enough. Disassembling the pieces and putting everything back together is doable too, since almost everything is secured with brackets and screws you can remove and reuse.

The tricky part, according to iFixit, is the inner display. A thin matte nano-texture finish sits on top of the OLED screen, and if you’re not careful, you could easily peel the layer off. (Apple strictly tells users not to remove this finish on a support page.)

Removing the full inner screen requires melting the adhesive with a heat gun and some real elbow grease to pull the screen away. Users have reported breaking this part of the screen, and it seems it is the most fragile component and the hardest to replace. (Apple has an inner screen repair service, but it costs $999.) The Duo also collects dust in typical places like the USB-C port, outer edges, and speaker, but it accumulates along the inner hinge, causing it to sound “crunchy” like other folding devices when dust gets inside.

Apple long resisted letting users repair their own devices. When it finally started offering real repair options in 2021, its programs got off to a rough start and were less helpful than they could have been. Slowly but surely, thanks to various legislation in the US and the European Union, Apple has been introducing efforts to let people fix their own things. The iPhone Duo had a widely accessible repair manual on launch day, which hasn’t always been the case for how the company shares that kind of information.

The iPhone Duo may not be perfectly repairable—folding phones might never get there. But when a device starts having problems like cracked screens, it’ll be nice to know how to fix the thing.

Updated at 2:35 pm ET on October 26: We've added a statement from Apple.

Additional reporting by Julian Chokkattu.

#334. Unpaid carers say chancellor’s plan to scrap allowance cliff edge falls short

Digital taper system for benefit expected in budget, but those already hit with overpayments will still have to pay

“On the surface it sounds really positive – and I really hope that it is,” says unpaid carer Guy Shahar of the government’s decision to abolish the notorious carer’s allowance cliff edge.

But Shahar is angry that most carers hit with unjust overpayments in the past will still have to repay them, for all the promise of a different future. And he notes that the cliff edge remains in place for four more years, potentially catching out more carers.

“They are effectively saying: ‘We know that the system was unfair but we will still punish you for having been the victim of an unfair system,’” he says.

The chancellor is expected to announce the introduction of a new digital taper system into the carer’s allowance benefit in the budget, in effect ending a rule that has landed hundreds of thousands of carers with life-shattering financial penalties.

Unpaid carers have broadly welcomed the move – and there is a general acknowledgment that ministers are moving positively, if slowly, to reform a system with inbuilt injustices and terrible management that birthed what became known as the carer’s allowance scandal.

But there is also disbelief that the new system – which must be built from scratch – will be live before 2030, and a feeling that the Department for Work and Pensions (DWP) has a lot more to do to regain the trust of unpaid carers.

Shahar and his wife, Oksana, who care for their autistic son, Daniel, were victims of the cliff edge. This rule meant that a carer who earns more than the weekly earnings limit allowed – currently £204 – will forfeit their entire week’s carer’s allowance payment, now £86.45.

A carer who earned a penny over the limit for a year would breach it by 52p but would be asked to repay £4,495.40. Last year, the Shahars were landed with a £10,000 repayment bill for relatively trivial earnings breaches dating back five years.

Oksana had earned more than the allowed limit by just £1.92 a week on average while juggling caring with part-time minimum wage jobs. In some weeks, she was paid just 38p more than the carer’s allowance earnings threshold.

Over the period, she earned a total of £505 more than the rules allowed – but was duly issued with a bill for £10,130.45 – plus a £50 civil penalty. The Shahars are challenging the charge at tribunal but the stress is real and ongoing.

Unpaid carer Elizabeth Tait, who looks after her disabled son, Oliver, is staggered that the taper system is years away: “We live in the age of tech; it is ludicrous it is going to take so long. They need to make it a priority.”

Tait, a former teacher and now a private tutor and educational consultant who ended up with a bill for £1,623 after unwittingly breaching the earnings threshold, says the carer’s allowance cliff edge symbolises a “whole crazy system”.

Reforming carer’s allowance is essential but it needs to be part of a wider examination of the crucial role of unpaid care in propping up England’s social care arrangements, she says: “We care willingly and lovingly, and yet we end up a casualty of the system.”

Andrea Tucker, who for years acted as an unpaid carer for her mother, is one of a handful of carers to challenge a cliff edge penalty and win, successfully overturning a £4,600 overpayment at a tribunal in February 2025.

As a result, she did not have to repay the money, although she suffered months of stress along the way. She welcomes the decision to introduce a taper but believes carers punished unfairly by draconian rules should be compensated.

“People repaying overpayments should no longer have to, and they should be given back what they have already paid,” she says.

Tucker recalls how for years she carefully monitored her monthly earnings from her part-time charity shop role to stay under the limit. Some weeks she would work six hours for nothing so as not to risk losing carer’s allowance.

Even so, she was hit with an overpayment when DWP officials decided – unlawfully – that she could not average her earnings over a one-year period.

There is raw bitterness among carers about the cliff edge, she says. “My friend is paying back their overpayment at £10 a month. She went £12.50 over as a result of receiving holiday pay. She always tells me: ‘I hope I die before I have paid it all back.’”

#331. American Express is cutting 5% of its workforce to 'prepare for AI'

American Express is cutting thousands of employees as the leading credit card company prepares for artificial intelligence to take over more of its internal processes.

Amex is eliminating 5% of its global workforce and taking a $336 million restructuring charge, CEO Stephen Squeri told analysts Friday on the company's third-quarter earnings call. The firm had almost 77,000 employees at the end of 2025.

Chief Financial Officer Christophe Le Caillec told CNBC in an interview that the restructuring was to make American Express "leaner, more agile, and also to prepare for AI."

"You don't want to automate processes that are not optimally structured," Le Caillec said. "So what we're doing here is to reduce layers and increase spans of controls, so that we can automate and take advantage of AI in the coming quarters and the coming years."

The Amex announcement reflects a broader trend across financial services, where banks are working to replace repetitive roles with AI automation. On Thursday, Barclays confirmed plans to trim 5,000 jobs — also roughly 5% of its staff — in another tech-driven cost initiative. The moves come even as the sector is enjoying robust profits from growing consumer spending and resilient credit.

During the analyst call, Squeri said the charge was to improve the company's efficiency.

"We've got good momentum, but to accelerate innovation and win over the next few years, we needed to become [a] more agile and efficient organization," Squeri said.

Squeri devoted a 10-minute section of his prepared remarks to his views on AI, focusing mainly on how it will be used to personalize experiences and offers for Amex cardholders.

While the CEO emphasized the need to meet customers' desires to use AI, including agents, he added that Amex is "not going to let assistants run rogue through our system."

"We need to protect our customers [and] merchants, and we're only going to allow agents to access our systems that are either approved through API or MCP channels," he said, citing two ways to securely connect outside platforms with Amex systems.

Amex posted quarterly results that topped Wall Street profit expectations. Adjusted earnings per share were $4.68, beating the $4.48 average estimate of analysts, according to LSEG.

The company raised its full-year earnings guidance to a range of $18.30 to $18.60 per share, on an unadjusted basis, up from its previous target of $17.30 to $17.90.

That metric was helped by proceeds from Amex's sale of its stake in Global Business Travel Group, part of which went into the restructuring plan, Le Caillec said.

Total revenue rose 9% to $20.06 billion, coming in slightly below Wall Street's $20.12 billion LSEG forecast. Executives attributed part of the spending drag to recent exits from small business co-brand partnerships.

Shares of the card company fell roughly 4% in afternoon trading.

#326. Serbia: Hope, tension and apprehension as voters prepare to elect new parliament

After two years of protests, Serbia faces a pivotal election on Sunday, with a student-led challenge to Aleksandar Vucic's lengthy rule. Allegations of electoral irregularities and fears of a post-election crisis abound.

A sense of hope swept through Belgrade on Thursday evening as thousands gathered for the final rally of the Student List, the group of candidates put forward by the student movement that has been protesting against the government for almost exactly two years.

The Student List is calling for an end to the current regime in Serbia via the ballot box in Sunday's parliamentary election.

Many of those who attended the rally had spent days walking to the Serbian capital from all four corners of the country, turning the campaign finale into a display of solidarity and peaceful determination.

"We have walked across Serbia from one end to the other, visited villages and neighborhoods, looked you in the eye and realized that we can count on you," biology student Arsa Stojanovic told the crowd.

"People, it smells like freedom," added Aleksandar Cupic, professor of transport and traffic engineering, and a candidate on the Student List.

After 24 months of protests sparked by the collapse of a concrete canopy at Novi Sad railway station, which killed 16 people in November 2024, Serbia is heading into an election unlike any other in the 14 years since the Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) first came to power.

For the first time in years, the ruling party faces a serious political challenge, which this time comes from a student movement that has channeled widespread public discontent into an electoral campaign.

"These are the first elections since [Aleksandar] Vucic came to power in 2012 that are competitive and whose outcome is uncertain," political scientist Dusan Spasojevic told DW. "The student movement has generated hope among citizens and substantial support. Polls suggest that they could even win more than 40% of the vote."

The Student List has framed the election as a chance to confront corruption and demand accountability for a disaster that became a symbol of institutional failure.

In doing so, the students have sought to put aside the ideological, social and foreign-policy divisions that have long shaped Serbian politics.

Just a few kilometers away, also in Belgrade, the ruling SNS held its own final rally.

The message here was familiar: A vote for the SNS means stability, continuity and further prosperity, while a change of government would risk plunging the country into chaos.

The party framed the election as a battle for Serbia's future and sovereignty, accusing its opponents of serving foreign interests.

"They tried to destroy our country on foreign orders, and that is why this is the hardest battle," former President Aleksandar Vucic told his supporters. "They attacked us with billions of euros, and we defended ourselves with integrity, our program, love for our homeland and Serbia's achievements."

The campaign has increasingly been overshadowed by violence, reflecting a society in which political opponents are often portrayed not simply as rivals, but as enemies.

By the final week of the campaign, CRTA, an independent election-monitoring organization, had recorded 116 incidents involving physical violence or verbal threats. The incidents included physical confrontations, attacks on campaigners and attempts to seize campaign banners.

CRTA also reported that opposition activists, particularly those linked to the Student List, were also subjected to surveillance  and police questioning. In some cases, the organization said, police were slow to protect opposition candidates and supporters.

In the final runup to the election, there have also been a series of investigative reports examining alleged links between people connected to the ruling SNS and criminal networks.

Published by independent media in the days before the election, the reports have brought renewed attention to questions of corruption, political influence and accountability — issues at the heart of the student movement's challenge to the government.

But Spasojevic is skeptical that the revelations will make a significant difference to the election outcome.

He argues that those who trust investigative media are likely to have already made up their minds, while voters who rely on pro-government outlets may never even see these reports.

There are also serious concerns about the fairness of the election process.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

CRTA told DW that the election campaign has not been either free or fair from day one, citing "a narrowing of freedoms and deepening inequality" among candidates and voters.

Its report found a pronounced imbalance in media coverage: Almost three-quarters of airtime devoted to political players on national television has featured government representatives, while more than 80% of coverage on the Student List has been negative.

Ahead of the vote, the Balkan Investigative Reporting Network (BIRN) published the testimony of a former member of the SNS, who described how public-sector employees were pressured to deliver votes for the party.

DW has also learned from sources who want to remain anonymous that voters have been offered between €30 and €150 to vote for the SNS, with holiday packages reportedly offered to entire families if they pledge all their votes to the ruling party.

Many fear a period of turbulence, uncertainty and political crisis after the vote, with election night itself a particular source of anxiety.

For weeks, the authorities have warned that students, allegedly with the help of foreign intelligence services, are preparing violence in the streets. In the final days of the campaign, several people were arrested on what the prosecution has said was the suspicion of plotting disturbances on election night.

The best-case scenario, says Dusan Spasojevic, would be a decisive victory for one of the two opposing camps, leaving little room for the other to challenge the result.

"But polls suggest that the margin will be narrow, and we will probably enter some kind of post-election crisis," he said, adding that a narrow victory for the ruling party would almost certainly be contested, given longstanding concerns about the fairness of the electoral process and allegations that some votes may have been obtained illegally.

Whatever happens, the European Union's response could be crucial.

"I think the international response will be crucial in preventing major incidents and a further escalation of the crisis. It is the only thing that can persuade the authorities to accept defeat and refrain from an overly heavy-handed response to any protests or incidents," he told DW.

An EU Commission spokesperson acknowledged to DW that key recommendations on improving Serbia's electoral framework remain unimplemented and also stressed that democracy and the rule of law are key to the accession process. Serbia has been an EU candidate country since 2012.

Back in Belgrade, the students ended their rally with a kolo, a traditional Serbian circle dance, projecting confidence in their chances at the ballot box.

But the path to victory could prove difficult. The movement has vowed to defend every vote — setting the stage for a potentially tense election night if the results are disputed.

U.S. Bancorp is looking to sell as much as C$1 billion ($713 million) of bonds in Canada, tapping the loonie market for the first time in more than two decades.

#324. The 5 best handheld gaming consoles, tested and compared: Switch 2, Steam Deck, MSI Claw 8

When reviewing the best handheld gaming consoles of the year, we have to start with one brutal fact: the memory crisis has made handheld gaming a lot less fun. Some handheld gaming consoles are now pushing $1,500 or more, and games are more expensive than ever, too. We won't go so far as to say that the AI-fueled crisis has killed handheld gaming, but the price of entry can be pretty intimidating. Given this fact, we believe the Nintendo Switch 2 is the best handheld gaming console available in 2026. However, we also tested the latest handheld gaming PCs from Lenovo, MSI, and Xbox/ROG.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Thanks to the AI industry hogging all the memory components, gaming consoles are getting more expensive. In the past 12 months, Nintendo, Microsoft, and Sony all raised prices on their gaming consoles, and Windows laptops are seeing the same increases. It's not a good time to be a gamer. Even before the wave of price increases, handheld gaming PCs were already expensive. The Lenovo Legion Go 2 launched last year for $1,099, but costs $1,999 today. The Legion Go S with SteamOS was originally available for $749.99, but you'll have to pay $999 today at Best Buy — and that's with a $580 discount.

The Steam Deck has also been beset by price increases and has periodically gone out of stock. The Switch 2 has also gotten more expensive, though it's by far the most affordable option in the category.

So far, only one handheld gaming console has held steady at its original price — the ROG Xbox Ally X. We don't know how long it will continue to be available for $999, but we're not complaining.

When purchasing a handheld gaming PC, you generally have three options: Nintendo, Windows, or SteamOS. The right answer comes down entirely to personal preference. If you're a PC gamer who primarily buys games through Steam, then go with a Steam Deck or Lenovo Legion running SteamOS. If you're an Xbox or cloud gamer, then the Windows 11-based MSI Claw 8 or ROG Xbox Ally X will be more your speed.

Read our full review of the Nintendo Switch 2.

Those who want to stay on the cutting edge of handheld gaming consoles need to get a Nintendo Switch 2 at some point. It's the latest and greatest version of the handheld, with more power, improved features and accessories, and a constantly-expanding slate of games that'll almost certainly induce FOMO if you don't hop on the bandwagon. If you're excited about playing the long-awaited The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake, love all things Pokémon, or have a kiddo that needs to be introduced to the joys of Mario Kart, then the Switch 2 is a must-have.

The Nintendo Switch 2 is finally here, and it's unsurprisingly great. The new console has been eight years in the making, and many improvements have been made since the last generation of Switch consoles. And even after Nintendo raised the price by $50 in September, it's still less than half the price of other handheld gaming devices. For all these reasons, the Switch 2 is easily the best handheld gaming console for most people.

Nintendo went with the "make it again, but better" approach with the Switch 2. The concept is the same, but here, you'll get more processing power, massively improved features and accessories, and an upcoming game release calendar that looks promising. (The Switch 2 also allows for backward compatibility with your favorite Switch games, with some games even getting upgrades for the new console.) The handheld is bigger than its predecessor, but still thin and light, so it feels great to hold.

The new display, while a visual step down from the Nintendo Switch OLED, is still really good. It has HDR support, a 120Hz refresh rate, and a nice 1080p resolution within the 7.9-inch LCD panel. Still, we look forward to the inevitable OLED upgrade that's sure to come out in a few years. One of the best hardware upgrades, though, comes in the form of the new Joy-Con controllers, which snap onto the Switch using magnets instead of the original's sliding mechanism. They feel way better to attach and detach, and they're also a bit bigger, which helps with comfort a bit (although they still aren't that ergonomic for extended play sessions). They're also more responsive and have better button push feedback. It remains to be seen if Joy-Con drift will be a problem in the future, but we'll keep you posted. Older Switch controllers, including the Pro controller, can be used wirelessly with the Switch 2, which means you don't have to blow a bunch of money on new accessories if you don't want to.

In terms of software features, we've fallen in love with GameChat. It lets you join an online party to talk with your buddies, but doesn't require a headset, which means multiple people in one room can experience the chat as much as the player. It also has a livestream function that lets you share and watch gameplay with others in real-time without having to pause.

While we think the Switch 2 is pretty marvelous, some things could be better. For starters, pricing. The console is expensive, but that's not exactly the problem — it's all the stuff you have to buy after you get the handheld that can quickly become frustrating. Want to buy a Switch 2 camera? That'll be $54.99. New Joy-Con? Get ready to fork over $94.99. There's also the Pro Controller for $84.99, and the slate of Nintendo Switch 2 first-party games that range from $69.99 to $79.99. The Switch 2 is also exclusively compatible with microSD Express cards, so if you want to expand that 256GB of internal storage, you'll have to buy one of those instead of using your old Switch's microSD card. Those aren't cheap, either. Finally, battery life is just good, not great. We knew it'd be marginally worse than the original Switch as a sacrifice for more horsepower, but two to six hours on a single charge isn't anything to write home about, especially when we regularly got an average playtime of around three hours before needing a recharge.

While it has its hang-ups, the Switch 2 is a worthy upgrade to one of the greatest gaming consoles of all time, and one you need to get if you loved the original handheld in any capacity.

Read our full review of the Valve Steam Deck OLED.

The original Steam Deck was unlike any device that came before it, bringing the traditionally less-accessible PC gaming experience to a streamlined and impressively powerful handheld. And when the Steam Deck OLED arrived in 2023, it delivered big upgrades that were irresistible to a lot of gamers. Once upon a time, you could buy this handheld for just $399, but today, it will set you back $789, or $999 if you get the 1TB version, which you should. That's a 98 percent price increase, and it hurts.

Still, there's no denying that the Steam Deck OLED is one of the best handheld options for PC gamers. So, if you feel like you have PC gaming FOMO, the Deck will finally fix that.

The Steam Deck OLED is a bit dated compared to the latest MSI and Lenovo handhelds, but it's undoubtedly the best handheld for PC gamers who have a long list of titles in the Steam library. Likewise, if you want to get into PC gaming without getting a gaming laptop or custom PC rig, this is your best bet.

Using Steam's built-in OS, the Steam Deck OLED gives you access to the entire Steam Store library (including your own account, if it's already attached to a PC rig). Not every game in there is compatible with the Deck, but Steam kindly labels the ones that can run on it within their storefront — and the selection is enough to keep you busy for a long, long time. From there, you can install games directly onto the device and start playing.

The power of the Steam Deck is remarkable. We had multiple "pinch me" moments as we booted up demanding games like Elden Ring, Uncharted 4: A Thief's End, and God of War, and experienced zero performance issues. And now that the handheld is equipped with an OLED display, we're even more impressed by this device. (Seriously, playing Red Dead Redemption 2 on a handheld for the first time was surreal.) We also appreciated the Deck's desktop mode, which basically turns it into a pint-sized computer that you can use for web browsing and more. If you're patient enough, you can even program it to boot up non-Steam launchers like EA Origin and Ubisoft Connect, but it's a complicated process that requires some Linux know-how. It's a downside for those who want an all-encompassing PC experience with no limitations, but there's still so much here to enjoy that it's not a dealbreaker by any means.

"Valve didn’t just transfer the same display specs from [the] LCD model into an OLED form. Instead, the screen is better in almost every way. Its 7.4-inch size mildly improves on the 7-inch dimensions of the original (while retaining the same 800p resolution). Crucially, the original model’s 60Hz top refresh rate has been expanded to 90Hz, enabling gameplay of up to 90 frames per second for games that support it," wrote our reviewer Alex Perry in 2023.

PC gaming has traditionally been inaccessible to a large swathe of people because of the steep financial investment and need for constant troubleshooting. While the price advantage is no longer relevant, the Steam Deck is still able to democratize PC gaming for all players, and to us, that's a gigantic feat.

Read our full review of the ROG Xbox Ally X.

When the ROG Xbox Ally X first came out, we noted that it was expensive compared to the competition. But what a difference a year makes. While every other gaming handheld has gotten a price increase, the ROG Xbox Ally X is holding steady at $999.99. Now, to be clear, that doesn't make it cheap, by any means. However, it's definitely worth noting that it's overall value has only gone up since it was first released.

If you're an Xbox or cloud gamer, then the Xbox-branded handheld from Asus ROG is a natural choice or alternative to the Xbox Series X/S. You'll be able to use this handheld to play Grand Theft Auto: VI when it comes out (finally), as well as Xbox titles you already own and games on Xbox Game Pass. The hardware is impressive, and you can use the built-in Copilot features to get an instant AI assist if you get stuck in a game.

As our reviewer wrote, "Overall, the ROG Ally X will feel very familiar to gamers in the Xbox camp, in a good way. The device even has a fingerprint reader, adding a layer of security while providing an easy way to jump right back in and play...I love the ergonomics of the ROG Xbox Ally X, and I'm sure Xbox gamers will agree. The integrated grips simulate the feel and comfort of a standard Xbox controller. Previous ROG Ally iterations didn’t have these kinds of grips, so that makes this a step up already in that aspect."

Our reviewer noted that the handheld played Lies of P impressively well. "Lies of P running in top shape really demonstrated how much power the Xbox ROG Ally X had." The handheld also supports quite a few "Play Anywhere" titles that can be downloaded and, well, played anywhere. Good luck taking your gaming laptop on a long flight.

Our biggest complaint with this handheld? The ROG Xbox Ally feels a little too much like a Windows PC experience. So, if you already have a gaming laptop or PC, it's hard to justify the investment. We wish it had an OLED display for the $1,000 price tag, but the display it does have is rock solid.

PC gamers with plenty of money to spare will appreciate the power and design of the new MSI Claw, equipped with the ultra-powerful Intel Arc G3 Extreme processor. However, that $1,799.99 price tag is impossible to ignore, and you can buy a powerful gaming laptop for the same price. However, if you're looking for the best possible performance and graphics in a handheld gaming console, there's none better.

The MSI Claw 8 is the pinnacle of handheld gaming at the moment, but it's still tough to recommend because of the price tag. As MSI proudly notes, this compact gaming console offers "Peak performance, peak ergonomics, [and] peak software." After testing it out, we can verify that's absolutely the case. While it doesn't have an OLED display, the brightness and color clarity were excellent. And thanks to the powerful processor, you can run intensive AAA titles without compromising on performance or graphics.

We loved the void purple color and overall design, and it has a very comfortable fit in your hand. Like the ROG Xbox Ally X, it feels like you're holding console game controllers, and for long gaming sessions, the ergonomics are impressive. It also has a bevy of ports, with a 3.5mm headphone jack, MicroSD card reader, and two Thunderbolt 4 USB-C ports. While some gamers may prefer the Linux-based SteamOS, we definitely didn't mind the familiar Windows 11 setup.

One minor note: The appeal of gaming handhelds is that they're plug-and-play, and don't require as much tinkering as a gaming PC or laptop. However, thanks to the powerful new Intel Arc G3 Extreme processor, we did have to go through an annoying setup process, which involved downloading the latest drivers and updating the firmware repeatedly. If you're spending almost $2,000 on a device like this, then you'll likely have the know-how to get through this process, but it was a bit of a pain point for us.

Read our full review of the Nintendo Switch Lite.

If you want to get the Nintendo Switch experience in a smaller package, the Nintendo Switch Lite was made just for you. It has many of the same capabilities as the regular Switch, but it's way easier to take on the go with you.

Nintendo's smallest, slimmest, and cheapest system is the Switch Lite, a handheld-only console from 2019 that ditches the detachable Joy-Cons for built-in controls. It can't be used for TV or tabletop play, and it lacks any sort of revolutionary specs, but that's not the point here: Its chief purpose is to offer a friendly, approachable portable gaming experience, and it totally delivers.

Our testing included several fun sessions with Hades and Breath of the Wild, and it really is incredible that you can get titles of that caliber on a device that's the size of a small clutch. Plus, you're technically not missing out on any power: It runs on the same NVIDIA CPU as Nintendo's other Switches, and its LCD touchscreen (while not OLED-pretty) is plenty bright and colorful. You won't have access to the entire Nintendo library from the get-go, mind you. Some games are incompatible with the Switch Lite out of the box because it doesn't natively support HD Rumble and IR motion controls. The good news is that it's only a handful of titles, and the problem is easily remedied by pairing the console with a separate Joy-Con.

The other thing about the Switch Lite that blew us away in our testing was its feather-light build: This thing weighs just over half a pound. (For comparison's sake, the standard Switch weighs 0.88 pounds — it doesn't seem like much, but it makes Senior Shopping Reporter Haley Henschel's hands get cramped and numb after an hour or so.) It's extremely comfortable to hold for long periods of time, with a familiar layout that features a traditional D-pad instead of a four-button setup like the other Switches. We'd play the Switch Lite all day if it let us — its battery sadly maxed out around four to five hours in our testing. If you're going to take it with you on an extended bus ride or flight, make sure you juice it up beforehand. Finally, the Switch Lite's onboard storage is capped at 32GB, just like the standard model, so you'll definitely need to buy a separate microSD card if you want to load it with more than a couple of games. But as with all of its shortcomings, the Switch Lite's exceedingly reasonable $200 price point makes this easy to swallow.

What about the Lenovo Legion Go 2 and Lenovo Legion Go S?

We were impressed with the Lenovo Legion Go 2 when it launched last year, but it's since gotten a price hike that makes it a lot harder to justify the investment. Instead, we would recommend going with the more affordable Lenovo Legion Go S, which we previewed at CES 2025. Specifically, we recommend the Legion Go S that's powered by SteamOS.

If you're looking for the best alternative to the Steam Deck but don't want to go with a Windows-based gaming handheld, then the latest Legion Go S is a solid choice. However, after receiving a price increase, it's harder to recommend this device, especially now that the Steam Deck OLED is back in stock.

Unless otherwise noted, we spent at least 10 hours apiece with each pick on our list, playing at least three different games on each console throughout our testing periods. (If we really liked the console, it received hundreds of hours of playtime.) When taking each one for a spin, we consider factors including their overall value, Bluetooth support, form factor, storage capacity, and any unique features the consoles brought to the table. But when deciding which options would make the final cut, we prioritized four core categories:

The types of games you're interested in will probably be a (if not the) deciding factor in your purchase, since each different handheld supports wildly different titles and formats. In other words, don't buy an Analogue Pocket if you want to get into Elden Ring or Animal Crossing: New Horizons.

Some handheld consoles are hybrid systems, meaning you can play them without any external setups or use a docking station (typically sold separately) to connect them to a TV or monitor. The Nintendo Switch takes things one step further and works in tabletop mode, where you disconnect the Joy-Con and use the console as a standalone screen while it's propped up on its built-in kickstand.

Handheld consoles have small screens by nature, but a display should still be crisp and bright enough so that players can read small text and take in tiny details without squinting.

A handheld console's battery life can vary drastically depending on the games you play and your brightness settings. (The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild at full blast will slurp up juice way faster than Stardew Valley at half brightness, for example.) But generally, it's a huge plus if you don't need to charge it after just a couple of hours of use.
